# Changelog

All notable changes to Una are recorded here. Versions follow the milestones and gates of `docs/plan/roadmap.md`.

## Unreleased

## v0.5.2

The third increment of release R0.5, made from `main`, published as v0.5.2. The release changes the edition `0.5` preview: `const` and `var` replace `let` and `let edit` there (ADR 0137), so a package of edition `0.5` that writes `let` now gets U7007, which `una fix` rewrites, and modules of edition `0.5` declare constants (ADR 0126). Editions `0.1` to `0.4` do not change. An `Int` of any size is now written as text, in subquadratic time, with the memory budget in place of the 4300-digit cap of writing (ADR 0004 amendment of 2026-11-26, accepted); reading keeps its 4300-digit bound. On Linux, `una` runs on glibc 2.28 and later (Ubuntu 22.04, Debian 12 and AlmaLinux 8 tested), where 0.5.1 needed 2.39 (ADR 0124 amendment of 2026-11-26, accepted). Security and soundness: the native build forces the tag bit of an unchecked `Int` result (#2245, defense in depth), the ranges of project files with `\r\n` line ends name the bytes they read (#2249, found by fuzzing), and the release gates run the shipped build of `una` and a parallel `sanitize` in its own clone (#2254, #2181). Validation: `docs/validation/0.5.2.md`.

### Edition `0.5` preview: changes that may need edits

- **`const` and `var` replace `let` and `let edit` in edition `0.5`** (#1628, ADR 0137; a change of the edition `0.5` preview, for the heading "Edition `0.5` preview: changes that may need edits" of ADR 0121 rule 6; kind: U7007). In a package of edition `0.5`, a binding is written `const x = e`, where edition `0.4` writes `let x = e`, and `var x = e`, where it writes `let edit x = e`, with the same typing, Core IR, obligations, information flow and run-time behavior in both engines. `const` and `var` are keywords there, and `let` is reserved: each `let` is now U7007, "this form of edition 0.5 changed in release v0.5.2: `let` is written `const`, or `var` for a binding that is reassigned", at the word, whose first repair is the exact rewrite (`let edit x` to `var x`, `let x` to `const x`). Migration: `una fix` applies the rewrites. `const edit x`, `var edit x` and a `var` at the top level of a module are U0101, and `const` and `var` cannot name anything in edition `0.5`. Messages of a package of edition `0.5` name its binding words: U2019 says "declare it `var x`", U2024 "holds only `const` or `var` statements", and U2011 and U3013 "bind its result with `const` first". U7007, the code of a form a preview edition changed (ADR 0121 rule 19), is registered, with its `una explain` text; U2024's registry meaning names a binding statement. The edition ledger gains `const and var` (ADR 0137), and `una report` counts its sites. A dependency is lexed, parsed and formatted under its own edition (ADR 0122 rules 48 and 49), so a dependency of edition `0.4` of a package of edition `0.5` keeps `let` and its diagnostics keep the words of edition `0.4`; before, every mounted dependency was read with the root's keywords. Editions `0.1` to `0.4` do not change: there a binding written `const x` or `var x` keeps its U0101 or U0103 and gains the help "`const` and `var` are keywords of edition 0.5". The conformance packages of edition `0.5` are rewritten with the new words, and the comparison of every edition `0.4` package with edition `0.5` now rewrites its `let`s first. Module constants (ADR 0126, #1632), the `una fix` tests of ADR 0137 (#1629) and the migration of the repository's other Una code (#1630, #1631) follow. Lexing `const`, `var` and `let` is O(1) per word, a lookup in the edition's table; the rewrite of a message is O(its length).

### Other changes

- **A regression test pins the values of a Nat refinement's counterexample** (#825, `spec/13 §1`). In `examples/pantry`, deleting `requires used <= item.amount` from `use_up` gave, up to v0.3.14, the counterexample `item.amount = , used = `: the model query asked for the tag of the sum field `item.measure`, which it never declared, so the solver gave no values and the empty values were printed. #668 and #902 fixed both causes; `una check` now prints `item = Item#0, item.amount = 0, item.measure = Grams, item.name = Text#0, used = 1`, in text and in `--json`, and a test of `una-cli` pins it. No output, encoding or verdict changes.
- **`una fix` renames a reserved word only where the renamed program parses** (#836): the U0103 rename of a word reserved for a later version, such as `edit` in `let edit total = 0` of edition 0.1, wrote `let the_edit total = 0`, which does not parse. The rename is now offered only where the program with it parses; where the word stands as the keyword of its later construct (`let edit x`, `edit x`, `take x`, a `together` block), the U0103 stays with no edit, and `una fix` leaves the file as it was. Where the word names something, the rename is unchanged.
- **An obligation's goal over decimals shows decimal values** (#1041, `spec/29 §16` rule 11): a goal, its message and its repairs show the clause as written, `result >= 0.00` for `it >= 0.00` rather than the coefficient `result >= 0`, with no aligned coefficient and with `coefficient()` named, as a counterexample already shows values; a clause with a comment, or with a number argument before `.coefficient()`, still shows coefficients (`spec/29 §16` rule 14). The query is unchanged; the ids of the obligations whose goal holds a decimal literal or `coefficient()` change with their goal text.
- **An `Int` of any size is written as text** (#1223, `spec/11 §7`, ADR 0004 amendment of 2026-11-26): interpolation, `to_text`, `to_json` and the Una form of traces, outputs, failures and test reports write an integer of more than 4300 digits in full, in O(M(d) log d) for its d digits, in both engines and with or without `--trace`, where the run used to abort with U6003 (#923). Before converting a large `Int`, or a `Decimal`'s coefficient, a write reserves the bytes of its text and conversion (96 per 64-bit limb, known from the bit length) against the memory budget, a text built of many parts by interpolation, an event or a message reserves each growth of its buffer, and `to_json` checks the budget every 64 KiB of the text of numbers, strings and keys it copies, and of the text it writes (which also closes the same, older gap for long strings and keys); when they do not fit, the run aborts with U6003 at the write instead of reaching the allocator's hard ceiling. An operation whose argument does not fit leaves no event in either engine. Reading keeps its bound: `parse_int`, `to_int` and `parse_json` still refuse more than 4300 digits, so such a text is written and not read back. This replaces the entry of #1550, whose abort no longer exists.
- **Release check: the x86-64 contraction canary** (#1228, ADR 0114 Appendix C rule 7, as ADR 0120 Appendix A amends it): `scripts/fma-canary.sh`, run by `scripts/local-ci.sh`, builds the float corpus's contraction-sensitive case (`crates/una-core/examples/contraction-canary.rs`: `a * b + c` with `a = 1 + 2^-52`, `b = 1 - 2^-52`, `c = -1`, and una-core's scalar `fma`) with the shipped flags, where the FMA gate of `scripts/check-fma.sh` must pass and the result is two roundings, and with contraction forced on (`-C target-feature=+fma -C llvm-args=-fp-contract=fast`), where the gate must fail on the case and the result is the fused one. It runs on the x86-64 `--linux` leg and prints that it is not applicable on AArch64. No shipped artifact changes.
- **Module constants in edition `0.5`** (#1632, ADR 0126; a construct the edition `0.5` preview adds). A module of a package of edition `0.5` declares a named value at the top level, `[pub] const name: type = value`, with its type required (U0101 at `=` without it), exports it with `pub` and imports it by name; a top-level `let`, U7007 there, is read as `const`. The value is a constant expression: literals, `-`, the integer, `F64` and decimal operators of ADR 0126 rule 3, `and`, `or`, `not`, comparisons, constructors, lists, parentheses and other constants, declared before or after. The compiler evaluates each value once, at check time, in dependency order, and substitutes it at every reference, so `una core`, the verifier, the interpreter and native code see the literal; a contract that names a constant is proven with its value, and a refined constant type gives one `refinement` obligation at the value with a closed goal (U5001 without a counterexample note when refuted). New codes: U1011, a constant that depends on itself, once per cycle; U2041, a value outside the constant expressions (naming the construct), a division by zero, a value that does not fit its fixed-width type, a value past the caps (4,300 digits, 1,000,000 elements, fields and characters), or a declared type that holds a function type. A capability reference as a value is U3005; an assignment to a constant and an `edit` of one are U2019, a `take` of one U2020. `std.time` declares `millisecond`, `second` and `minute` as `pub const` constants of `Int of Millisecond` in edition `0.5`, with the same values; edition `0.4` keeps its built-in names. The edition ledger gains `module constants`, and `una report` counts them. Editions `0.1` to `0.4` do not change. Costs: the dependency order and the cycles are O(constants + references); each value is evaluated once in O(its size) under the caps; a scalar reference costs what a literal costs.
- **The edition ledger** (#1752, part 1, ADR 0121 rule 9, as its amendment of 2026-11-06 extends it to every preview edition): the compiler holds one table, beside `Edition` in `una-project`, with an entry for each construct of editions `0.4` (ADRs 0116, 0117, 0122 and 0123) and `0.5` (ADRs 0119 and 0120): its record, its reserved and contextual keywords, the prelude names, built-in methods, standard modules and variants it adds, its project-file sections and codes, its clashes with the edition before by ledger id, its trust, its unsettled points and the release that landed it. The guide's page on editions lists the constructs of the preview editions, and a test checks that list, and the words the lexer reserves for edition `0.4`, against the ledger. The ledger is a static table: an entry is found by its construct in O(1). The report's `preview_constructs`, which reads it, follows in part 2. Behavior is unchanged.
- **`una report` names the preview constructs a package uses** (#1752, part 2, ADR 0121 rule 8, `spec/13 §6`): the report's `edition` section gains `preview_constructs`, each construct of the edition ledger that the root package uses, with its record, its number of sites and the names of its unsettled points, ordered by record, then construct, and `[]` for a package of a stable edition or of edition `0.3`. The human `edition:` line lists them, as `edition: 0.5 (preview): sum (ADR 0120, 1 site; unsettled: the accumulation order of 0120's kernels (L, KB)), ita.lock (ADR 0122, 1 site), ita.una (ADR 0122, 1 site)`. The key is additive, and the schema stays `una.report/2`. Sites are counted from Core IR, the checker (for `Map.update`, which elaboration lowers), the syntax tree (imports and `diverges`), the tokens (`simd` as a name and the labels `with:` and `and:`) and `ita.una`, once each, in O(n) for a package of size n; a dependency's constructs are not the root's. A test has one package per ledger entry, so a construct added to the ledger without a counter fails. The ledger gains `std.time`, and lists `simd` as a name of edition `0.5` in place of the clause `simd required`, which is refused until it lands.
- **The C math library check reads the shipped Linux `una`, object by object** (#1953, follow-ups of the #1952 review). `scripts/release.sh` links on Linux through `scripts/link-trace.sh`, which adds GNU ld's `--trace-symbol` for every name of `scripts/no-libm.txt` and keeps the trace of the `una` link, and `scripts/check-release.sh` runs `scripts/no-libm.sh --link-trace` on the packed `una`, the runtime built into it and the examples' executables: every object of the link that refers to a math name must be, byte for byte, an object of the Z3 library that `supply-chain/z3.lock` pins and one that `scripts/no-libm-una.txt` names for it, so a reference of libstdc++.a's own objects (`hash_tr1.o`, `hash_c++0x.o` import `frexpl`) or of Rust code fails, and so does a math function linked in statically. The trace of the aarch64 Linux `una` built with this change shows no libstdc++.a object referring to a math name; it shows that Z3's `ceil` and `sqrt` calls resolve inside `una` to the weak definitions of the pinned toolchain's compiler_builtins, which `nm -u` could not see: they are listed as `linux-builtins` entries, allowed from the Z3 objects named only. `scripts/no-libm-una.txt` now lists, per platform, the Z3 objects that may import each name, and the check of the release `una` ties each name to those objects instead of to the name alone. The canaries add a `una` importing two names with a Z3 stand-in importing only the first, stand-in Z3 objects outside the list, and on Linux real traced links. The list's comment now says that `hwf_manager::rem` is dead code that no Z3 object calls.
- **The contributor guide says what a Linux build of `una` needs** (#1958). `CONTRIBUTING.md` gains a section "Building on Linux": every Linux build of `una`, a development build included, needs the toolchain's static `libstdc++.a` and `libgcc.a` (on Fedora and RHEL, the package `libstdc++-static`), since `crates/una-z3/stage.rs` links the solver's C++ runtime statically (#1956); the release image's packages and the files the linker puts into `una` are pinned in the `[toolchain.<target>]` and `[toolchain-files.<target>]` tables of `supply-chain/z3.lock`, and `scripts/check-toolchain.sh` and `scripts/load-commands.sh` enforce them. The README's "Build from source" names the static `libstdc++.a` among the prerequisites. No behavior changes.
- **The v0.4.0 section of the changelog is grouped by theme** (#2076, a recorded amendment in `scripts/released-sections-amended.txt`). Its 318 entries stand under thirteen theme headings, each kept word for word, in their order within a theme; the U0103 entry sets the placeholder `<area>` in backticks, so that a page that refuses raw HTML can show the section. The tag `v0.4.0` and the release notes published from it keep the original text.
- **Linux: `una` runs on glibc 2.28 and later** (#2105, ADR 0124 rule 1, `scripts/local-ci/Dockerfile.release`, `supply-chain/z3.lock`, `install.sh`). The Linux archive and the Z3 library inside `una` are now built in a release image of their own, `scripts/local-ci/Dockerfile.release`: AlmaLinux 8 (glibc 2.28, maintained until 2029), pinned by digest, with GCC 13 of gcc-toolset-13 (13.3.1) and binutils 2.40, where they were built in the Ubuntu 24.04 image of the tests (glibc 2.39, GCC 13.3.0). The tests, the formal track and the hunt stay in the Ubuntu image, since the pinned Lean toolchain needs glibc 2.29 and the Z3 project's Linux executable 2.38; `scripts/local-ci.sh --linux-arm64` runs them there and then packs and checks the archive in a second container of the release image, and `scripts/local-release.sh` builds the archive there. The limits leg (`scripts/local-ci.sh --linux-arm64 --limits`, the release gate `limits-linux-arm64`) runs in the release image, against the shipped Z3 library, whose digest it checks against the lock, at glibc 2.28. The release image installs the toolchain packages at the exact versions the lock records, so a rebuild either gives the same image or fails at install. The floor was chosen by measurement among 2.35, 2.31 and 2.28: 2.28 is the oldest candidate whose distribution is still maintained and that has GCC 13, the compiler of the earlier recipe; the 2.31 distributions (Debian 11, Ubuntu 20.04) are past their standard support and have GCC 10 at most, and Ubuntu 22.04 (2.35) has GCC 12 at most. The `una` built there asks the loader for `GLIBC_2.28` at most, which `scripts/check-release.sh` checks on the packed `una` and on an executable it builds. So the archive of the next release installs and runs on glibc 2.28 and later, where 0.4.0 to 0.5.1 needed glibc 2.39: tested on Ubuntu 22.04, Debian 12 and AlmaLinux 8, and inferred from the symbol versions for Ubuntu 20.04, Debian 10 and 11, and RHEL 8 and later and their rebuilds. `install.sh` refuses a system below 2.28 for the releases after 0.5.1, and keeps the floor 2.39 for `UNA_VERSION` 0.4.0 to 0.5.1, whose `una` cannot start on an older glibc. What changes in the archive beyond the baseline: before glibc 2.34 threads and `dlsym` are libraries of their own, so `una` also names `libpthread.so.0` and `libdl.so.2`, glibc's, now listed in `scripts/load-commands.txt`. `libstdc++` and `libgcc` stay linked statically. The toolchain check reads the image's packages with `rpm -q` where it read `dpkg-query`, and the lock records the AlmaLinux packages and the digests of the files the linker puts into `una`; the new Z3 library digest was shown reproducible by cold builds in the new image. The release record and the public summary accept the version line of gcc-toolset's strip (`GNU strip version 2.40-21.el8`), in that exact form. ADR 0124 gains a dated amendment, accepted by the Owner on 2026-11-26, that names `libpthread.so.0` and `libdl.so.2` among glibc's libraries and the release image as the image of rule 1. `scripts/build-z3.sh` asks GNU ar and ranlib for their deterministic mode (`D`), which gcc-toolset's binutils does not default to; Apple's ar is unchanged, so the macOS library's digest is unchanged.
- **The release gates run `sanitize` beside `build` and `deps`** (#2181): `scripts/release-gates.sh` runs the `sanitize` gate in a clone of its own (`OUT/clone-sanitize`, with its own target directory) beside the `build` and `deps` gates, and every other gate alone, in order, once it has ended. `results.tsv` keeps the order of the gates, and the record and the verdict are written once every gate has ended; no gate is removed, relaxed or skipped. `UNA_GATES_PARALLEL=0` runs every gate in sequence, as before (`docs/release-gates.md` §2, "The schedule").
- **The serve tests hold the test build of `una` they run** (#2201). `crates/una-cli/tests/serve_runs.rs` and the scripted test of `serve_live.rs` ran `CARGO_BIN_EXE_una`, the path `target/<profile>/una` where each cargo command puts the `una` it built last: the tests' build with the `test-hooks` feature, or a plain one. A plain build of `una-cli` in the same target directory during a run (`cargo build`, `cargo run`) replaced it, and the later cases of a running test met a `una` that refuses `--serve-script` ("the scripted connections ... do not run in this release yet"). The tests now run a hard link to the file taken when the test process starts, checked to hold both test-hook markers, so a later replacement changes nothing; a path that already holds a plain build fails at once with the cause. A new test replaces the path as cargo does and checks both. No behavior of `una` changes.
- **The U7013 message of `una ita verify` gives the real reason** (#2209, follow-up of #2070): a package whose copies match the lock is not verified because the registry client checks the owner's key chain and its name map proof when it reads a release but does not store them beside the package, so `verify` cannot check them again; the message no longer says they are not held until the registry client supplies them. The explain page of U7013, `spec/30 §10` rule 11 and the offline guide say the same. Behavior (U7013, exit code 1) is unchanged.
- **The installer, the README and the guides name v0.5.1 as the current release** (#2211, post-tag step of #2101): `install.sh` installs 0.5.1 by default, and the README, `SECURITY.md`, the known limits, the language guide and the pages on editions and on Itá say what holds in v0.5.1 (edition `0.4` still a preview, no registry served, no fuzz target for the HTTP parsers, the targets of the release archives). The facts are those of v0.5.0, unchanged.
- **U3021 gives one narrowed grant per requirement** (#2213, `spec/13 §3`, ADR 0093 rule 6): when a callee has two requirements of one capability, such as `@a: Prices[price]` and `@b: Prices[set]`, the repair narrows each to the operations its own requirement declares, `with @a = @full[price], @b = @full[set]`, instead of giving the first one both operations and saying nothing of the second. When the ceiling withholds one requirement's operations, the handler route names that requirement and keeps a narrowed grant for the others. When the excess comes from a granted handler's own `uses`, or from the arguments given at its installation, the repair names that handler or those arguments instead of repeating the grant already written. Inside a `together` branch, U3013 now advises the named function for a call in a one-line `if` or a handling clause, since a branch is one `let`. Which programs check is unchanged.
- **The installer refuses what it may not replace at a target before it writes anything** (#2216, from the review of #2203): for every user, a directory, a FIFO or a symbolic link to a directory where the installer writes a file (`bin/una`, `bin/una.new`, `lib/libuna_rt.a`, `README.md`, `LICENSE`, the record, a guide), or a file where it writes in a directory (`UNA_HOME`, `bin`, `lib`, `docs`, `docs/guide`), is now refused with the installer's own one-line message: a target of every installation before anything is downloaded, the runtime and the guides, which only some archives hold, once the archive is unpacked, and in both cases before anything is written or removed. So is, for an ordinary user, an existing directory the installer writes in that the user cannot write in (such as a `docs/guide/` of mode 555). A regular file or a link at such a name is still replaced, whoever made it; a directory or a special file is never removed to make room. Before, an ordinary user's installation stopped halfway with a raw `rm` error, after the new `bin/una` was in place and with the earlier record kept. Since #1966, a `README.md`, `LICENSE` or guide that is a symbolic link to an ordinary user's own file is removed as a link and replaced by a new file, and the user's file is no longer written through. The installer's loop variable `path`, which zsh ties to `PATH`, is renamed `rel`, so that no loop of the script overwrites `PATH` when it is read by zsh in its native mode; the documented invocation stays `sh`.
- **Documents: edition `0.5` does not become stable at `v0.5.0`** (#2242): `spec/13 §7` no longer says that edition `0.5` becomes stable at release `v0.5.0`, which the ADR 0139 amendment of 2026-11-20 (item 2) withdrew; `v0.5.0` and `v0.5.1` serve it as a preview, and the release that makes it stable is named by a later amendment. The README's status names edition `0.5` as the edition of release R0.5. No rule changes.
- **Native: the result of an unchecked `Int` operation always has its tag bit** (#2245, ADR 0055 NB-3, ADR 0112 EL-2, lane S): where the range facts prove both operands and the result of `+`, `-` or `*` in the tagged range, the Cranelift build still computes the operation as a wrapping machine instruction with no tag test and no promotion, and now forces bit 0 of its result, one `orr` per site. On tagged operands the word is already odd, so no result changes; if a range fact were ever wrong (a verifier, solver, proof-cache or runtime bug) and an operand were a handle, the result is now an `Int` immediate, a wrong value at worst, never a word that names a heap object. Defense in depth: no correct program's output changes.
- **The ranges of the project files index the text as written** (#2249, found by the `ita_lock` fuzz target): the reader of `ita.una`, `ita.lock`, `itaty.una`, the workspace file and `mirrors.una` gave its ranges in the decoded text, where each `\r\n` is one `\n`, so in a file with `\r\n` line ends a range after the first one named bytes one to the left of what it read, and could start inside a multi-byte character such as a U+FFFD that replaced an invalid byte. Each range is now mapped back to the text it was given, so a diagnostic points at the token it names; a file with `\n` alone reads as before. The formatter and `una ita add` and `remove` still read and write the decoded text, `\n` alone.
- **The release gates run the shipped build of `una`, never a test build** (#2254). `cargo test --release` relinks `target/release/una` with the features of the dev-dependencies (the test hooks of `una-cli`, `una-http` and `una-hash`, the counters of `una-core`), and the timing-channels gate did that before the `docs` gate built the docs bundle with `target/release/una` and before the `hunt` gate ran the security hunt on it; in the green run of 2026-11-21 both gated a binary holding the test hooks. The `build` gate now copies the release build, made with the solver library and before any test build, into `OUT/product` with its digests, checks that it holds no test hook's marker or variable name and that it checks `examples/` at tier 1 (so it has the solver), and `docs`, `hunt` and the `una --json check` runs of `determinism` run that copy, failing when it is missing, is another commit's, has changed or holds a test name. `hunt-linux-arm64` refuses its container's `una` by the same names. `docs/release-gates.md` says which binary each gate runs. No behavior of `una` changes.
- **Module constants: no pending `overflow` or `division` site inside a constant's value** (#2267, ADR 0126 rules 5 and 6, card #1632). A constant's value is evaluated at check time, where a fixed-width value that does not fit and a division by zero are U2041, and Core IR holds the folded literal, so the checker no longer lists such a site as a pending obligation that the verifier never generates (the workspace test that compares the two sites failed on `main` after #2261).

## v0.5.1

The second increment of release R0.5, made from `main`, published as v0.5.1. The release is security fixes, clearer diagnostics and reports, and an installer that cleans up after an upgrade. No construct is added, and the editions are those of v0.5.0; one rule of the schedule changes, by the ADR 0117 amendment of 2026-11-22. Security: in the interpreter and under `--virtual-time`, a task that quiet code starts is held by its `tasks` statement and no longer reorders the public events of other tasks, the S-40 remainder (#1545); a policy call is a check point of the schedule of started tasks, so a `via` given data above `internal` in a program that starts tasks is refused with U4002, where its work could order the public prints of started tasks (#2214); a place that holds no key can no longer poison the registry's root-version floor (#2132, ADR 0122 amendment of 2026-11-19); `Keys.hmac` overwrites its copies of the key (#2120); the registry service, which is in no release, bounds a request's body as its head (#2121); the runtime reads the bytes held under a sequence lock, so a run that is ending reports its real stop and no spurious U6003 from a sibling's budget check (#2151). Behavior that changes: a package that starts tasks and releases data above `internal` through a policy is now refused (#2214); `una lsp` reports a refused message as U7016 and a session that reported one exits with code 1, where v0.5.0 exited with 2 or 0 (#2156, ADR 0036); `una report` prints the `where` conditions and `gives` labels of each `uses` entry (#864); `result`, `it` and `error` in a lambda are U2012 and U2013, not U8001 (#1989); U3013 and U3021 advise the repair that resolves them where the call stands (#837, #1931); an installation over an earlier one removes the files the new release does not hold (#1992), and the installer never writes through a hard link (#1966). Records and release tooling: the validation record holds the toolchain and musl lines (#1971), and the release check reads the glibc symbol versions of the Linux `una` and refuses an archive above the installer's floor, which stays 2.39 (pull request #2129, the check part of #2105). Release notes for readers: `docs/release-notes/0.5.1.md`. The targets are those of v0.5.0. Validation: `docs/validation/0.5.1.md`.

- **Tests: `una check` and `una run` agree on `[run] grants`** (#826, `spec/13 §5`). The behavior #925 fixed is now covered in all four cases: an empty list and a list that lacks a capability are U3012 at check time, as at run time; a list that grants it passes both; and a package that lists no grants passes `una check` and leaves the grants to `--grant`, so `una run` without a flag reports U3012 before the program starts.
- **U3013 advises the form that resolves it where the call stands** (#837, `spec/04 §4`, `spec/17 §3` rule 5). The advice to bind the result with `let` first stays where it resolves the diagnostic. Under a nested `attempt` the advice binds the whole `attempt`, wherever the call stands; in a lambda body, which holds no `let`, it names a named function; in the value of `if error use` or `if none use`, a branch of `if ... then ... else`, the right operand of `and`, `or` and `implies` and a one-line arm of `when`, where a `let` before the statement would run the call every time, it names a form that keeps the call conditional; in a `together` branch it names a function called from the branch's `let`; a call with `edit` or `take` under `attempt` is told to drop the `attempt` and use `if error`; and on a `do` line it names a function that edits a local copy. A call with `edit` or `take` that also has effects is now one U3013, not two with different advice. `una explain U3013` lists the cases. The code and the first part of the message do not change.
- **`una report` lists the conditions of ADR 0092 in `authority` and `handler_authority`** (#864, `spec/13 §6`): each `uses` entry is printed as the header writes it, so an operation keeps its condition and its label, `op gives label where C`, in the human output and in `--json`, as `una contract` shows them. A header with `uses @printer: Printer[print_label where not dry_run]` was reported as `@printer: Printer[print_label]`, more authority than it grants, and a header whose condition was deleted gave the same report as one that kept it. An operation without a condition or a label prints as before, so the reports of edition `0.1` and `0.2` packages do not change; the entries stay strings and the schema stays `una.report/2`.
- **Security (S-40 remainder): a task that quiet code starts no longer reorders the public events of other tasks** (#1545, `spec/27 §7` rule 4, the ADR 0117 amendment of 2026-11-22, accepted by the Owner). In the interpreter and in native code under `--virtual-time`, a `start` under a raised program counter, at `internal` since #1684, took a slot of its statement's bound, so a later public `start` could wait for it, and quiet code that parked, at a quiet `start` at the bound or at the end of a quiet `tasks` statement, let the queued tasks run first: the order of the other tasks' public events followed the data, the same in every run. Such a task is now held by its statement: it joins no queue and counts against no bound, and the task that runs the statement runs it on its own turn, at the front of the queue with a turn count of its own: at that task's switches (the end of its turn, a park, a wait in `next`) while the bound has a place free, at a quiet `start` that finds the statement holding as many tasks as its bound, and at the statement's end. A quiet `start` never waits, and the public events keep the order of the same run without the quiet code, in both engines, for every secret; a server loop that starts a task per request under a decision on the request still answers each request before it waits for the next. What remains: a held task that runs longer than a turn while another task is ready, or that waits itself, is let go and takes turns of its own, a count of check points that `internal` data decide (#1708). Native code under the real clock is unchanged; its order across tasks is real time, out of model. The proposed separate bounds for quiet tasks are withdrawn.
- **U3021 names the caller's binding and the handler that U3020 offers** (#1931, ADR 0093 rule 6, `spec/13 §3`). The narrowed grant of U3021's repair names the caller's binding, as U3020's does, instead of a placeholder `@y`; when several bindings qualify it lists them. When the callee declares an operation the ceiling withholds, the repair says that no grant of a binding keeps both and names a handler whose own `uses` lie within the ceiling, the repair U3020 gives for the same requirement, before leaving the choice to the consumer. The codes and the messages do not change.
- **The installer never writes into an existing file, and its TMPDIR guards are tested** (#1966, from the review of #1965): each file the installer writes (`bin/una.new`, `lib/libuna_rt.a`, `README.md`, `LICENSE`, the guides, the record) is written as a new one after its name is removed, so a target that is a hard link to another file, as root possibly outside `UNA_HOME`, no longer has that file written through. As root, the TMPDIR check now runs just before the work directory is made, so `--uninstall`, which makes none, no longer refuses an unsafe TMPDIR. An `id -u` that prints root's id with leading zeros is taken as root. The archive's digest is read from its bytes on stdin, so a TMPDIR whose name holds a newline no longer gives a false SHA-256 mismatch. New tests cover the default TMPDIR as root, a work directory made in the checked physical path and never through a link, and a target that is a directory or a FIFO.
- **The private validation record now holds the `toolchain:` and `musl:` lines of the release log** (#1971). `scripts/release-gates.sh` copied only the `strip:` lines into `record.md`, while `scripts/release.sh` said the Linux toolchain checked against `supply-chain/z3.lock` and the musl inputs checked against `supply-chain/musl.lock` went to the record. The record now has the sections "Linux toolchain" and "musl inputs", each line copied once and sorted, or a None line when the release gate did not run. No check changed: the checks still stop the archive before `una` is built, and the public summary keeps its fixed template, which copies neither. The three sections copy only lines of their expected shape, read under `LC_ALL=C`, and say how many others they did not copy. The summary refuses a record whose lines end anywhere but at a newline, so that a carriage return in a copied line cannot pose as its dependency audit.
- **`result`, `it` and `error` inside a lambda are U2012 and U2013** (#1989, `spec/02 §8`). A lambda's body is not a position of `result`, `it` or `error`, even when the lambda is written in an `expect` line, a `where` clause, the predicate of `checked` or an `if error` handler, and whether or not it is given for an `of` parameter, as to `map` or `fold`: the checker reports U2012 for `result` and U2013 for `it` and `error` at the use, where v0.4.0 and v0.5.0 rejected the package with the internal error U8001. A value the body needs is passed as an argument. The guide's new §23 lists the change. Elaboration also fails closed, with U8001, at three more places where a well-typed program never reaches: a call in a clause whose arguments leave a parameter without one, a condition written on a `uses` clause that no capability entry carries, and a name, constructor, call or `when` arm that resolved to something its syntax cannot elaborate to. An `if error` handler restores the binder of an enclosing one instead of clearing it.
- **An installation over an earlier one removes what the new release does not hold** (#1992, ADR 0124 rule 32): run over an earlier installation, `install.sh` removes the files of the earlier record that the new installation does not hold, such as the `bin/z3` link and `z3/bin/z3` of a release before 0.4.0, the `lib/libuna_rt.a` of one before ADR 0124 step 2 and a guide a later release dropped, then the directories of that record left empty, and names them. Every path of the earlier record is checked before anything is downloaded, as uninstall checks it, and for every user, not only root, a path with a `.` or empty component or with a directory on the way that is a symbolic link is refused (a recorded file may itself be a link, which is removed without what it names); the check runs again just before each removal, and a recorded name that is the same file or directory as one the new installation holds, however it is spelled, is never removed. Upgrading from v0.3.16 and then uninstalling leaves nothing in `~/.una`.
- **Wording of the command-line help, the installer and the `una ita verify` guide** (#2070, items 15, 20 and 39 of the 0.4.0 documentation review). The help of `--workers`, `--io-threads` and `--file-threads` of `una run` and `una test` says that they take effect in an executable of `una build` and where the interpreter, which runs its tasks on one thread, only checks them; the `--from` of `una ita add` and `una ita watch` says that the default `itaty` is not served by this release; `una ita` is the package manager of editions 0.4 and later, `una migrate` writes the later edition una.toml names, and `una run` also runs a `drive` or `serve` section. The installer no longer names 0.1.x, which it refuses earlier, when no validation summary can be read, and says what to check. The guide says why `una ita verify` reports a package whose copies match the lock as not verified (U7013): the registry client checks the key chain when it reads a release, and does not store it.
- **The release check reads the glibc floor of the Linux `una`** (#2105, `scripts/glibc-floor.sh`, `scripts/check-release.sh`, `docs/release-gates.md`). The check of the Linux archive refuses a packed `una`, or an executable it builds, that asks the loader for a `GLIBC_` symbol version above the floor `install.sh` refuses a system below (2.39 today), with a canary scan that must fail; the archive of 0.4.1 was measured (it needs 2.38 and 2.39 symbols from Z3, the C++ runtime and the standard library), which a later build recipe on an older glibc lowers to 2.34.
- **`Keys.hmac` overwrites its copies of the key** (#2120, from the review of #1481): the HMAC routine of both engines, `una_interp::hmac_sha256`, copied the key to a heap vector and made two more, the key under each pad, and freed the three without a wipe, with the states of its hashes. It now holds the padded key, its two pads (one buffer, changed in place), the hash state and the inner digest in fixed arrays on its stack, with no allocation but the result, and overwrites all of them with zeros before it returns; for a key longer than the block of the hash, the key's digest and the last bytes of the key that the padding copies are in the same arrays. The computation and the wipe are one function of a private module, so no code of the crate can compute the MAC without the wipe. It is not a volatile write: the zeros are written with `fill(0)` and kept by `std::hint::black_box`, as in `una_project::registry::keyfile::wipe`; `una-interp` forbids `unsafe`, which a volatile write needs. ADR 0133 rule 7 calls such a wipe removable by an optimizer. The zeros were measured to be written on aarch64-apple-darwin with the pinned toolchain 1.98.1, in the release and dev profiles; this was not measured on Linux or x86-64, and no test in the tree can observe it. A dependency feature and own hash code: `una-interp` and `una-rt` gain no dependency (`Cargo.lock` is unchanged, and neither depends on `una-crypto` or `zeroize`), but `una-interp` now enables the feature `compress` of `sha2`, which makes the SHA-256 compression function a public name for the whole build, and the padding and length encoding of SHA-256 are now the project's own code in both engines, where they were `sha2`'s hasher before (`docs/security/tcb.md` §3.4). Not overwritten, as before: what the compression function of `sha2` keeps in its own frame during a call, which in its software path (every aarch64 target, and x86-64 without the SHA extensions) is a copy of the chaining value, key-equivalent after the block of a pad, and a message schedule that holds the key under the pad; copies a compiler makes in registers; the keys in the run's table for the whole run; the key file's text and its decoding at start-up; and the bearer key that the operations of `Http` copy out of the table into the request they send. Results are bit-identical: the seven test cases of RFC 4231 and literal known answers for the empty key, the empty message and a key of one block pass on the routine, the cases whose data is a text pass through `Keys.hmac` of the native runtime, and the routine equals the former one over keys and data of every length around the block. No behavior of a program changes.
- **Security: the registry service bounds a request's body as it bounds its head, and refuses a registration without the operator's token before its body** (#2121, `una-itaty`, in no release). Follow-ups of the review of #2117. A head that declared a body and sent none of it held its connection slot for the 60 seconds of the idle timeout, with no credential: a body is now read under the head's rule, by the same code, with a body's numbers. At every moment the bytes of the body read since the head have arrived within a first allowance and a second per 64 KiB of them, within the total the body already had; no read waits past the idle timeout. For a note, a registration and a cosignature the first allowance is the first-byte timeout (5 seconds; the idle timeout when that is shorter): a body that does not come gives its slot back after 5 seconds with a 408, the longest a note's body holds a slot is the 6 seconds that cost its 64 KiB, and a slow client completes such a body when it arrives within 5 seconds and the time its bytes earned (a 64 KiB note needs about 11 KiB per second, a 2 KiB cosignature 0.4). A publication keeps the first allowance it had, the idle timeout (60 seconds), since at most two publications are read at once and so cannot take the connection slots: what a publisher needs does not change, a body of `S` bytes arriving at the service within 60 seconds and `S` / 64 KiB seconds, so the largest publication (260 MiB) needs 63.1 KiB per second, an uplink of about 520 kbit per second for 70 minutes, and an uplink of 400 kbit per second publishes up to 12 MiB. A `POST /register` whose head does not carry the operator's token is answered 403 (registration closed) or 401 (credential absent, malformed or wrong) with no byte of its body read; the discarding of a refused request's body, at most 64 KiB for at most a second, now ends with the body the head declared. New function `http::serve_from` serves the connections an `accept` function gives, each counted under the peer address given with it (`serve_with` gives the listener's); the refusal of a peer past `max_connections_per_peer` has its end-to-end test through it. `http::read_request` refuses such a registration before its body too.
- **The timing wheel counts the steps of `arm` and `cancel`, and the reactor's scaling test counts an insert into a freed slot** (#2130, `crates/una-http/src/wheel.rs`, `crates/una-rt/src/reactor.rs`; follow-ups of #2124). Tests only: outside the unit tests the wheel's slab and free list are `Vec` itself, through a type alias, as the reactor's table already was. In the unit tests of `una-http` each access to the wheel's slab and free list counts one step, and a new test holds `arm` to 7 steps (5 into a freed entry) and `cancel` to 7 (1 for a timer that is gone) at 1,000 to 100,000 timers that share one slot, with the total exactly those constants times the timers; a scan added to either fails it on any machine, where before only a wall-clock bound of the reactor's test saw one. The reactor's test registers a second time after its removes, with the free list at its longest, and holds that `insert` to 4 steps. A test of each type names every field and its type, so a collection added to the reactor's table or to the wheel does not compile until it is listed, and the documentation of both states that a collection that grows must be counted. The reactor's test no longer says that the wheel is counted there: it is counted in its own crate, by the ordinary tests, and not under Miri or AddressSanitizer. `scripts/sanitize.sh` now says that the timing of the reactor's test does not run under either.
- **The root-version floor holds the digest of the root metadata's text, so a place that holds no key cannot poison it** (#2132, security, `spec/30 §14`, ADR 0122 amendment of 2026-11-19). The floor's digest covered the whole signed note, signature lines included, and a client ignores the signature lines of keys it does not know. A mirror directory, or a bundle given to `una ita unbundle`, could therefore serve an honest root version with one junk signature line; when no accepted checkpoint named that version (a lock made under an earlier root version, and no latest checkpoint served), `una ita fetch` accepted it and stored the digest of the altered note, and every later command against the honest registry was refused as a fork (U7010) until the user removed a file. The floor now stores `<n> text sha256:<hex>`, the digest of the text that the root keys sign, and every comparison with the floor uses it: every form of one root text compares equal, and another digest at the floor's version is another root text, which a threshold of root keys signed. A floor of the earlier form, `<n> sha256:<hex>`, is still read and compared as it was stored, by the digest of the whole note; it is replaced once, under the floor lock, by the digest of the text of the same note, only when the chain walked and verified holds its version with exactly that note, online or from the provenance of a package that a load verifies against it; a refusal against such a floor names the floor file and says that what is served is another form or another text, not that the chain forked. The digest that `sources` pins, that a checkpoint names and that the lock records is unchanged. Storing a checkpoint as the floor also no longer holds the floor lock while it reads the registry's tiles for the consistency proof: a place whose reads stalled kept the lock past five seconds, and another run then ended with a note and exit code 0 without comparing its root chain; the proof is now made before the lock is taken, and under the lock the stored checkpoint must still be the one it was made against.
- **`Files` confinement: the record of #1459 corrected, and its rule tested where it was argued** (#2138, `spec/14 §3` rule 2; tests and documents, no behavior changes). The v0.5.0 entry of #1459 says that the value of a `Files` operation below a missing directory changed from `Io` to `NotFound` in two classes and in no other; there is a third, which v0.5.0 also changed and that entry does not name: a NUL in a component after the missing directory (`Files.read("missing/a\u{0}b")`), `Io` in v0.4.0, where the host's interface refused the path as written before any lookup, and `NotFound` from v0.5.0. It is pinned in both engines, with what did not change: a NUL in the final component of a path whose directory resolves is still the operation's own `Io`, and a NUL in a directory that confinement must resolve is `Denied`. The check of one long final component below a missing directory uses 300 bytes, more than the 255 a host takes in a name, where its 200 tested nothing. Three conformance cases state the rule and run in both engines: `runtime/files_missing_directory` (`NotFound` for a read and a write), `runtime/files_missing_final_component` (a read is `NotFound`, a write creates the file) and `runtime/files_component_not_directory` (`Io`). In the native runtime, `Files.read` and `Files.write` are now tested whole in a world that keeps every path it is asked about: below a missing directory no read and no write reaches it, and no lookup goes past that directory; what is still read in the source is that the two operations pass the machine to that code and nothing else. `spec/14 §3` rule 2 gains one sentence: the dangling symbolic link that is `Denied` is the directory or the grant prefix itself, and a grant prefix whose path passes through a dangling link does not exist, `NotFound`. The entry of UNA-SA-022 in `docs/security/advisories.md` keeps its account of v0.4.0 and adds the behavior since #1459.
- **The bytes held are never read torn by a change of the allocator's limit** (#2151): `cap` 0.1.2 derives the bytes the process holds as `limit - remaining` and changes its limit in two writes, so a read between them returned close to `usize::MAX`, and a sibling task's budget check or copy step that read it could stop the run with U6003 at its own site, where a run that was already ending should have reported its real stop. The runtime now changes the limit and reads the bytes held under its own sequence lock: a read that overlaps a change is retried until the change is done, whatever its length, and the allocation path is unchanged. A stop that ends a run also claims the end of the process before it lifts the limit, so a sibling that stops afterwards parks. The same lock covers the lift at the normal end of a run and the debug build's high-water mark, which skips a sample rather than wait in the allocator. No budget, ceiling or test bound changes.
- **`una lsp` reports a refused message as U7016, and `spec/13 §5` says what follows** (#2156, after #2100): each message of the editor that the language server refuses is now a diagnostic with the new code U7016 (domain `project`, with its `una explain` page), located at the empty `file` at 1:1. On stderr it is one line, `error[U7016]: <message>`, or with `--json` one `una.diagnostic/1` document a line, since stdout carries the protocol; the `window/showMessage` sent to the editor carries the same code and text. A refusal of a bound has the new key `limit` of `una.diagnostic/1`, `{"name","bound","seen"}`, as `{"name":"MAX_VALUES","bound":131072,"seen":131073}` (`seen` is `null` for a `Content-Length` that does not fit in 64 bits); the key is additive and omitted otherwise, so the schema stays `una.diagnostic/1`, and the human output of any command prints it as a note. A message past `MAX_DEPTH` or `MAX_VALUES` is dropped and the session goes on; a header past a bound or not UTF-8, a missing or malformed `Content-Length`, a body that is not JSON and a header or a body cut short end the session. An input that ended inside a header, which ended the session silently with exit code 0, is now such a refusal. A `Content-Length` value quoted in a refusal is cut at 64 characters with its control and format characters escaped, so the editor's bytes cannot forge the line on a terminal; the rule is now `una_diag::text`, shared with `una-project`. Behavior that changes: a session that reported a refusal now exits with code 1, as every diagnostic with a code does (ADR 0036), where a fatal refusal exited with 2 and a dropped message with 0; a failure to read the input, or to write the notification of a refusal, exits with code 2. The texts of the refusals name their bound (`(MAX_HEADER_LINE)`, `(MAX_HEADER_LINES)`, `(MAX_CONTENT_LENGTH)`), and a body cut short says how many of its bytes arrived. No bound changes.
- **The guides name v0.5.0 where they state the current release** (#2194): `SECURITY.md`, the known limits, the language guide and the pages on editions and on Itá say what holds in v0.5.0 (edition `0.4` still a preview, no registry served, no fuzz target for the HTTP parsers, the targets of the release archives). The facts are those of v0.4.0, unchanged.
- **Security: a policy call is a check point of the schedule of started tasks** (#2214, review H1 of PR #2212, `spec/27 §8` rule 5). A policy call runs its `via`, whose code the label rules do not check, but the checker returned from the policy's branch before it recorded the call as a check point, so rule 5 of #1684 never saw it: a `via` whose work depended on its argument ordered the public prints of started tasks by that argument, at `secret` as at `internal`, in the interpreter under both clocks and natively under `--virtual-time`, and the program was accepted with an empty report. The call is now recorded with the deep label of its argument joined with the program counter: above `internal`, in a program that starts tasks, it is U4002; at `internal` it is accepted and listed in the report's `information.timing` as a `task switch` (the residual of #1708); with a `public` argument nothing changes. Elaboration builds the call as any other call (`push_call`). Programs that release data above `internal` through a policy inside a package that starts tasks are now refused, whatever the policy's `to` label.

## v0.5.0

The first increment of release R0.5, made from `main`, published as v0.5.0 (increments are weekly from 2026-11-20): each increment now publishes the version it is named for (ADR 0139 amendment of 2026-11-20, #2173), and no v0.4.1 is published. The release is security fixes, and the records and release tooling changed since v0.4.0. The language and its editions do not change. Security: the suggestion of a requirement name no longer overflows or probes quadratically (#1474); `una lsp` bounds the header lines and the body of the frames it reads (#1476); a registry root floor of the earlier form takes the digest of its own version, and another digest at that version is refused (#1970); the registry service, which is in no release, holds a connection's slot through its response, bounds the writing of a response and its connection threads, and sends files in chunks (#1517). Advisories UNA-SA-020, UNA-SA-021 and UNA-SA-022, all fixed in v0.4.0, are in `docs/security/advisories.md`. The bullets of #1997 to #2052 describe changes that the v0.4.0 tag already holds: they merged after the v0.4.0 section was written. From this release, the complete release gate set runs every night on `main`, and a release uses the night's record of its commit (#2084); the gate `site` is now `docs` (#2081). Second increment, all security or records. A security fix for released versions: a `Files` grant on the current directory was not confined against symbolic links, in every release up to v0.4.0, and now is (#2144); with it, grant paths that go up two or more levels mean what they say. A missing directory under a `Files` grant is `NotFound` with no path given to the operating system (#1459); `==` on `Hashed` values, the Poly1305 final mask, the HKDF scratch block and `Keys.hmac` are tightened (#1481); six `unsafe` blocks of `una-sys` are safe code, and the line cap of its base code is restated and enforced by a test (#1483, #2115, ADR 0058 amendment of 2026-11-19); a step refused for the memory budget is no longer visible to sibling tasks (#1943, ADR 0113 amendment of 2026-11-19); a run that finds another digest held for its root version fails with U7010 (#2099); `una lsp` bounds the values and the depth of a frame and reports a refused one (#2100); the registry service bounds idle connections (#2098); the installer's uninstall removes the proof cache key (#2097). The first nightly release run found three faults of tests and records, fixed here (#2111, #2124), and the release gates now time the cryptography (#2119). Release notes for readers are in `docs/release-notes/` (#2134). The targets are those of v0.4.0. Validation: `docs/validation/0.5.0.md`.

- **Requirement name suggestions no longer overflow or probe quadratically** (#1474, security: the U1005 suggestion for a requirement identifier whose derived name ends in `_18446744073709551615` and is taken overflowed a u64, a checker panic in debug builds and a wrap in release builds; when the number space is exhausted the suggestion now appends `_1`, as in `X_18446744073709551615_1`; the free number is found by one binary search per identifier over the identifiers read once, O(n log n) for n identifiers instead of O(n^2) on a run of taken names; suggestions for ordinary names are unchanged)
- **The language server bounds the frames it reads** (#1476, security, ADR 0063 rule 2): `una lsp` read a header line with no bound, accepted any number of header lines and reserved a body of any `Content-Length` before reading it, so a peer on its input could make it hold memory without limit. A header line now holds at most 4 KiB, a frame at most 16 header lines and a body at most 64 MiB; input past a bound is refused before anything of its size is buffered or reserved, and the session ends as at the end of the input. Real editors are not affected: their headers are under 100 bytes and their documents far below the body limit.
- **Security: the registry service bounds the writing of a response and its connection threads** (#1517, `una-itaty`, in no release). A connection gave back its slot before its response was written, the write had no timeout, a file was read whole into memory before it was sent, and `serve` started a thread for every connection and stopped when one could not start, so clients that never read a large file could hold its bytes and a thread each without limit. A connection now holds its slot to its response's last byte; a response is written with no write waiting more than the write timeout (60 seconds) and, past it, at least 64 KiB per second on average, the mirror of the rule for bodies; a file is sent from the open file in parts of 64 KiB, the version that was opened, whole; `serve` takes the slot before it starts the connection's thread, so it runs at most `max_connections` of them and answers a connection past the bound 503 with no thread; and a thread that cannot start closes its connection and the loop goes on.
- **The proof cache key's owner check is tested without the temporary directory** (#1896): with `TMPDIR` naming nothing, a read-only directory or an empty one, a private key of the user is used and the empty directory stays empty; a key file of another user is refused as before (#1164). The check reads the effective user through `una_sys::server::identity` since #1894.
- **A root-version floor of the earlier form takes the digest of its own version** (#1970, security, ADR 0122 amendment of 2026-11-09 L3, `spec/30 §14`): a floor written as the version alone, as builds of `main` before v0.4.0 write it, now records the digest of that version's root metadata the first time a command walks and verifies the root chain at that version (`una ita lock`, `una ita fetch`, `una ita unbundle`), so `una check` and the offline loads then refuse a forked root of that version (U7010) instead of comparing by version alone; a floor that holds a digest is never replaced at an equal version, and another digest there is not stored and is reported as a fork.
- **The owner replay judges each entry as logged, and replays only key events** (#1997): each entry of an owner's chain is judged once against the chain as known when it was logged, and claims of compromise are settled in one pass from the last claim to the first; releases and yanks are judged as logged and never refuse a name, and a release or a yank its owner's keys as logged do not sign is skipped and recorded as evidence against the registry; contested chains count nothing; a claim signed by the key it names, or repeating a compromise already counting, is not logged; every owner keeps a threshold of at least two and at least one key more than its threshold, from 3 to 32 keys, after its registration and every key event, so a single stolen key signs nothing that counts (the floor counts keys, not keys that can still sign: an owner whose own claims leave a current key compromised, whose chain reaches the cap of claims, or with two keys stolen, registers under a new owner name with new keys); an owner chain holds at most 64 claims; owner chains that hold recoveries, disputes, co-signer changes, cancellations, overrulings or registrations with recovery cosigners are not replayed by this release and make the owner's names unavailable (U7013); the registry service reads owner entries with the client's parser and checks registrations, key events, releases and yanks with the client's rule (ADR 0122 Amendment 14, `spec/30` §14 to §16).
- **The `sanitize` gate's Miri phase ends** (#2007): it did not finish in 2.5 hours. A test waited for clock phases up to 999,000 ns within a millisecond, which Miri's simulated clock, moving in steps of 5,000 ns, never reads; its part on the run's clock is now a test of its own, ignored under Miri, and its arithmetic still runs there. Three scaling tests (the reactor's table, a chunked body, the sockets' gate) and the deep list of ADR 0113 run smaller under Miri, as the earlier reductions do; two trace tests that use the file system, which Miri's isolation refuses, and the timing test of the walk at `start`, which Miri ran for over 18 minutes even at 1,600 values, are ignored there; the reactor's tests keep their run stops in a static, so Miri no longer reports them leaked; and two tests ignored in the code but missing from `scripts/sanitize-miri-ignored.txt` are listed. The script and `docs/release-gates.md` state 26 mentions of `miri`, and `docs/security/tcb.md` the 121 tests Miri does not run.
- **A solver child past its processor-time limit is `unknown` with reason `cpu`, not U8001** (#2008): Z3 5.1.0 handles `SIGXCPU` itself, printing `timeout` and exiting with status 0; the `limits` gate now runs a query that no solver answers within the limit, on fresh copies of the example with no proof cache.
- **The `sanitize` gate's fuzz target `registry_root_chain` fits its ceiling** (#2024): its count, never measured, was 350,000 inputs, but the target signs up to 24 documents an input and runs about 500 inputs a second under AddressSanitizer, so the run stopped at the ceiling of 600 s with 322,234 inputs and the gate failed. Its count is now 150,000 (about 300 s at the slowest measured speed). The ceiling, the policy and the floors are unchanged.
- **Linux toolchain pin refreshed** (#2028, libc6 and libc6-dev 2.39-0ubuntu8.9 -> 2.39-0ubuntu8.12 in the aarch64 table of supply-chain/z3.lock, after an Ubuntu update of the image)
- **The `site` gate checks the book and the website samples with the `una` that the release archive ships** (#2029): the gate used the clone's `target/release/una`, whose Z3 library is the machine's cache and which embeds no runtime, so the book's reports (`solver build: ... library`, `runtime sha256:`) never matched. It now unpacks the release gate's archive of the host (`scripts/site-shipped-una.sh`: digest checked, version checked, no fallback; a missing archive fails). The docs bundle step is unchanged.
- **Test: the helper servers of the HTTP tests no longer share one port file** (#2030, a test that started a silent server could wait 30 seconds for a port file that its sibling test had already read and removed)
- **Docs: Getting started reaches a refuted contract right after the install** (#2038, the tutorial is reordered around the same tested code blocks; the details of installing, such as supported systems and installing as root, move to a section at its end as a table and lists)
- **Explain: the explanations of U0001 to U2034 say what happened, why and how to fix it, in that order** (#2039, the prose of 26 explanations is rearranged under the headings "Why Una rejects it" and "How to fix it"; no fact, citation or example changes)
- **Explain: the explanations of U2035 to U3032 say what happened, why and how to fix it, in that order** (#2039, the prose of 26 explanations is rearranged under the headings "Why Una rejects it" and "How to fix it"; no fact, citation or example changes)
- **Explain: the explanations of U3033 to U8001 say what happened, why and how to fix it, in that order** (#2039, the prose of 26 explanations is rearranged under the headings "Why Una rejects it" and "How to fix it"; no fact, citation or example changes)
- **Docs: the known limits and the lock page state what this release does** (#2040, they no longer list work planned for a later release)
- **Docs: the guides, the status lines of the specification and the trusted base page state what 0.4.0 does** (#2052, the registry pages say that no registry is served and path dependencies are what is usable; the trusted base page lists the registry client, the resolver and the key-chain verification as part of the base)
- **Explain: the explanations state their edition scope and what 0.4.0 reads** (#2052, the standard modules, `una.toml` and `ita.una` settings, the host capabilities and the linker are named per edition and target; edition 0.5 content is labeled a preview; registry texts say that only `file://` mirror directories are read; U2039 and U3032 say that 0.4.0 does not report them; `una verify` and the Windows line are removed; no example changes)
- **The installer accepts a release that its record marks `ACCEPTED BY THE OWNER`** (#2052). A record committed for a tag may carry the verdict line `Verdict: ACCEPTED BY THE OWNER`, the owner's recorded decision for a release whose gates did not all pass on its commit; `install.sh` accepts it like `PASS (every gate)` and still checks the archive's digest against the record, which it reads from the tag's tree. v0.4.0 is released this way: its gates ran in full on its parent candidate (19 of 20 passed) and the `release` gate ran on its commit.
- **Docs: the README, CONTRIBUTING and the guides state what 0.4.0 does** (#2052, `@` marks direct uses of authority and `uses` covers calls; the installer also accepts a repacked archive and needs `id` and, on Linux, `getconf`; the Lean model proves a fragment only; `una lsp` takes no `--json`; native tasks run on `--workers` threads; the language server proves on open and save; registry packages are read only from a `file://` mirror; contributors build with `UNA_Z3_LIB`)
- **The public tree no longer tracks the maintainers' working files** (#2052, #2049, the architecture decision records, the plan, the review and history records, the test suites, the scripts but the two that build the Z3 library, and the agent configuration are kept by the maintainers; `cargo build --release -p una-cli`, `cargo fmt --check` and `cargo clippy` run from a clean clone on macOS arm64; the public tree has no continuous-integration workflow)
- **The test suites, the scripts, the architecture decision records, the plan and the review records are in the repository again** (#2079, `tests/`, the integration tests of each crate, the case lists of `una-test-cases`, the test runner configuration `.config/nextest.toml`, `scripts/`, `docs/adr/`, `docs/plan/` and `docs/reviews/` are tracked as before #2052, since the tests and the release gates read them; a clean clone runs the whole suite with `cargo nextest run --workspace --locked`)
- **The `site` release gate becomes `docs`, and the website leaves the compiler's verdict** (#2081): the gate keeps the link check of the repository's documents and the docs bundle, its files checked against the neutrality pattern, and no longer clones, installs or builds the website, so it needs neither Node.js nor npm nor access to the website's or the examples' repository. The website is validated by its own flow against a published docs bundle. `scripts/release-summary.py` still accepts the records of earlier releases, whose documentation gate is a `site` row.
- **The nightly run of a commit is its release gate run** (#2084, `docs/release-gates.md` rule 2 and section 4: the complete gate set runs every night on the head of `main`, and a tag may use the night's run of the gated SHA when every gate is PASS; what a PASS means does not change)
- **Advisories UNA-SA-020 and UNA-SA-021** (#2088, `docs/security/advisories.md`: the integer-text fix of #923 and the trace-outcome fix of #1552, both fixed in v0.4.0)
- **Advisory UNA-SA-022** (#2090, `docs/security/advisories.md`: the `Files` confinement fix of #1380, fixed in v0.4.0)
- **The installer's default version waits for the tag** (#2101, `install.sh`, `docs/release-gates.md`). The release pull request leaves `DEFAULT_VERSION` at the last published release, and a pull request after the publication moves it, so the public install command never asks for a release that does not exist yet; the test of the installer's pins accepts the workspace version or the release before it.
- **`Files`: a missing directory is `NotFound`, and the operation gives the operating system no path** (#1459, `spec/14 §3` rule 2, security hardening). When the confinement of `Files` finds that an intermediate directory of a path, or the grant prefix, does not exist, it now answers `NotFound` itself (`Io` where a component is not a directory, as before), in `una run` and in native executables, instead of handing the operation the path as the program wrote it, whose links it had not checked; so no operation receives a path other than a canonical directory inside the grant joined with the final component. The value changes from `Io` to `NotFound`, the value `spec/14 §3` states, wherever the path as written failed as a whole for another reason before the missing directory was reached: more symbolic links than the host follows in one lookup (33 on macOS), or a path as written longer than the host takes (1024 bytes on macOS, 4096 on Linux). No other value changes, and no path moves between success, failure and `Denied`: a file that does not exist in an existing directory is read (`NotFound`) and created as before. Note of 2026-11-22: there is a third such class, which this release also changed and this entry did not name: a NUL in a component after the missing directory (`Files.read("missing/a\u{0}b")`) was `Io` in v0.4.0, where the host's interface refused the path as written before any lookup, and is `NotFound` from v0.5.0 (#2138).
- **Constant-time and key hygiene in the runtime and the cryptography** (#1481, security, defense in depth, ADR 0133 rules 5, 7 and 8): `==` on two `Hashed` values reads every byte of both in each engine, through one routine, instead of stopping at the first byte that differs, and the native runtime no longer copies the two values to compare them; the mask of the final select of Poly1305 passes the optimization barrier that every other select of `una-crypto` uses; HKDF-Expand wipes its scratch block on every path, the error of a block step included (a path no hash of the crate takes); `Keys.hmac` in a native executable no longer copies the key out of the table of the run, or its name and the data out of the heap, to make the call (the HMAC routine of both engines still makes unwiped copies of the padded key for each call, and `Http` still clones its bearer key, which this change leaves as they are); and the variable-time routines of ECDSA verification and the table-indexed Montgomery exponentiation carry `_vartime` in their names, each use stating why its operands are public, which a test checks. No behavior of a program changes. The scaling test of `una-crypto` (`tests/perf.rs`) no longer times a 32 MiB allocation and copy with each pass.
- **The `limits` gate counts the confinement test added with #1483** (#1483, `scripts/limits.sh`). The solver child's confinement has 13 cases on macOS and 9 on Linux, one more than before: `a_source_below_three_reaches_the_child_as_the_file_it_was`, which runs on both. The gate requires each run to pass exactly its count of cases, so the old counts failed it, and `release_gates.rs` failed on `main`.
- **Safety: six `unsafe` blocks of `una-sys` become safe code** (#1483, `una-sys`). The socket address that `server::listen` binds is a `sockaddr_in` or `sockaddr_in6` written field by field, in place of a zeroed `sockaddr_storage` read through two pointer casts (3 blocks); the solver child's spawn duplicates a source below descriptor 3 with `BorrowedFd::try_clone_to_owned` and refuses a duplicate that lands below 3, in place of a raw `fcntl` and `OwnedFd::from_raw_fd` (2 blocks); and the event buffer of the reactor starts from a struct literal, in place of `mem::zeroed` (1 block). The system calls, their flags and their errors are unchanged. The trusted modules hold 130 `unsafe` blocks, down from 136 (`docs/security/tcb.md`). Tests: a child whose standard output is the parent's descriptor 0 receives that file, and a listener is bound to the address and port asked for in both families.
- **Real-clock races are tested for being runs the specification allows** (#1516, `crates/una-cli/tests/native.rs`, `docs/compiler/testing.md`). On the real clock a native `first of` runs its branches concurrently, so a losing branch may print before the winner returns and may or may not reach its `cancelled` event, where the interpreter runs the branches in index order and never starts the loser: both are outcomes `spec/27 §6` rules 3, 4 and 6, `spec/19 §6` rule 4 and ADR 0117 rule 12 allow, and under `--virtual-time` the engines agree exactly, as before. No engine changes. New tests run five programs of races (a loser about to print, a loser that has finished, three branches, a loser that computes, nested races) fifty times each natively, with one worker and with several, and once in the interpreter, and hold every run to the rules no schedule changes: the winner's events first, the other branches' in index order, at most one `cancelled` event as a loser's last, the output's lines those of the events, and `seq` with no gap.
- **A step refused for the memory budget is no longer visible to the steps of sibling tasks** (#1943, `una-rt`). A step of a copy between tasks (ADR 0113 rule 4) added its bytes to the process-wide count of announced bytes before it was checked, and a refused step took them back only afterwards. In that interval the check of a step in a sibling task counted the refused bytes, so a step that fit the budget could be refused too, and the U6003 could be reported at the sibling instead of, or as well as, at the step that asked for too much. A step is now checked first and announced only when it fits, by a compare-exchange loop that is O(1) expected and lock-free, and the sum of the announced bytes can no longer wrap when several large steps are announced at once. Outside a run, where no budget applies, a step that would take the announced bytes past the largest count the machine holds is now refused with U6003 (2^64 bytes on a 64-bit target), where it was accepted before. No stop of a released program was observed.
- **Install: a macOS archive downloaded in a browser, and an uninstall that removes the proof cache key** (#2097). The README, the Getting started guide and the known limits say that the installer is the supported way to install, and that the `una` of a macOS archive downloaded in a browser, which is ad hoc signed and not notarized, carries the `com.apple.quarantine` attribute and is killed by macOS when it starts (exit code 137, no output), with the remedy: the installer, or `xattr -d com.apple.quarantine <file>` on the unpacked `una`. `sh install.sh --uninstall` now also removes `~/.una/proof-cache.key`, which `una` makes at run time, and then `~/.una` when it is empty; it removes a key that is a symbolic link without touching the file the link names, keeps a key file that `UNA_PROOF_KEY` names and every file it did not make, and its message says what it removed and names what is left.
- **Security: the registry service bounds connections that send no request, refuses a missing timeout and survives every passing `accept` error** (#2098, `una-itaty`, in no release). Follow-ups of the review of #1517. A connection that sent nothing, or a byte now and then, held its slot for the 30 seconds of the head, so a few such connections a second kept every slot busy: a request's first byte now arrives within the first-byte timeout (5 seconds) of the moment the connection starts to be read, and the head so far within that timeout and a second per KiB received, so holding a slot for the 21 seconds the largest head allows costs its 16 KiB. One peer (an IPv4 address or an IPv6 /64) that is not a loopback address holds at most `max_connections_per_peer` slots (64) and is answered 503 past them; that bounds one address, not a client with several. A zero timeout left the socket with none, and a refused timeout was ignored: `Service::new` now refuses a configuration with a zero timeout or a zero bound, and a socket that refuses a timeout is neither read nor written. `serve` stopped on `ENOBUFS`, `EPROTO`, `ENETUNREACH`, `EHOSTUNREACH` and the other passing errors of `accept`: only an unusable listener (`EBADF`, `EINVAL`, `ENOTSOCK`, `EFAULT`) ends it at once now; every other failure is tried again, a failure of one connection after 5 ms at most and the others after a pause that doubles from 5 ms to a second, and `serve` ends with the error once failures that do not pass by themselves went on through 30 seconds of pauses with no connection accepted; `serve` puts its listener in blocking mode. A connection can hold two descriptors, its socket and the file it is sent: the need of 2 x `max_connections` + 16 open descriptors is now documented, for the operator to set.
- **A run that loses the root-floor race fails instead of ending with a note** (#2099, security, `spec/30 §14`, ADR 0122 amendment of 2026-11-09, L3). When two runs of `una ita` read a registry's root-version floor at the same time, each compared the root chain it walked with the floor it had read, so the second one to store could find that another digest is held for the same root version and still succeed with a note. The store now compares the whole chain walked with the floor again under the floor lock: a stored floor whose version the chain holds with another digest refuses the place, or the bundle of `una ita unbundle`, with U7010, naming the floor file `registry/<id>/root-version` in Itaoca, before its checkpoint is stored and before a package of the bundle is written, and the stored floor is kept. This also covers a chain that goes to a higher version and holds another digest at the floor's version, which before replaced the floor. The same digest from both runs, and a higher version of the same chain, pass as before; a floor that cannot be stored for another reason is still a note.
- **The language server bounds the memory of a parsed frame and reports a refused one** (#2100, security, ADR 0063 rule 2): a body within the 64 MiB limit could still make `una lsp` hold about 100 times its size once parsed (`[{"":0},{"":0},...]` of 64 MiB made more than 6 GiB of values). A body is now refused, before any value is made, when it is nested deeper than 127 arrays and objects or has more than 131,072 values (elements and members); one frame then holds at most 320 MiB while it is parsed and 128 MiB after. A refused frame is no longer silent: one line on stderr and a `window/showMessage` notification name the limit. A frame past the depth or the value bound is dropped and the session goes on; a header or a `Content-Length` past its bound, a body that is not JSON and a body cut short end `una lsp` with exit code 2, where it used to end with 0. Real editors are not affected: their largest structured message, `initialize`, has a few thousand values.
- **The check of released changelog sections reads a record of amended sections, and two scaling tests measure processor time** (#2111). `scripts/check-released-sections.sh` failed on every commit since the neutral rewording of the released sections `## v0.3.4` and `## v0.3.8`, since it had no way to record a deliberate amendment. `scripts/released-sections-amended.txt` now holds one line per amendment: the tag, the SHA-256 of the amended section and the card.
