The failures a deadline causes carry the timing label of the loop's body, which one checker rule now computes for every site with a deadline and for the driven step: a body whose timing depends on data above `internal` is U4001 at the loop. `una report` lists each `for ever within` with its timing label in `information.timing`. A host stop and the deadline cut the same waits, whichever comes first. `within` stays reserved elsewhere.
- **Virtual time and `stop after` in tests, edition 0.4 preview** (#945, ADR 0116 rule 19, `spec/26 §8`). An example may end with `stop after N`: a host stop then arrives once `N` iterations of `for ever` loops have started, every `for ever` stops at its next iteration start with a `stop` event and runs its `on stop`, and only under that line may the `do` line call a function that says `diverges` (U3027 otherwise). Time in a test is virtual: a clock from 0 that never waits, which `std.testing.elapse(d: Int of Millisecond)`, with `requires d >= 0`, moves forward at once. `una run` and executables take `--virtual-time`, which gives the run that clock: `Clock.now` reads it, the driver's ticks jump to their due moments, so a tick a day runs at once with `missed` 0, and both engines give the same output and trace, the clock's readings included. Before edition 0.4, `stop after` does not parse and `elapse` is not in `std.testing`. The deadline of `for ever within` is measured on the run's clock in both engines, so under virtual time it passes only when the virtual clock reaches it; `--deadline` stays on the wall clock.
- **The formal model covers ADR 0116** (#946, `spec/26 §9`). F1 gains Core's exits (`stop` and `skip`), `for until` and `for ever` with `on stop`, and the host's stop schedule. T1 and T6 cover them, T6 for every fixed stop schedule; new theorems prove that a decreasing measure ends a `for until` and that a host stop ends a `for ever` at its stop point; erasure, host independence, authority confinement and trace safety extend to them. The re-check of `una.core/1` reads and checks the new forms: a `stop` or `skip` above the public program counter, and a `for until` condition that may fail, are reported as not re-checked, never accepted, and a `for ever within` is not re-checked, since deadlines are timing. The iteration budget of `--stop-after-iterations` and `stop after`, per loop instance since S-36, is such a fixed schedule; the trace events that the runtime appends by itself, a host stop's `stop` event among them, are outside the model (S-39). The host-driven step is modeled under a fixed schedule as a loop over the ticks it delivers. `formal/Theorems.lean` states the new theorems, with no `sorry` and no axiom beyond Lean's own.
- **A hash table made in `on stop` or in a `for until` condition gets its key** (#1199). The analysis that decides whether a run reads the process key (ADR 0119 rule 9) now walks the `on stop` block of a loop and the condition of `for until`. A run whose only table came from one of them stopped with an internal error at the table's creation; it now reads the key before `main` and runs.
- **A `for until` condition keeps the bindings it reads again** (#1202). The interpreter now counts the condition as evaluated later while the loop runs, so a binding that the condition passes to a call is not given up before the next check. A program such as `for until done(xs, n: count)` stopped with U8001 in the interpreter while native code ran it.
- **A driven module's functions take no precondition** (#1589, `spec/26 §2`), edition 0.4 preview. A `requires` on `init`, `step` or `on_stop` of the module a `drive` section names, or a refinement at any depth of the type of a parameter or of the result of any of them (at its top, in a type argument such as `Option[Pos]` or `List[Pos]`, or in a function type), written there or carried by a refined kind, is U7008 at the section's `module`, with the clause or the type as the related span. The host calls these functions with no fact it establishes, so the verifier assumed a precondition that nothing proved; the same rule holds for `handle` and `on_stop` of a `serve` module (`spec/31 §7`).
- **The state of a driven module keeps its labels** (#1642, `spec/26 §2` rule 2). The host hands the state of a `drive` module from `init` to `step` and from `init` and `step` to `on_stop`, and that hand-off is now a flow into the parameter's declared type, checked at every position of the type: `init() -> secret Int` with `step(edit count: Int, input: Tick)` is U4001 at the type of `count`, where before it checked clean and `una run` could print the `secret` value on every tick. A state labeled higher in `step` or `on_stop` than in the result of `init` stays legal. Edition `0.4` preview: a driven module whose state parameter is labeled below the state it receives is now rejected; label the parameter at least as high.
- **The state of a driven module is one type with labels aside, function types included** (#1644, `spec/26 §2` rule 2). The host's shape check of `una run` and `una build` compared the labels inside a function type of the state. So `init() -> fn(Int) -> Int` with `step(edit f: fn(Int) -> secret Int, ...)`, which `una check` accepts, was refused with exit code 2. It now runs in both engines; the labels are the checker's (U4001 at each hand-off, #1642). The run-time check also ignored refinements. Before U7008 (#1641) refused every refinement in the state in `una check`, `init() -> Int` with `step(edit n: Pos, ...)` ran with a state nothing proved positive: the interpreter stopped at a division by zero the verifier had discharged, and the native build, which elides that check, printed a wrong result. As defense in depth behind U7008, the run-time check now compares refinements predicate by predicate and in order at every position, and a function type's `uses` and `fails` exactly; a difference is the usage error of the shapes, exit code 2.
- **The state of a driven module keeps its units of measure** (#1647, `spec/26 §2` rule 2), edition `0.4` preview. The host hands the state of a `drive` module from `init` to `step` and from `init` and `step` to `on_stop` unchanged, and the units of the three types are now compared at every position, at the top, in each type argument and in function types: `init() -> Int of Second` with `step(edit state: Int of Millisecond, input: Tick)` is U2028 at the type of `state`, as the same flow is in ordinary code, where before it checked clean and `una run` took 7 seconds as 7 milliseconds. Core IR has no units, so `una run` and `una build` compare the units the checker recorded as well, and refuse a difference as a usage error, exit code 2. A driven module whose state changes units at a hand-off is now rejected; declare the state with one unit and convert it in the body.
- **A program that handles stops runs under a soft limit of open files below 514** (#1757). The wake pipe of the stop handlers moved to descriptor 512 or above, which `fcntl` refuses at or above the limit, so every such run under a limit of 513 or less, launchd's default of 256 among them, ended with the internal error U8001; the pipe now moves as high as the limit allows, never onto a standard stream, and a limit below 16 or too few free descriptors is a usage error, in both engines.
- **The guide's page for loops that run until stopped** (#1801, ADR 0116, `spec/26`): `stop` and `skip`, `for until c decreases m`, `for ever` and `on stop`, `diverges`, `for ever within`, a test's `stop after N`, and the host stops (SIGINT and SIGTERM, `--deadline`, `--stop-grace` and the hard stop, `--stop-after-iterations`, `--virtual-time`), each example checked; linked from the `for` page, the language guide and the READMEs.
- **`examples/metronome` and the driven programs guide** (#1802, `spec/26 §2`), edition 0.4 preview. The package names its module in a `drive` section of `ita.una`, with a tick every 500 milliseconds; `init`, `step` and `on_stop` count the beats of bars of four, and `una run . --stop-after-iterations 5` prints the same five beats and the stop in both engines. `docs/guide/driven-programs.md` explains the `drive` section, the three functions, stopping, testing and the mistakes the checker catches. The example and native differential tests now run a driven package with a stop after five steps.
- **`una run --help` describes `--stop-after-iterations` correctly** (#1806, `spec/26 §6` rule 1). The help text called the flag a request for a host stop once N iterations had started. It is a budget of N iterations for each `for ever` loop instance, the driver's loop included, which ends at the start of its iteration N + 1; it is no host stop request and starts no grace period. Only the text changes; the runtime already did this.
- **A failing `init` of a driven module names `init`** (#1809, `spec/26 §2` rule 2). When `init` of a `drive` section fails, `una run` and the native executable report U6001 as `` `init` of the driven module `m` failed with `V` ``, where they said `` `main` failed with `V` `` although the package has no `main`. The position, the shown failure and the exit code 1 are unchanged.

### Tasks and time

- **Tasks run as coroutines on a few workers** (#966, ADR 0117 rules 1 to 3, `spec/27`). In native code each task of `together` and `together for` runs on a coroutine with a guard-paged stack from a pool, pinned to one of `--workers` threads; a join parks its task instead of blocking a thread, and `Files`, a blocked `Console.print` and `Http` run on helper pools (`--file-threads`, `--io-threads`) while their task parks. A program without tasks runs as before, with no worker and no helper. The stack each task uses now counts toward `--max-memory`. New flags on executables, `una run` and `una test`: `--workers`, `--task-depth`, `--io-threads` and `--file-threads`, each a usage error (exit code 2) when it is not a number or out of range. The context switch and the stacks live in `una-sys`, behind a safe interface: a panic inside a coroutine aborts the process, and dropping a suspended coroutine aborts without running code on its stack. Soundness: a task's worker depends only on the program's structure (#1145, `spec/27 §1` rule 2). The `i`-th part of a block, `i` counted from 1, runs on worker `(w + i) mod N`, `w` being the worker of the task that runs the block (0 for the root, which runs on its own thread), instead of the next worker of a counter shared by the whole run, which carried how many tasks earlier code had started, possibly decided by `secret` data, into the timing of a later `public` block, and a deadline around that block into a value (ADR 0116 IF6). A program that holds a task construct or a `for ever` starts every worker and both helper pools at their full sizes, and fills the stack pool with stacks of one size, before `main` (#1145, ADR 0116 amendment P8, `spec/27 §1` rule 4), so whether a thread exists is never state that earlier code left; a thread that cannot start, or a stack that cannot be mapped, stops the run there with U6003, and a program without these constructs starts no thread. Release note: an executable whose program has tasks or a `for ever` reserves, before `main`, 64 task stacks of the size `--max-depth` gives, about 21 GiB of address space at the default 10,000, of which it uses only what its tasks touch; under strict overcommit (Linux `vm.overcommit_memory=2`) the reservation can fail, and the run then stops with U6003 before `main`; a lower `--max-depth` shrinks it. The real-clock oracle of these fixes, `crates/una-cli/tests/timing_channels.rs`, is ignored in the ordinary test run and runs in the new step "Timing channels (security oracles)" of `scripts/local-ci.sh` on native hosts and in the new release gate `timing-channels`.
- **`within` bounds the time of an expression** (#967, ADR 0117 rules 4, 5 and 13, IF2, `spec/27 §5`), in edition `0.4`. `let quote = fetch_quote() within 2 * second` fails with `Timed[E]` of `std.time` (`TimedOut`, or `Failed(error: x)` for a failure in time), or `Deadline.Expired` when the expression cannot fail; `attempt f(x) within d` holds the time-out in its `Result`, and `if error pass` passes it as the function's own `TimedOut`. Past its deadline the expression is canceled at its next check point, with the tasks it started, and its `Http` waits are cut; an operation in progress, its handler's code included, completes first, and the trace gains a `timeout` event after the operations' own outcomes. Checks: U2028 for a deadline that is not in milliseconds, U5001 for `within 0`, U3007 when the time-out is not handled, U3008 for `if error pass` without a field-less `TimedOut`, and the timing label of ADR 0117 IF2 (U4001, U4002, U4003), with each `within` above `public` in the report's `timing` list. Timers live in a timing wheel, O(1) to arm and cancel.
- **`first of` races branches** (#968, ADR 0117 rules 4, 6, 10, 12 and 13, IF3, `spec/27 §6`), in edition `0.4`. `let quote = first of` with two or more indented branches runs each branch as a task: the first to return gives the value, and the others are canceled, their frames left by the failure path, and a request they were waiting on ends `interrupted` at once. It fails only when every branch fails, with the lowest-indexed failure. The trace lists the winner's events first, then the other branches' in index order, each canceled one ending with `cancelled`, under the paths `p.f<c>.<i>`. The interpreter, and native code under `--virtual-time`, run the branches in index order up to the first that returns, so both engines agree, ties going to the lowest index. Checks: U2035 for a body with fewer than two branches or a line that is not an expression, U2023 for captures, U2001 for branches of different types, and the timing floor of IF3 over every branch (U4003, U4002), with the value labeled by the timing of every branch and each `first of` above `public` in the report's `timing` list. The report's `out_of_model` list gains "which branch of `first of` wins, beyond its label". A branch's depth is bounded by `--task-depth` below its block.
- **`tasks` and `start`** (#969, ADR 0117 rules 7, 10, 11, 12 and 13, `spec/27 §7` and `§8`), in edition `0.4`. `tasks [at most n at once]` runs its block in the current task, and each `start` in it starts a task for an expression and goes on, parking at the bound, by default `max(64, M)` for the memory budget `M` in MiB, at most a quarter of `vm.max_map_count` on Linux. The statement ends once its block and every task it started have ended; a block that fails or stops cancels them first, and leaving it by `stop` or `skip` of an enclosing loop waits for them. A started task that runs past `--task-depth` ends alone, with its `aborted` event and one U6003 line on stderr, while the others run on; a host stop reaches the `for ever` of the block, whose `on stop` runs, and its tasks drain. Paths are `p.s<c>.<k>`. Checks: U2035 for a `start` outside the block of a `tasks`, inside a task within it, in a lambda or with a `with` clause; U3029 for a `start` that can fail; U2011, U2023, and U5001 for a bound below 1. Traces are written as they happen, with `--trace <file>` on `una run` too, the file made before the run starts. The interpreter runs started tasks, and the root of a program with `tasks`, on `una-sys` coroutines under a first-in, first-out schedule that switches after 1024 check points; native code follows the same schedule under `--virtual-time`, on one worker, so both engines agree, and refuses `--virtual-time` for an executable built with check elision. Under the real clock a task's turn ends after 10 ms while another waits. `una-sys` coroutine scopes take bodies that borrow what outlives them. The messages of `async` and `await` name `tasks` and `start` in edition `0.4`, and the report's `out_of_model` list gains "waits at limits and at `start`".
- **Limits on `Http` grants** (#970, ADR 0117 rule 8, IF4, `spec/27 §9`), in edition `0.4`. An `http` grant of `run grants` in `ita.una` takes limit lines, `at most <n> per second`, `minute` or `hour`, `at most <n> at once` and `waiting at most <n>` (1024 without it), and a `--grant` spec takes them as suffixes, `;100/minute;8/once;waiting=1000`; an invalid line is U7008. Both engines enforce them in the HTTP client they share, below every handler and rebinding: a token bucket per grant kept by GCRA on the run's clock, so that under `--virtual-time` a wait for a refill moves the virtual clock and both engines read the same times, a count of requests in flight, and a queue served by ticket. A request is charged to every grant that allows it, and each redirect hop to the grants of its target, releasing its slots before the next hop asks, so crossing redirect chains complete. A request over a limit waits, within `--http-timeout` and the deadlines of `within` and `for ever within`, and fails with the new `HttpError.RateLimited` when the queue is full; a canceled task's wait leaves the queue unsent. `una check` reports U4001 at each `Http` request timed by data above `internal` when `run grants` carry limits; `una build` embeds `L_lim`, and `una run` and executables refuse a limited `--grant` before `main` (exit code 2) when `L_lim` is above `internal` or the program is of an earlier edition.
- **`std.limit`** (#971, ADR 0117 rule 9 and IF5, `spec/27 §10`), in edition `0.4`: rate limits computed in code. The module, read only by a package that imports it, declares the capability `Limit` with `op acquire() -> Unit fails Limited accepts internal`, its failure kind `Limited = | RateLimited`, and the handler `limiter(at_most: Int, per: Int of Millisecond, waiting: Int)`, which the runtime provides: `with @gate = limiter(at_most: 50, per: second, waiting: 200)` installs one bucket of `at_most` tokens that refills evenly over `per`, kept by GCRA over the run's clock as the limits on grants are, shared by every task that uses the binding, the started tasks of a `tasks` statement included. `acquire` takes a token at once or waits in the bucket's queue, served by ticket, within the deadlines of `within` and `for ever within`; it fails with `RateLimited` when `waiting` calls already wait, when a covering deadline passes or a host stop ends the wait, with no token taken; a canceled task's wait ends `interrupted`. Under `--virtual-time` a wait moves the clock and takes no wall time, so both engines agree. Installing it carries the obligations `at_most >= 1`, `per >= 1` and `waiting >= 0` (U5001, U5002); `una check` reports U4001 at the `with` binding, or the `given` line, when the statement it scopes over is timed by data above `internal`, the label `Limited` carries.
- **The reactor** (#972, ADR 0117 rules 3 and 4, `spec/27 §2`). In native code, an `Http` request of a task runs on the task's own worker, and each wait on its connection parks the task on one reactor thread, over `kqueue` on macOS and `epoll` on Linux, which wakes it when the connection is ready, its deadline passes, a host stop reaches it or its task is canceled. A request to a server that never answers no longer holds a helper thread, so thousands of requests wait at once, and an interrupted request's connection is closed as its task resumes. Name resolution and the connection itself still run on the I/O pool (`--io-threads`), with the wait there covered by the request's deadline; a job whose request was canceled or expired opens nothing, and the job hands the lookup and the connection to the bounded lookup and connect pools started before `main` (#1148); a cancellation raises the request's abandon, so the job's wait on those pools ends at once. The sockets of parked requests are capped process-wide at the soft limit of open descriptors less a reserve of a quarter of it (at least 64), with a first-come, first-served wait for a place, so a server that never answers can no longer make `Files` operations fail. A run's own stop at `--stop-after-iterations` now writes the wake pipe, so requests parked in other `for ever` loops see it at once. Outcomes, failures and traces do not change, and the interpreter is unchanged. `una-sys` gains `poll`, the `kqueue` or `epoll` instance behind a safe interface, with one-shot registrations and a close-on-exec descriptor, `signals::wake_pipe` and `io::descriptor_limit`; it stays under its 1,200-line cap, at 1,199 lines.
- **`una run --cost-clock`** (#973, ADR 0117 step 7, `spec/27 §8` rule 6). With `--virtual-time`, each check point of the interpreter, each call, operation and loop iteration, moves the virtual clock by one millisecond, so deadlines, refills and `Clock.now` follow the run's work: the clock of the security hunt's timing oracle, where a missing timing label shows as a difference of values. Without `--virtual-time` it is a usage error.
- **Task stacks sized from a frame bound** (#1152, ADR 0117 amendment of 2026-11-09, accepted; `spec/27 §4` rule 1). `una build` records the largest frame Cranelift laid out for any compiled function, plus linkage and alignment, as the program's frame bound, and an executable sizes each task stack as `16 MiB + depth × bound`, never more than before. At the default `--max-depth` of 10,000 and a typical bound of 128 bytes, the 64 stacks pooled before `main` reserve about 1.1 GiB of address space instead of about 21 GiB. Both inputs are public, so every pooled stack still has one size. A program whose frames the compiler does not state, or whose largest frame is over 64 KiB, keeps the old size, and the interpreter keeps its own.
- **Limited grants beside tasks** (#1154, ADR 0116 amendment of 2026-10-25, C, slice 7 of the amendment, ADR 0117 IF4, `spec/27 §9` rule 5). `L_lim` takes the precise `τ_conc` of `spec/27 §11` at each request in a function's body, instead of `⊤` beside any task construct, so a server whose requests run in started tasks beside work on `public` and `internal` data can carry limits on its grants; a request beside work on a `secret` stays refused.
- **Limits count only the code between two requests** (#1155, ADR 0116 P11). `L_lim` is now the meet of P10's label over the ancestors' whole bodies and P11's over the code that can run between two requests of one task: `Gap` of every body over a control-flow graph of its Core IR (failure edges, the cuts of `within`, loops with their back edges, `start` and the task constructs as calls, prelude functions that apply a lambda), the driver's gaps (`τ⁰(step)`, `Suf(init) ⊔ Pre(step)`, `Pre(on_stop)`), `τ_conc` at each request and `L_res`. Work on data above `internal` before a run's first request, a handler's parameter its operations never read, and calls of lambdas whose code is known no longer refuse a limited grant (`limits_between_before_first`, `limits_between_timed_before`, `limits_lambda_handler`); work between two requests still does, and U4001 names the body whose code between requests raised it. The labels are computed in linear time; Core IR is built for them only when P10's label is above `internal`.
- **One lookup per name on the `Http` lookup pool, and bounded pool lines** (#1265, `spec/24 §4` rule 2, ADR 0117 slice S7 review R7-10): requests of one name that wait at the same time share one lookup thread, names compared without case, each until its own deadline, and a lookup whose requests all gave up never answers a later request; lines are pruned of given-up steps once they double, so a pool holds O(peak waiting requests + threads) steps and names at an amortized O(1) per step; a step that panics fails its request at once with `Unreachable`; the per-zone bound is left to #1717.
- **A `within`'s cut of a `tasks` statement ends a request of its tasks with `TimedOut` on every path** (#1266, ADR 0116 amendment P9, ADR 0117 rules 3 and 4, `spec/27 §2` rule 4, §5 rule 6 and §7 rule 4). When the deadline of a `within` around a `tasks` statement cut its block, a started task whose request was parked on the reactor was woken as a cancellation and its request ended `interrupted`, while the same request on a helper of the I/O pool ended with its own `TimedOut`, and one still queued for a helper ended `interrupted` too. The outcome at that deadline, which is timing information, so depended on which path ran the request and, on the reactor, on whether the task or the statement saw the deadline first, a scheduling race. The cut now lowers the statement's count to a value of its own, which wakes no wait parked on the reactor, as it wakes no job running on a helper: the wait ends at its own time-out, the same deadline, and a request still queued for a helper is woken at once and fails with `TimedOut`, never sent; an `acquire` of `std.limit` queued the same way fails with `RateLimited`. Every other cancellation, a failure of the block, a race that a task lost or a cancellation from outside, still ends a waiting request `interrupted` at once. The interpreter's outcome, `TimedOut`, is unchanged. These constructs are part of the edition `0.4` preview.
- **Fix: a wait that a `within` cuts never ends before the `within`'s deadline** (#1355, `spec/27 §5` rule 6, §9 rule 4, §10 rule 3). In native code a request waiting at a limit of its grant, or an `acquire` of `std.limit`, read its covering deadline in whole milliseconds as the run's clock reading plus the time left, each rounded down, so the wait could end with its time-out failure up to 2 ms before the deadline in nanoseconds; the `within` then found its deadline not yet passed and let the failure stand, so the native trace lacked the `timeout` event the interpreter's had. The deadline a wait at a limit reads is now the first whole millisecond at or after it, a request's own time-out on the run's clock is rounded the same way, and in both engines an operation that a covering deadline cut returns only once the run's clock has reached that deadline (on its helper natively, never on a worker); the scheduler tests of turn ends (#1268) now check the end the scheduler sets instead of a turn length measured on the wall clock, so load no longer fails them.
- **Started tasks that install handlers compare task by task in both engines** (#1542, `spec/27 §8` rule 4, `spec/16 §5`). Installations are numbered run-wide in the order they happen, so under the real clock two started tasks that both install handlers may interleave their numbers natively, where the interpreter gives each task its numbers in turn; the comparison of the two engines under the real clock now sets aside one renumbering of the run's installations, and the spec says so: a one-to-one renaming that keeps each handler and, within each task, the order of its numbers. Every program of the differential whose trace has events of started tasks is also run in both engines under `--virtual-time`, whose output, exit status and whole trace must be equal, so a counter that skips numbers or counts quiet installations still fails. A new conformance case, `started_tasks_install_handlers`, and its native test lock both.
- **A TLS response cut by a bare close fails** (#1822, `spec/24 §3` rule 3). Over `https`, a response whose body ends at the end of the connection (no `Content-Length`, not `chunked`) is now `Protocol` unless the server sent TLS's `close_notify` before closing; before, an on-path attacker could truncate such a body with a TCP FIN and the program received it as complete. A response delimited by `Content-Length` or by its last chunk still succeeds when the server then closes without `close_notify`. Plain `http` is unchanged. Both engines.
- **Native `within` under `--virtual-time` is cut at the check point after a wait at a limit** (#1823, `spec/27 §5` rule 5, §8 rule 3). A wait for a refill at a `std.limit` limiter, or at the limits of an `Http` grant, moves the virtual clock, up to the covering deadline. The interpreter then cut `e` at its next check point, while an executable noticed the passed deadline only when `e` returned, so it still performed the effects in between and ran the tasks of a block after the wait. Every check point of an executable under `--virtual-time` now compares the clock with the earliest deadline that covers its task, own or inherited, so both engines give the same output and trace. Runs on the real clock are unchanged.
- **A request canceled while it connects keeps its socket place until its socket is closed** (#1824, `spec/27 §2` rule 3). In an executable, a request parked on the reactor whose task was canceled while its connection was still being made, as the loser of a `first of` racing a host that drops SYNs is, gave its place among the sockets back at once, while the connect pool's thread kept the socket open until the request's deadline. Such requests could open more sockets than the cap of three quarters of the descriptor limit, so a `Files` operation of another task could fail with `EMFILE`. The connect pool's job now keeps the place until it has closed its socket. Requests that connect, and the interpreter, are unchanged.

### Serving HTTP

- **Strict framing for HTTP servers** (#1003, ADR 0123 step 1, `spec/31 §3` and §4). `una-http` gains `strict`, the server's own framing beside the client's reader, which does not change: CRLF line ends only; header lines with no folding, no whitespace before the colon, no control characters and no repair of a value that is not UTF-8; a `Connection` list of its own headers only; one `Content-Length` of 1 to 19 digits; `Transfer-Encoding: chunked` alone and never with `Content-Length`; chunk sizes of 1 to 16 hex digits with no extension; trailers within what the header section left of its limits; origin-form targets of RFC 3986 characters, decoded segment by segment with `.`, `..`, encoded `/`, `\` and control characters refused. No construct is added yet.
- **The HTTP server's request reader and reply writer** (#1004, ADR 0123 step 2, `spec/31 §3` to §5). `una-http` gains `request`, which reads one request fed in pieces, with no I/O, on the strict framing of #1003: limits checked before bytes are kept (`414`, `431`, `413`), exactly one `Host` of strict syntax, absolute-form targets rewritten only for their own host and scheme, `415` for a content coding, `417` for an unknown expectation, `100-continue` reported, the connection's headers removed, and the next pipelined request left unread. It also gains `reply`, which checks a reply (`BadReply` for a reserved or injected header, a body on `204` or `304`, too many headers) before writing its bytes with `Date`, `Content-Length` and a default `text/plain; charset=utf-8`. No construct is added yet.
- **`serve:` grants** (#1005, ADR 0123 step 3, `spec/31 §2`), edition 0.4 preview. A grant `serve:<scheme>://<address>:<port>`, with `;<item>` suffixes in `--grant` and item lines under a `serve` line in `ita.una`, names where a run may accept connections, with every limit of ADR 0123 rule 7 at its default unless the grant sets it: connections in all and per peer, handshakes, requests per connection, sizes of the request line, headers and body, byte budgets for bodies, replies and delivered requests, and the `idle`, `read`, `write` and `answer` times. Addresses are IP literals in canonical form, never names; overlapping grants are refused; `hosts` lists the names `Host` may carry, and without it only `localhost` and IP literals are accepted. Before `main`, the run raises its descriptor limit, opens the TLS certificate and key once each and checks the open descriptors (a regular file of at most 1 MiB; the key owned by the run's user or root, with no bit for others and no group write bit), and binds one listening socket per grant, with `IPV6_V6ONLY` and without `SO_REUSEPORT`. Errors are U7008 in `ita.una`, usage errors (exit code 2) for `--grant` and for the files and binding, and U7002 for a `serve:` grant in `una.toml`, in every edition. The TLS server configuration is pinned (TLS 1.3 only unless `tls12`, which requires the extended master secret) but not built, and the key is not yet checked against the certificate: both wait for the decision on the TLS provider (#1059). Nothing accepts on the listeners until the serve threads land.
- **The serve threads** (#1006, ADR 0123 step 4, `spec/31 §6`), edition 0.4 preview. The host code for `serve:` grants now accepts, reads, limits, queues and writes: one listening socket per grant, never shared through `SO_REUSEPORT`, registered in one accept poller that one serve thread watches at a time (the turn passes round by a message), so exactly one thread accepts at a time and a listener event wakes one thread, at most 16 connections per event, with a slot of `connections` taken before each `accept`; each connection goes to the serve thread named by a keyed SipHash of its addresses, under a key of the run that nothing shows. A connection reads its first request within `read` of the accept and later ones within `idle` and `read`, waits at most `answer` for its reply, and writes within `write`; `408`, `421` for a `Host` the grant does not name, the host's own `503`, `500`, and `100 Continue` after a body is admitted come from the host, and every refusal drains the peer for at most 64 KiB or one second and closes. Shared counters on their own cache lines hold `connections`, `waiting`, `/once`, a rate kept as one timestamp, and the reserves of request bodies (`buffers`, `peer_buffers`), delivered requests (`delivered`) and replies (`replies`, `peer_replies`), with a table of peers (an IPv4 address or an IPv6 /64) in 64 shards. Replies over `replies` close the slowest connection of the peer furthest over its share, found in O(log c) from heaps built only under pressure. A host stop closes the listeners and idle connections, refuses what is not delivered and drains the rest; the hard stop answers each open exchange once without waiting. `--serve-threads <n>`, from 1 to 256 (by default the number of cores), is taken by `una run` and by executables. The descriptor limit a serving run raises is the sum of its connections, listeners, poller and wake sockets, 256 for `Http`, the `Files` helpers and 64 for the runtime, and the cap on the sockets of `Http` requests leaves the rest to the server. The timing wheel moves from `una-rt` to `una-http`, unchanged. An `https` grant is not served until the TLS provider is decided (#1059). The serve threads are tested by scripted connections (`tests/serve`), loopback sockets and a consumer written in Rust; programs reach them through `HttpServer` (#1571), and `una run` and `una build` serve with them (#1008, #1782).
- **The `HttpServer` capability, checked** (#1007, ADR 0123 step 5, `spec/31 §1`), edition 0.4 preview. `std.host` declares `HttpServer` with `next`, `reply` and `close`, the kinds `Method`, `Param`, `Request`, `Exchange`, `Status`, `Reply` and `ServeError`, and the opaque type `ReplyRight`, which only an import brings into scope; in earlier editions an import of them is U1001. The decoded query of a request is the field `params`, since `query` is a reserved word. A `Reply`'s status is refined to 200 to 599 (U5001); its fields are `internal`, so a `secret` body is U4001; `reply` accepts `internal`, `next` accepts `internal` and `close` accepts `public` (U4003), and the failure of `reply` is `internal` (U4002 for `if error pass`). New U3033: an operation of `HttpServer` in a branch of `together`, the body of a `together for` or a branch of `first of`, performed there or reachable through a call, a function value, a handler installed or an operation whose handler can perform one. A `start` whose captures can hold a reply right is checked as the host's answer for its task (IF6, U4003). In a package that performs an operation of `HttpServer`, the label `L_srv` of the serve threads joins every timing site, at least `internal`, with the preview's terms. The operations run in both engines (#1571), and `una run` and `una build` serve a program whose entry uses `HttpServer` (#1008, #1782); `una check` checks such a program and `una test` runs tests with a `given` handler of `HttpServer`.
- **`una run` serves** (#1008, #1009, ADR 0123, `spec/31 §1` rule 10 and §7 rule 5), edition 0.4 preview. `una run` of a program whose `main`, or a driven module's `init`, `step` or `on_stop`, uses `HttpServer`, and of a package with a `serve` section, now runs it over live listeners in the interpreter, with no test flag: the serve threads start before `main`, and the run writes one line on stderr for each address it serves, `serving on 127.0.0.1:8080 (granted by the package)` or `` (granted by a `--grant` flag) ``, before its first request. A run that takes host stops, one with a `serve` section or a `main` that says `diverges`, drains at SIGINT or SIGTERM and ends with exit code 0; a server whose `main` does not say `diverges` keeps the default action of SIGTERM and ends at once, exit code 143, with no drain. What stays closed: a `within` in the `serve` section is still U4003; an `https` grant is still refused before `main`, so no plain bytes are served on an `https` address; `--virtual-time` with a `serve:` grant and no `--serve-script` is still a usage error; and the scripted connections of `--serve-script` still run only in the toolchain's own tests.
- **The `serve` section of `ita.una`, checked** (#1008, ADR 0123 step 6, `spec/31 §7`), edition 0.4 preview. `serve` names the module whose `pub handle(request: internal Request) -> internal Reply` the host calls for each request, with an optional `pub on_stop() -> Unit`, and an optional `within` in milliseconds from 1 to 86,400,000. A missing `module`, a value out of range, an unknown line, a module that is not in the package, and a `serve` section beside a `run entry` or a `drive` section are U7008. A `requires` or a refined parameter or result type on `handle` or `on_stop`, a precondition no call of the host would establish, is U7008, and a section with any problem inside it is not read. The `uses` of `handle` and `on_stop` are checked against the grants, the final grants of `una run` included (U3012); `on_stop` cannot declare `fails` (U3029); neither may say `diverges` (U3027) or use `HttpServer` (U3033: in this form the host owns every exchange). The driver is a hand-off site of `L_srv`, whose preview label is then the top label, so a `within` in the section is U4003 at its value until `L_srv` is computed more precisely. `una report` lists `HttpServer` and each `serve:` grant with its scheme, limits and `Host` check under `tcb.serve_grants`, the server code under `tcb.server`, three server entries at the end of `out_of_model`, and a new `warnings` list (plain `http` off loopback, `hosts=*`, `peer=none`, `peer_buffers=none`, `tls12`), within `una.report/2`. `una run` and `una build` of a package with a `serve` section report the module's shape, then (for `una run`) a missing `serve:` grant, each a usage error before any listener is bound, and otherwise run or build its driver (#1587, #1008, #1782); `una check`, `una test` and `una report` work, and tests call `handle` directly.
- **`examples/health`, a server example** (#1009, ADR 0123 "A health endpoint", `spec/31 §7`), edition 0.4 preview. The package's `ita.una` grants `serve "http://127.0.0.1:8080"` and names the module `health` in a `serve` section; `handle` answers `GET /health` with `200 ok` and every other request with `404`, and its tests call it with requests from `test_request`. It checks at tier 1 with every obligation proven and passes its tests; `una run` serves it, and `una build` makes an executable that serves it (`spec/31 §1` rule 10, #1008, #1782). The example tests now read `ita.una` packages.
- **The script of connections of `--serve-script`, read and checked** (#1009, ADR 0123 rule 21, `spec/31 §8` rule 6): one JSON object per LF-ended line in four forms, opening, bytes in padded base64, paced reads and closing, each key once and nothing else on the line, checked in full with times that never decrease and each refusal named by its line, in O(n) of the script, for the flags that take it next.
- **The scripted engine behind `--serve-script` and `--serve-out` in the toolchain's test builds** (#1009, ADR 0123 rule 21, `spec/31 §8` rule 6): with `UNA_TEST_RUN_SERVER=1`, `una run` and executables of test builds serve a checked script in place of listeners and write what each connection received, the same bytes, output and trace in both engines; every other run with `--serve-script` is a usage error.
- **Pipelined requests and each refusal of a request run alike in both engines** (#1009, ADR 0123 rules 8, 10 to 12 and 21, `spec/31 §3`, `§4`, `§6` rule 8): scripted runs of pipelined requests answered in order, of 86 refused requests in six cases by class (request line, header lines, `Host` and target, framing with a request behind each refusal, the 64 KiB drain), each with its status and closed connection, and of the requests exactly at each limit, accepted, give the same bytes, output and trace in the interpreter and in native code.
- **Limits and timers of a `serve:` grant run alike in both engines** (#1009, ADR 0123 rules 12, 13, 17 and 21, `spec/31 §2`, `§6`): scripted runs of `waiting` full and `/once` holding delivery, a rate delaying it, bodies at and one byte over what remains of `buffers` and of `peer_buffers`, IPv6 peers counted per /64 up to its last bit, and the `idle`, `read` and `write` timers, a silent first connection included, give the same bytes, output and trace in the interpreter and in native code.
- **Pressure and a program that holds most of its memory, alike in both engines** (#1009, ADR 0123 rule 13 and Conformance, `spec/31 §6` rule 6): under 64 paced connections that never read 1 MiB replies, 64 that never read 3 MiB replies and 64 that trickle 1 MiB bodies, a fresh request is answered and the connections furthest over their share are closed as replies are reserved; a program whose state takes more than 80% of `--max-memory` still delivers and answers; both give the same bytes, output and trace in the interpreter and in native code.
- **`--serve-script` and `--serve-out` are read and checked in both engines** (#1009, ADR 0123 rule 21, `spec/31 §8` rule 6): `una run` and executables take a script of connections, one JSON object per line, which needs `--virtual-time` and, after the key files, is checked in full as a regular file of at most 64 MiB, its errors named by line and the same in both engines; `--serve-out` needs `--serve-script`; outside the toolchain's own test builds the scripted connections do not run, a usage error that makes no file.
- **Host and hard stops of a scripted run alike in both engines** (#1009, ADR 0123 rule 18, `spec/31 §6` rule 9): a host stop with a request in flight, one queued, one being read and idle connections, and a hard stop after `--stop-grace` that ends with U6007 and answers the held exchange `503`, give the same bytes, output and trace in the interpreter and in native code.
- **`test_request` and `test_exchange` in `std.testing`** (#1009, ADR 0123 rule 21, `spec/31 §8`, `spec/14 §4`): in edition 0.4, a test builds the `Request` the host would read for a method, a target and a body, read by the host's own reader with `host: test`, peer `192.0.2.1` and listener `test`, failing `Refused` of `TestRequestError` where the host would answer `400`, and an `Exchange` of it with a fresh reply right, for tests that call a server's handlers directly.
- **The security hunt's server run: replies with injected headers** (#1011, ADR 0123 S9). Family N5 echoes a request's decoded query value into a header of the reply, CR, LF, NUL and other control bytes included, and writes header names the host writes itself or that are not tokens, and bodies on `204` and `304`: the reply must fail with `BadReply`, send nothing and keep its reply right, and no byte of the injection may reach the peer. The release gate's fixed run is unchanged.
- **The security hunt's server run** (#1011, ADR 0123 S9). `python3 -B -m hunt servers` runs the hunt's new family N with a test build of `una`: servers fed by scripts of connections, so no run binds a listener, that receive every refusal of `spec/31 §3` and §4 (request line, line ends, header lines, framing and smuggling, `Host`, the target and path confusion) and its well-formed twin, each followed by a request a smuggling peer wants read, whole and split at random byte boundaries. A peer must receive the host's own answer alone, and the request behind it must never be read. The release gate's fixed run is unchanged.
- **The two-run oracle over servers** (#1011, ADR 0123 S9). A mode of the security hunt's oracle runs servers over their scripts of connections and compares, at `internal`, every byte their peers receive, since `reply` accepts `internal`. Family N6's request tasks read a secret balance and keep it from the reply, release it through a policy, or miss its label (closed calls a correct checker rejects); `hunt servers` runs them through must-reject and that oracle, with a canary that must leak on the wire alone. The release gate's fixed run is unchanged.
- **The re-check's refusal of the server is kept in step with the checker** (#1012, `spec/31 §9`). A test of `una-check` fails when the formal importer's lists of the server's names differ from the checker's (`SERVER_CORE_KINDS`, `STD_TESTING_SERVER_NAMES`) or when a name of the server is neither refused nor exempt, so a kind ADR 0123 adds is never read as a record of the program. The importer also refuses `std.testing`'s `TestRequestError`, `test_request` and `test_exchange` by their module: a declaration that held one was already not re-checked, as a form the importer did not read, and the reason now names ADR 0123.
- **The formal re-check refuses ADR 0123's server by name** (#1012, ADR 0123 S10, `spec/31 §9`). F1 does not model `next` as an input stream, `reply` as an effect or the reply rights of `HttpServer` yet, so the re-check reports a declaration whose Core holds a `ReplyRight`, a kind of the server (`Method`, `Param`, `Request`, `Exchange`, `Reply`, `ServeError`), or an operation, a grant or a handler of `HttpServer`, and what reaches it, as not re-checked, never accepted, with the reason "F1 restriction (..., ADR 0123)". The importer read `Request`, `Reply` and `Param` as records of the program before, so a declaration that built a `Reply` or read a `Param`, and what called it, could be accepted; it is now not re-checked. Every other such declaration was already not re-checked, as a form the importer did not read; the reason now names ADR 0123. `formal/UnaFormal/F1/ServerImportTests.lean` pins the verdicts on `una`'s own export, a program's own kind named `Request` read as a record included.
- **Serve thread faults** (#1376, after #1375, ADR 0123 step 4, `spec/31 §6` rule 13), edition 0.4 preview. A panic in a connection's step, in the hand-off of an accepted connection, in a peer's expiry, or in a callback registered on the serve threads, is caught and counted: it closes that connection at most (at accept, its slot and its peer's place come back as far as recorded) and never ends a serve thread. A panic inside a thread's timing wheel or readiness registrations, which every connection of the thread shares, is not contained and ends the thread, also when it happens in the close that follows a contained fault. A serve thread that ends closes every connection it owned and gives back their `connections` slots, peers' places, requests, exchanges and replies, as far as their state records them, hands the peers' expiries it was timing to a live thread (so `https` peer entries still expire), hands on the turn to accept and registers again every listener whose event its last wait returned; a message sent to it later is handled by its sender, and a new connection whose keyed owner ended goes to the next live serve thread, or is refused at accept when none is left. A connection whose answer timer cannot be armed no longer leaves a dead entry in a delivery queue. A second registration of `on_ready` or `on_slot_freed` is now the defined error `AlreadyRegistered` in every build instead of stopping a debug build. The turn to accept is recorded before the message that passes it, so the record is never older than the last pass. Contained faults are counted but not yet shown to the program, and each still prints through the panic hook. No program can name `HttpServer` yet (#1007).
- **Serve engine fault policy** (#1391, ADR 0123 amendment of 2026-11-02 decision 10, `spec/31 §6` rule 13), edition 0.4 preview. A panic of the host is still contained to the connection it happens on, and the run now stops with the hard stop when a serve callback (`on_ready`, `on_slot_freed`) panics, when a panic happens with a lock of a grant's shared state held (a delivery queue, an exchange's request, a connection's pending reply, the pressure heaps, a registry shard, a peer table shard), or when the run's contained faults pass 64. After a stop nothing is accepted, a reply is discarded, and the next delivery reports the failure, whose message names the cause. Contained faults no longer print through the panic hook; the panics that stop the run or end a serve thread still do, and a callback that panicked is not called again. Three windows that left a slot or a peer's place held are closed: a fault in the peer's admission after its place was taken, a fault in the hand-off before the connection reaches its owner's inbox (or its owner's table), and a panic inside the closing of a connection, which is now resumed past the faulting step. Faults are counted per grant and for the run; `faults()` and the trace event come with S5 (#1571). No program can name `HttpServer` yet (#1007), so no released line is affected.
- **The operations of `HttpServer` in the interpreter, over the serve engine** (#1571, ADR 0123 step 5, `spec/31 §1`), edition 0.4 preview. `next` checks an abort of the run, `close` and the host stop first, in that order, then takes one request from the engine in one atomic step, lets the other ready tasks run while none can be delivered, and waits for the engine only when no task is ready; `reply` checks the holder of the reply right (`NotHeld`) and the reply (`BadReply`, the right kept), and returns `unit` without sending when another transition ended the exchange; `close` closes the listeners once. Reply rights are words of a table shared by both engines (`una_http::serve::rights`), held by one task at a time: `next` gives the right to its caller, `start` moves the rights its captures reach, closures included, which Core IR now names on the `start` node (`rights`, from the checker's capture mark of IF6), and a task's end answers what it still holds, `500` or `503` with `Retry-After: 1`, each with its `unanswered` event. The arguments of the operations of `HttpServer` are written in traces in the reduced form of ADR 0123 rule 1 (the entry on the reduced trace form), and native code moves reply rights at a `start` in the same order (the entry on native code). The runs of `tests/serve_runs/` drive a server through the real engine from a script of connections. `una run` and `una build` serve such a program with no test flag (#1008, #1782, `spec/31 §1` rule 10).
- **The operations of `HttpServer` in native code** (#1571, ADR 0123 step 5, `spec/31 §1`), edition 0.4 preview. `next`, `reply` and `close` run in `una-rt` over the same serve engine and the same table of reply rights as the interpreter, with its checks in its order: an abort of the run, then `close`, then the host stop, then one atomic delivery; a `next` with nothing to deliver lets a ready task that can make progress run first, and otherwise waits for the engine, on the worker under virtual time and on a helper of the I/O pool while the task parks under the real clock. A `ReplyRight` is now a value of native code: `==` compares the exchange it names, and interpolation writes `<reply-right>`. `start` moves the reply rights its captures reach before the task is queued, in the interpreter's order. A task's end answers the rights it still holds, `500` or `503` with `Retry-After: 1`, each with its `unanswered` event. In a run that serves, an abort of the run by a started task lets every task stop at its next check point, and an abort of the root unwinds it through its `tasks` statements, which cancel their tasks, so the host answers each task's rights and writes its events, as in the interpreter; a `next` that resumes after an abort ends `aborted` even when a cancellation came with it, in both engines; a hard stop and an internal error still end the process at once, after the host answers every right left with `503`. The runs of `tests/serve_runs/` run natively too, through a scripted engine that only the toolchain's own test builds of the runtime compile, and must give the interpreter's bytes, output and trace.
- **The reduced trace form of `HttpServer`** (#1571, ADR 0123 rule 1, `spec/31 §1` rule 7), edition 0.4 preview. The events of `next`, `reply` and `close` of the host's `HttpServer` are redacted by their labels, as every event's are, and beneath that redaction are written in the reduced form whatever their labels, in both engines through one implementation: the values of the headers `authorization`, `proxy-authorization`, `cookie`, `set-cookie`, `x-api-key` and `x-auth-token`, in any letter case, as `<credential>`; bodies, and the values of query parameters in `params` and in `target`, as `{"length": n, "hmac": "<hex>"}`, an HMAC-SHA-256 under a key of the run that is all zeros under a script of connections; a reply right as `<reply-right>`. A `reply` whose `Reply` is built from `public` data is now written `Reply(status: <redacted:internal>, headers: <redacted:internal>, body: <redacted:internal>)` instead of `<redacted:internal>`, since every field of `Reply` is `internal`. The runs of `tests/serve_runs/` compare the reduced form of both engines byte for byte, with the redaction of labels lifted by the scripted engine of the toolchain's own tests only, and check that no credential or body reaches a trace. The test build of the runtime with its serve hook is now built in a target directory of its own, so the plain `libuna_rt.a` that other tests link is never replaced.
- **The serve threads of a program that serves, over live sockets, in both engines** (#1571, ADR 0123 S4 amendment B.2 and rule 18, `spec/31 §6` rule 14), edition 0.4 preview. A run whose entry functions use `HttpServer` and that holds a `serve:` grant starts its serve threads, their pollers and wake pairs and the accept poller before `main`, over the listeners it bound, draws the key of its reduced trace form from the host's random source, and accepts nothing until its first server operation; `next` waits for the engine on the real clock and fails with `TimedOut` at its deadline. The run's watcher of host stops hands the host stop and the hard stop to the engine, and the run's end drains the engine as a host stop does, for at most the grace period, then makes the hard stop. At the hard stop (U6007), in both engines, the engine's hard stop comes first: from then on it discards the program's answers and replies that have not yet taken their exchange, its serve threads answer every exchange still open `503` with `Retry-After: 1` in one non-blocking write, and the host writes no `unanswered` event, in the interpreter as in native code. A reply already in progress when the hard stop comes may be lost, with neither it nor the `503` reaching the peer. At an internal error (U8001) native code does the same, while the interpreter's tasks answer their rights `503` themselves with their `unanswered` events, as at an abort of the run. A run under `--virtual-time` that holds a `serve:` grant is a usage error before `main`, in `una run` and in executables. In native code the serve threads' start, stop and join and the engine's panic hook are reached only through a call that the code of a program with an operation of `HttpServer` makes before the run starts, so an executable that serves nothing links none of them (it still links the table of reply rights and the answer of an exchange, which the run's end calls when it serves); in a program that serves, the panic hook is installed over the runtime's, from one place, before any thread of the run starts. New tests run servers over loopback in both engines on the real clock, and two timing oracles, `serve_prefix_secret` and `serve_prefix_early_peers`, check that set-up code before the first server operation, and peers that connect during it, leave no trace in the time of a later reply.
- **One waiting `next` woken per request, and the serve threads in place before the first job** (#1571, ADR 0123, `spec/31 §6` rule 14), edition 0.4 preview: a queued request, an ended exchange, a closed connection and a delivery each wake one `next` waiting on the live engine instead of every one, the wake of a delivery lets through the further requests that one ended exchange can free room for in the `delivered` budget, a stop still wakes all, and a `next` that leaves at a cancellation hands its wake on, so a request costs O(1) wakes whatever the number of waiters (scaling test with 4 and 256 waiters); the serve threads report in place before the run starts, and a thread that cannot register its wake pair or the accept poller stops the start with a usage error (S4 design SC6).
- **The driver of the `serve` section in the interpreter** (#1587, ADR 0123 rule 16, `spec/31 §7` rules 4 and 5), edition 0.4 preview, run by `una run` with no test flag, as a program of the language form is (#1008, `spec/31 §1` rule 10). The driver is engine code equivalent to the program of rule 4, not Una source or Core IR: the serve threads start before it, it takes each request with `next` and starts a task per request that calls `handle`, answers `500` with an empty body when `handle` fails, never its payload, and ignores a failure of `reply`, so a `BadReply` ends in the task's `500`; at a host stop the requests in flight drain and `on_stop` runs on the main task, exit code 0, and at the hard stop the rights held are answered `503`, `on_stop` does not run and the run ends with U6007. Each request costs one `next`, one `start`, one move of one reply right and one `reply`, O(1) beside `handle` and its bytes. Its bytes and its trace are those of the program of rule 4 written out, byte for byte. Native code's driver is the next entry, and `una build` of the section came with #1782; a `within` of the section is still U4003.
- **Live runs and the scaling test of the `serve` section's driver in both engines** (#1587, ADR 0123 rules 16 and 18, `spec/31 §7` rule 4): over live sockets, a host stop drains the request in flight before `on_stop` runs, exit code 0, and the hard stop answers `503`, skips `on_stop` and ends with U6007, in the interpreter as in native code; a scripted run of 10,000 requests takes time linear in the requests in both engines, O(1) per request beside `handle`.
- **The driver of the `serve` section in native code** (#1587, ADR 0123 rule 16, `spec/31 §7` rules 4 and 5), edition 0.4 preview, built by `una build` with no test flag, as a program of the language form is (#1782, `spec/31 §1` rule 10); a `within` of the section is still refused. The executable's description carries the section as `"serve": {"module": ..., "within": ...}`, and the flag that lets a run take `serve:` grants is renamed `serve_grants` in it. The executable runs the driver as the interpreter does, engine code equivalent to the program of rule 4: for a reply, a failing `handle` (`500`, its payload never sent) and a reply that `reply` refuses, its bytes and its trace are the interpreter's, byte for byte, and without a `serve:` grant it stops before anything starts with the usage error `una run` gives (rule 1). Each request costs one `next`, one closure of one captured value, one `start`, one move of one reply right and one `reply`, O(1) beside `handle` and its bytes.
- **The serve timing oracles in both engines** (#1660, ADR 0123 S4 design §11.5, `spec/31 §6`). Each of the five release timing oracles of the serve threads, `serve_prefix_secret`, `serve_prefix_early_peers`, `serve_handoff_residue`, `serve_delivery_history` and `serve_owner_spread`, now runs once over the executable that `una build` makes (`<oracle>_native`) and once under `una run` (`<oracle>_interpreter`), with the same harness, assertions and sample sizes, since the serve engine's waiting and waking is separate code in each engine.
- **Oracle for the placement of connections** (#1660, ADR 0123 S4 design §11.5, SC1). The release oracle `serve_owner_spread` observes directly, in the toolchain's test builds only, which serve thread placement chose for each of 8 connections over 8 serve threads whose source ports are all multiples of 8, in both engines and for both keys of its program. Under the keyed placement no run may put all 8 on one owner, and over the 160 connections of 20 runs every serve thread must own between 3 and 44, bounds fixed in advance from the binomial distribution; as a positive control, the unkeyed port-mod-8 placement must put all 8 on one owner in every run. Its timings are logged as diagnostics and are not asserted. It shows that the keyed placement spreads connections that the unkeyed port-mod-8 placement concentrates; it does not show that a peer cannot predict an owner, which is assumed from the secrecy of the run's key and SipHash-2-4, and it does not cover a fixed key or other unkeyed placements.
- **Positive controls for the serve threads' timing channels** (#1660, ADR 0123 S4 design §11.5). The release timing oracles `serve_handoff_residue` and `serve_delivery_history` show on the machine that pending reply bytes and the time between deliveries under a rate change whether a later `next` under a deadline receives its request, the channels that `L_srv` labels; their `secret` versions are conformance cases refused with U4003.
- **Serve engine: an undeliverable request no longer blocks its queue, and `Host` refuses a last label of `0x`** (#1675, ADR 0123 rules 13 and 14, `spec/31 §3` rule 5 and `§6` rule 5). A request whose bytes exceed the whole of the grant's `delivered` is answered `503` with `Retry-After: 1` at once, when its head is read for a declared `Content-Length` and when it is read in full for a chunked body, instead of holding the front of its serve thread's queue until `answer`, so the requests behind it are delivered; a last label of exactly `0x` or `0X` is refused in `Host` (`400`) and in a grant's `hosts`, as one that starts with `0x` already was.
- **Native `next` waits on the reactor, not on a helper of the I/O pool** (#1723, `spec/27 §2` rule 3, `spec/31 §1`). A task of native code waiting in `next` held a helper of the `--io-threads` pool for its whole wait, which `Http`'s lookups and connections need too: with more waiters than helpers, or with every helper taken by `Http` calls to a slow backend, requests got `503` or delivery stopped for every request until a connect timed out. The wait now parks the task on the reactor with its wake left with the serve engine, which wakes one waiter per request and every waiter at a stop; an idle waiter holds no thread and is woken by no timer, only by a request, a stop, its deadline, its cancellation or an abort of the run; such a wait arms no timer on the reactor, and the macOS poller cuts a timeout to the `INT32_MAX` seconds `kevent` takes, so a far timer never fails its waits.
- **The `serve` section's tasks are bounded by the grants' `/once`** (#1724, `spec/31 §7` rule 4, decision S6-D6). The driver's `tasks` statement had only the default bound, so a peer that left, or an `answer` that passed, gave back its exchange's `/once` place while `handle` kept running: with `at most 2 at once`, ten requests closed early left ten `handle` tasks running at once. The statement is now bounded by the sum of `/once` over the run's `serve:` grants, within the default bound of a `tasks` statement, in both engines, so a place is given back when `handle`'s task ends; `spec/31 §7` rule 4 says so.
- **A slow backend no longer stalls a server under `una run`** (#1730, ADR 0117 amendment of 2026-11-09, `spec/27 §8` rule 2). In a run that serves live peers and has a `tasks` statement, the interpreter makes an `Http` request on a helper thread while its task parks, as native code does, so one request that waits on a slow or unreachable backend no longer holds the delivery of every other request until `--http-timeout`. The helpers are capped as native code caps its requests' sockets (`spec/27 §2` rule 3, usually 256 places in a run that serves): a request that finds no place free runs on the interpreter's thread, as before. A request in flight runs to its own deadline even when its task is canceled or the run aborts or stops, so the run can wait up to `--http-timeout` (30 s by default) for one at its end, and after a host stop end with 143 at twice `--stop-grace`. These requests share the run's limits, so a throttled grant can now fail one with `RateLimited` under `una run` too. Runs without a `serve:` grant, scripted runs and runs without `tasks` keep their requests on the interpreter's thread and their schedule.
- **`--serve-out` and a failed `--trace` end a scripted run alike in both engines** (#1734, ADR 0123 rule 21, `spec/31 §8` rule 6): when the trace cannot be written, `una run` now reports the run's own diagnostics, the warnings and `--serve-out` before the trace line, with the same line in both engines, as executables do, and `--serve-out` refuses a hard link.
- **Chunked request bodies on many serve threads** (#1780, ADR 0123 rules 12 and 13). A chunked body no longer allocates once a chunk: its size lines share one buffer, so it allocates only as its buffers grow, O(log b) times for b bytes, and serve threads no longer contend for the process's counting allocator on bodies of small chunks (1-byte chunks, 8 connections: 8 serve threads took 233 ms against 118 ms on 1 before, and now about 42 ms against 82 ms). A connection that used up its steps of one pump while bytes it had read were not fed yet is now pumped again on its thread's next pass; before, it waited for the socket to signal again, which a peer whose request was complete never made happen, and was answered `408` when its `read` timer ran out.
- **`una build` serves** (#1782, ADR 0123, `spec/31 §1` rule 10 and §7 rule 5), edition 0.4 preview. `una build` of a program whose `main`, or a driven module's `init`, `step` or `on_stop`, uses `HttpServer`, and of a package with a `serve` section, now makes a native executable, with no test flag. The executable checks its grants before `main`, starts the serve threads, writes the same `serving on ...` line per address as `una run`, and drains at SIGINT or SIGTERM with exit code 0 when `una run` would. What stays closed: a `within` in the `serve` section is still U4003, and `una build` still refuses one; an `https` grant is still refused before `main` by the executable as by `una run`; the scripted connections of `--serve-script` still run only in the toolchain's own tests. `scripts/check-release.sh` now serves `examples/health` with the executable the packed `una` builds as well as with `una run`.
- **Serving HTTP** (#1783): a guide page, `docs/guide/serving-http.md`, for a package of edition `0.4` that serves: `serve:` grants and their limits, the `serve` section and the language form, the `serving on` line, host stops, `test_request` and `test_exchange` with the tests of `examples/health`, what `una report` says, the refusals and the residual limits, each with its rule of `spec/31`. `una build` builds such a package into a native executable that serves and drains at SIGTERM (#1782), and `docs/guide/known-limits.md` lists the limits of servers.
- **A test of the drain under load** (#1881, ADR 0123 rule 18, `spec/31 §6` rule 9 and §7 rule 4). A package with a `serve` section, under `una run` and as the executable of `una build`, takes SIGTERM with 32 requests in flight (200 with `UNA_DRAIN_REQUESTS=200`): each request is answered once and completely, `200` by its `handle` or `503` with `Retry-After: 1` by the host, a later connection is refused, `on_stop` runs last, the run ends with exit code 0 before the hard stop, and its port is free.
- **Every block of the serving guide is checked** (#1887): its manifests, module excerpts and outputs against `examples/health` and live runs, its language-form server as a package that checks and serves, and its quoted `--grant` flag, which a test now runs; the known-limits page refuses an unchecked block.
- **The timing oracle `serve_delivery_history` waits with a `within`** (#1891, #898, #1660). Its positive control waited on a loop that stopped on `Clock.now`, which a program that performs an operation of `HttpServer` reads at `L_srv` in edition 0.4, so `una check` refused it (U4002); the wait is now a loop that never ends by itself under a `within` whose deadline the key decides, 1 ms or 1.5 s, the same channel. The conformance case `serve_delivery_history_secret` follows it, still refused with U4003, and the cases `clock_wait_server` (U4002) and `clock_wait_server_within` (accepted) record the two forms.
- **`una run` of a server installs its stop handlers before it names its addresses** (#1920, ADR 0116 rule 18, `spec/31 §6` rule 14). The interpreter wrote the `serving on` lines and started the serve threads before it installed the handlers of SIGINT and SIGTERM, so a SIGTERM sent once those lines appeared could end the run by the signal's default action, with no drain; it now installs them first, as the executable does.

### Packages and Itá

- **Package content, digest and archive** (#893, ADR 0122 rules 27 to 29, `spec/30 §9`). The files a package of edition `0.4` holds (Una sources, `ita.una` and its documents only, with no foreign code), the canonical listing and the content digest that names it, and the uncompressed archive it travels in, with a reader that refuses any path, length, order or hash it does not expect. The digest and the reader are linear in the package's size, with a scaling test. Nothing loads a dependency yet.
- **Itá project files: `ita.una`, `ita.lock` and `itaty.una`** (#893, ADR 0122, `spec/30`). A package of edition `0.4` describes itself in `ita.una`, read by Una's own lexer with a version token and text without interpolation, by a closed grammar; every problem is U7008 at its line, and every problem of `ita.lock` is U7009. The sections of `una.toml` have their `ita.una` form with the same checks. `needs`, `sources`, `replace` and `trust` are read and checked (reserved local names and their skeletons, owners bound to their source, no git origin), and each need is refused until dependencies load. `una ita lock [--check]` writes or checks the lock, the binary invoked as `ita` is `una ita`, and `una fmt ita.una` formats the file. The proof cache's key for edition `0.4` covers the lock's solver section only, under the domain tag `una-proof-cache/2`. Editions `0.1` to `0.3` do not change.
- **`una migrate`** (#893, ADR 0122 rules 63 and 64, `spec/30 §8`). It moves a package from `una.toml` and `verification.lock`, of any edition from `0.1`, to `ita.una` and `ita.lock` of edition `0.4`: names with `-` become `_` (`--name` for others), every table becomes its section, grants become their lines, and comments are carried above the line they precede or listed. It then prints the diagnostics the change of edition causes, applying none; `--dry-run` writes nothing and `--json` prints `una.migrate/1`. `una.toml` naming edition `0.4` is now U7002, with `una migrate` as its repair.
- **Minimal version selection** (#893, ADR 0122 rules 20 to 24, `spec/30 §10`). The resolver of edition `0.4`: for each identity, the highest of the minimum versions the reached needs ask for, never the newest; raises past yanked, advised and avoided releases that wait the 72-hour cooldown, which only a threshold-signed advisory lifts for its fix; every bound of rule 23; and a scaling test on random graphs of up to 100,000 releases. It runs in memory; no command reads a registry yet.
- **Verifying a registry's documents** (#893, ADR 0122 rules 35 to 39, `spec/30 §11`). The primitives of the registry client: RFC 9162 inclusion and consistency proofs, with a test on logs of up to 2^30 leaves that the work grows by one hash per doubling; strict Ed25519 (canonical `S`, keys and `R` neither non-canonical nor of small order, the cofactorless equation); C2SP signed notes, checkpoints and cosignatures with canonical base64; and witness time as order statistics over the witness threshold. Ed25519 is `ed25519-dalek`'s strict verification, pure Rust with no C, behind this module's own checks of canonical encodings and small-order points. No command fetches yet.
- **Path dependencies** (#994, ADR 0122 rules 7, 42, 47 to 52, `spec/30 §12`). A package of edition `0.4` may need a package from a local directory, `util from path "../util"` with `accept name`, and import its modules under its local name, `import util.text (shout)`. The dependency is checked with the root as one program, read with no symbolic link followed, and every module of it is a package extent with the ceiling the root writes in `may` lines: a capability reaches it only by `with`, within that ceiling (U3020, U3021). `una ita lock` locks it with its digest and the ceiling granted, and records a wider ceiling only with `--accept-authority` (U7009). New codes: U1010 (a module named like a dependency's local name) and U7012 (a dependency the root may not use as written). Registry and workspace sources, and dependencies with needs, labels, trusted modules, boundaries or contracts of their own, are refused until their slices land.
- **Effective ceilings, `una ita tree` and `una ita remove`** (#995, ADR 0122 rules 51, 57 and 58, `spec/30 §12`). The report gains `dependencies`, one entry per package of the program other than the root, with its source, digest, the ceiling each dependent writes, its effective ceiling (rule 51's fixpoint) and the authority its code uses; `tcb` gains `registries` and `dependency_trust`. `una ita tree` prints the same per package, and `una ita remove <name>` takes a need out of `ita.una` and writes the lock. Neither opens a connection.
- **Trusted modules of dependencies** (#995, ADR 0122 rules 10, 53 and 53.1, `spec/30 §13`). A path dependency's trusted modules are trusted only by a trust line of the root naming each of them, and by the lock's consent to the package's whole content, which `una ita lock --accept-trust <package>` records again after any change (U7012 otherwise). Each is an extent of its own, entered with `with`, and a release whose policy it declares is not a release into a boundary. The report lists them under the dependency's `trust` and `tcb.dependency_trust`.
- **Itaoca, vendoring and offline loads** (#996, ADR 0122 rules 17 and 27 to 32, `spec/30 §9`). Every command that loads a package of edition `0.4` checks each registry package of its lock, with no network: it must be in the vendor directory or in Itaoca (U7011 otherwise), and, read again on every load, each file once into memory through a descriptor opened with `O_NOFOLLOW` and checked against the entry listed, the bytes read must have the locked digest before any of them is used, and only those bytes are used afterward and a stored provenance that verifies against the lock: the source's pinned root, the cosigned checkpoint of the lock's resolution point, the release statement's digest and signature by the locked key, and its inclusion proof (U7010 otherwise). `una ita vendor` copies each locked registry package from Itaoca into `vendor/ita/<source>/` with its provenance, writing the verified bytes into a fresh private directory (mode 0700, files created exclusively, permissions set through the descriptor, everything synced) that is then renamed into place, as Itaoca stores packages, and verifies the copy as a load does; a vendored copy that does not verify is never replaced. Path dependencies are read through descriptors too. No command opens a connection for a package, which a test of the sources checks. New codes: U7010 (a package or its provenance that fails verification) and U7011 (a locked package in neither place). Registry packages are still not mounted in a program, and no command fetches yet.
- **Resolution and the `una ita` commands** (#997, ADR 0122 rules 23 to 25 and 57, `spec/30 §6` and `§10`). `una ita lock` resolves by minimal version selection over what is held offline, each path dependency as its directory holds it, within the bounds of rule 23, the root's needs counted as a release's. A registry need is refused with the new code U7013, no resolution, since this release holds no registry's per-name state, and a source is refused as a whole rather than resolved from a partial view; an avoided version with no later release and a bound exceeded are U7013 too. Every change of the lock is printed as a review before it is written: per package, its origin, versions, digests, the ceiling granted, and what its code uses at tier 0, also as `changes` in `una.ita/1`. New: `--dry-run`; `una ita update [<name>...]` with `--cooldown`, `--now` and the `--accept-...` flags; `una ita add <name> [<version>] [--as] [--from]`, which writes `ita.una` and the lock only when the resolution succeeds, and so, in this release, never for a registry need; and `una ita verify`, which verifies each locked registry package again in `vendor/ita/` and in Itaoca. `una ita fetch` and rule 26's check of two release statements join with the registry client. No command opens a connection or starts a program. Each consent flag names what it accepts, `--accept-authority <package>` and `--accept-trust <package>@<digest>`, and without it the review is printed with the flags to copy and nothing is written; consent is checked against the digest of the bytes the package was loaded from. Which identities admit a pre-release is a fixed point, so the result never depends on the order of needs. `una ita update <names>` resolves only the named needs; `add` and `remove` write the lock, then `ita.una`, putting the lock back on failure; and `una ita verify` reports a package whose copies match the lock as not yet verified (U7013), since the owner's key chain is not held. Itaoca stores a provenance without ever replacing one, and new fuzz targets read provenances, archives and `ita.lock`. `ita.lock` belongs to the toolchain: a symbolic link at its name is replaced by the lock, never followed, and the file it led to is left as it was; `ita.una` is written through a link only when it leads inside the package, as for sources.
- **The name map and per-name chains** (#998, ADR 0122 rule 36.1, `spec/30 §11`). The client's check that it sees every entry about a name: proofs of a name's head, or its absence, in a sparse Merkle map whose root each checkpoint carries, and the walk of the name's chain, whose length must match the map's count, so an omitted yank or advisory is caught.
- **The registry client** (#998, ADR 0122 rules 26 and 34 to 40, `spec/30 §14`). `una ita lock`, `update` and `add` resolve the needs of a source of `sources` from its registry, read from the first of its places, the mirrors Itaoca's `mirrors.una` lists then its own URL, whose copy verifies, and verified from the pinned root to every entry: the root chain and its expiry, checkpoints with their witnesses, freshness and floors, the log's tiles and proofs, each name's whole chain against the name map, the owner's keys at each statement's position with revocations and recoveries in witness time, and rule 26 between releases. `una ita fetch` fills Itaoca with the locked releases, each archive checked against its statement, what its code uses included, and a verified release that a need names is mounted in the program.
