A change of an owner's keys needs `--accept-key` (the new code U7014). A mirror that serves a bad copy is skipped and named (U7010). This release has no https transport, so a source with no mirror directory, listed as a `file://` URL, is refused (U7013), and plain `http://` is never used. A change of an owner's recovery co-signers waits seven days of witness time, as a recovery does, and is reported as pending (U7014); competing disputes freeze the owner's chain, whose names are then refused (U7010).
- **Publishing, yanks and advisories on the client side** (#999, ADR 0122 rules 24, 37, 41 and 43, `spec/30 §11`). The checks that refuse a publication before anything is built, the release statement a publisher signs, yank and advisory statements, and the per-name state folded from a name's chain: yanked releases, advised ones, and the fix that only an advisory with a second security key or the owner's threshold lifts the cooldown for. `una ita audit`'s findings come from it. No command sends to a registry yet.
- **`una ita publish`, `yank`, `audit`, `watch`, `bundle` and `unbundle`** (#999, ADR 0122 rules 33, 36.2 and 41 to 43, `spec/30 §15`). `publish` refuses what rule 43 names, a package in a git repository while the check for uncommitted changes is not available, and any diagnostic of a tier 1 check; reads the name's verified state at a fresh checkpoint; prints rule 26's advice for a public header the earlier release of the line had; refuses authority that grows within a major line; signs the release statement with the owner's keys of `~/.una/keys/`; and sends it to the source that `publish to` names, which stops for now since there is no https transport yet. `yank` sends a yank signed by the owner's threshold the same way. The registry client now reads yanks and advisories in a name's chain, each verified with its thresholds, and resolution skips a yanked or advised release and takes a fix that a lifting advisory names with no cooldown. `audit` reports U7014, once per package, for a locked release that is yanked, under an advisory, or signed after a stated compromise, and for a pending recovery or a key change of its owner, at a fresh checkpoint. `watch` follows an owner's chains from a position kept in Itaoca and reports a registry's broken promise to log a submission. `bundle` and `unbundle` carry a lock's registry packages, with the registry's files that prove them, to an air-gapped machine, where everything is verified at the lock's resolution point before Itaoca is written, and the stored checkpoint never moves backwards. Plain `http://` is refused wherever a URL is read, and `una ita add` refuses a `--from` other than the source that serves a package's owner.
- **The Itaty registry service** (#1000, ADR 0122 rules 34 to 46, `spec/30 §16`). A new crate, `una-itaty`, that logs release statements, yanks, advisories and owner key events in a transparency log only after verifying them: signatures by keys valid at that point, each name's chain, the name rules, immutability, needs already logged, rule 26, and the statement against its archive, whose authority and trust it computes from the code. It serves the log as C2SP tiles, the name map as content-addressed tiles, archives by digest, signed checkpoints with the witnesses' cosignatures it collects, and signed promises, over HTTP/1.1 on the standard library, from a file-backed store. A witness that replays the log and the map before it cosigns is included. An end-to-end test publishes with the client's code and verifies every proof with it.
- **The security hunt covers programs of several packages** (#1001, ADR 0122). Family D tries authority, information flow and trust across a dependency's boundary, from a path or from a vendored registry release; family Y tries registry packages, with a registry, provenance and locks the hunt builds itself from `spec/30`: forged vendored copies, provenance and locks, which must be refused, and minimal version selection with yanks, avoided releases, pre-releases and the cooldown, judged by `una ita lock --check`, and provenance across root rotations, with a must-accept control beside each rotated forgery. The hunt gate runs 6,888 programs, with three new canaries.
- **The guide's pages for packages** (#1002, ADR 0122, `spec/30`): `ita.una`, `ita.lock`, path dependencies, a dependency's authority and the lock's consent, trust a dependency adds, versions and cooldowns, `una migrate` and the `una ita` commands, each with checked examples; and "Migrating to edition 0.4" in the guide for agents. Three more pages: registry dependencies (`sources`, `mirrors.una`, `una ita add` and `fetch`), offline builds (`vendor/ita/`, `verify`, `bundle` and `unbundle`) and publishing (`publish`, `itaty.una`, the owner's keys, `yank`, `audit` and `watch`), each example package checked; the migration steps in the guide for agents; and `spec/13 §5` and `§6` and `spec/30 §6` completed with `una migrate`, `una ita`, `--fresh` and the `error` key of a resolving command's document.
- **A ceiling message names the package's manifest** (#1084). U3021 and U3024 named "the ceiling in `una.toml`" in every edition. In edition `0.4` a boundary's or a dependency's ceiling is written in `ita.una`, which the messages now name.
- **spec/30 and spec/21 clarified** (#1085). `una ita lock` locks path dependencies (§12) and refuses only a need of a registry or of a workspace member; each problem of a need is reported at its line in `ita.una`; `initial` of a map's `update` has the map's value type, so `initial: []` takes it.
- **spec/30 states the root-version floor with its digest** (#1538, ADR 0122 amendment of 2026-11-09, L3; `spec/30 §9` rule 7, §14 Floors, §15). The specification now gives the floor file's format, `<n> sha256:<hex>` and exactly one line feed, the rule that only a higher version replaces it, the earlier form `<n>` compared by version only, a file in neither form as a floor that cannot be read (U7013 offline), and the refusal (U7010) of a root chain that holds the floor's version with another digest, a fork, by the offline load, `una ita unbundle` and the registry client. Documentation only; the behavior shipped in slice 4.
- **The registry service names the root a checkpoint was signed under, and keeps it when it signs a size again** (#1561, `spec/30 §11` rule 9 and `§16` rules 5 and 9). Each checkpoint carries `ita-root <n> sha256:<hex>`; after a root rotation and a restart, a size whose checkpoint is stored keeps the version, key and bytes it was first signed with, and the rotation takes effect from the next entry. Opening checks every stored checkpoint against the root chain, with versions that never decrease, and a store has one writer, locked while open. A store written before this change, whose checkpoints name no root, no longer opens.

### Numbers and collections

- **`F64` in edition 0.3, a preview: the front end, the checker and the interpreter** (#819, ADR 0114, `spec/29`). IEEE 754 binary64 with one canonical NaN: float literals (`0.5`, `6.674_30e-11`, `1.0e21`), `+ - * /` correctly rounded, division by zero giving infinities or NaN, `==` by identity and `<` by IEEE totalOrder (so `F64` is `Eq` and `Ord`, sorts deterministically and keys a map), the IEEE comparisons as methods, `abs`, `sqrt`, `fma`, `rem`, the roundings, `copy_sign`, `ieee_min`, `ieee_max`, `clamp`, the classifications, `to_bits`, `to_f64()` on integers and `to_int_exact()`, and the prelude functions `nan`, `infinity`, `f64_from_bits`, `parse_f64`, `as_f64` and `json_number`. Rendering writes the shortest text that reads back to the same bits. New codes U2031 (a literal out of range), U2032 (a float pattern), U2033 (a comparison with a literal zero) and U6006 (a floating-point environment that is not the default one, tested before an edition 0.3 package is read). This step runs in the interpreter; native code and the verifier's floating-point encoding follow, and until then a float in a clause is U2014 and `una build` reports a float program as not compiled yet.
- **Keyed hashing for the hash tables of edition `0.5`** (#974, ADR 0119 rules 9 and 19). A new crate, `una-hash`, shared by both engines: SipHash-2-4 with its reference vectors, the hash encoding of an `Eq` value, the process key from the operating system's random source through `getrandom` (in the trusted base, `docs/security/tcb.md`) and one key per table, and the ordered hash table with its own index. A new runtime code, U6009, aborts a run when the host gives no random bytes. Its test hooks exist only in test builds, and the release checks of #1015 look for their marker in the shipped `una` and `libuna_rt.a`. `spec/28` starts with its section on keyed hashing. No construct is added yet.
- **`std.collections` and `Map.update` in edition `0.5`** (#975, ADR 0119 rules 0, 2, 3, 8, 11, 13 and 18). A package of edition `0.5` can import `std.collections`, which exports the types `Stack`, `Queue`, `Set`, `HashMap`, `HashSet`, `PriorityQueue` and `BitSet` and the record `Ranked`; their operations land with each type. A `Set` item and a priority need `Ord`, a hash key `Eq`; `HashMap`, `HashSet` and `PriorityQueue` have no `==`, with a message that names `same_entries(as:)` or `same_items(as:)`, and still interpolate. A package function named like one of their built-in methods, whose first parameter is that type or a type parameter, is U1002 in `0.5`, and so is an `update` on a `Map`. Maps gain `edit m.update(key:, initial:, change:)`, which calls `change` once and keeps the map when it fails. `spec/28` gains its sections on the module, the types and their equality, and the Una form. Earlier editions do not change.
- **`Stack` and `Queue` in edition `0.5`** (#976, ADR 0119 rules 5 and 6). `std.collections` exports `empty_stack()` and `empty_queue()`. A stack has `edit s.push(item:)`, `edit s.pop()`, `peek()`, `items()` and `.len`; a queue has the same, with `push` at the back and `pop` at the front, and `edit q.push_front(item:)`, `edit q.pop_back()` and `peek_back()` at the other ends. A removal gives `Option[T]`, `None` on an empty collection. Updates are O(1) amortized and in place when no other binding holds the collection, and the buffer shrinks as it empties. Both compare with `==` and write as a list, top or front first. Labels follow a list's: an update under a `secret` condition into a `public` collection is U4001. The verifier knows their lengths and the refinement of a pushed item; the native backend reports both types as not yet supported. `spec/28` gains their section.
- **`Set` in edition `0.5`** (#977, ADR 0119 rule 7). `std.collections` exports `empty_set`, and a `Set[T]` has the built-in methods `has`, `edit add`, `edit remove`, `with`, `without`, `first`, `last`, `between`, `items`, `union`, `intersection`, `difference` and `is_subset`, and `.len`. A set lists its items in ascending order and renders as `{1, 2, 3}`. Lookups, updates, `first` and `last` take O(log n); each set operation takes the cheaper of a merge, O(n + m), and lookups of the smaller side's items. Updates of a set held only once happen in place. The items are the set's shape: its top label covers their labels, `has` carries the item's labels, and an item above an editable set's top label is U4001. `add`, `with` and `union` store items with the `refinement` obligations of a refined item type. The interpreter runs sets; the native backend reports them as not supported yet. `spec/28 §6` states the operations, their costs and their labels.
- **`HashMap` and `HashSet` in edition `0.5`** (#978, ADR 0119 rules 8 to 12). `std.collections` exports `empty_hash_map` and `empty_hash_set`. A `HashMap[K, V]` has the built-in methods `has`, `get`, `edit set`, `edit remove`, `with`, `without`, `edit update`, `keys`, `values`, `entries` and `same_entries`, and a `HashSet[T]` has `has`, `edit add`, `edit remove`, `with`, `without`, `items`, `union`, `intersection`, `difference`, `is_subset` and `same_items`; both have `.len`. Keys and items are of any `Eq` type, records and lists included, hashed with SipHash-2-4 under a key of each table derived from a process key that the run reads from the host's random source before `main`, and only when its code can make a table; a host without random bytes aborts the run with U6009 before any effect. Tables list in insertion order, so no output or trace depends on the key, and have no `==`: `same_entries(as:)` and `same_items(as:)` compare as sets. Lookups and updates are O(|k|) expected, and updates of a table held only once happen in place. The keys and items are the table's shape for labels, `update` runs `change` under the key's label, and stored keys, values and items carry the `refinement` obligations of refined types. The interpreter runs both types; the native backend reports them as not supported yet. `spec/28 §7` states the operations, their costs and their labels.
- **`PriorityQueue` in edition `0.5`** (#979, ADR 0119 rule 13). `std.collections` exports `lowest_first` and `highest_first`, and a `PriorityQueue[T, P]` has the built-in methods `edit push`, `edit pop`, `edit pop_ranked`, `peek`, `peek_priority` and `items`, and `.len`. `pop` gives the item of the least priority for a queue made by `lowest_first`, of the greatest for one made by `highest_first`, and among equal priorities the one pushed first, so the order of the pops is the same in both engines. `pop_ranked` gives the item with its priority as a `Ranked`. A queue lists its items in the order of the pops, renders as a list, and has no `==`. `push` and `pop` take O(log n) comparisons, amortized, `peek` and `peek_priority` O(1), and `items` O(n log n); updates of a queue held only once happen in place. The priorities are the queue's shape: its top label covers their labels, every read carries it, and a priority above an editable queue's top label is U4001. `push` stores its item and its priority with the `refinement` obligations of refined types. The interpreter runs priority queues as a stable 4-ary heap; the native backend reports them as not supported yet. `spec/28 §8` states the operations, their costs and their labels.
- **`BitSet` and the conversions in edition `0.5`** (#980, ADR 0119 rules 14 and 16). `std.collections` exports `empty_bit_set(size:)`, which requires a size of at least 0, and a `BitSet` holds integers from 0 to `size - 1`, one bit each: `.size`, `.len`, `has`, `edit add`, `edit remove`, `with`, `without`, `union`, `intersection`, `difference`, `is_subset`, `first`, `last` and `items`. A bit set lists its members in ascending order and renders as `{1, 2, 3}`; two are equal when they have the same size and members. `has`, the updates, `size` and `len` take O(1); the set operations, `first` and `last` O(size / 64), and `items` O(size / 64 + len). `add` and `with` require their item in the range and the set operations operands of one size, `precondition` obligations that the verifier proves from the size `empty_bit_set` gives and every operation keeps; the run-time check stays and aborts with U8001 if a wrong proof lets one fail. `empty_bit_set` checks its words against the memory budget before allocating (U6003). The members and the size are the shape: a bit set made with a labeled size takes its label, `has` carries the item's labels, and an item above an editable bit set's top label is U4001. `std.collections` also exports the conversions `to_stack`, `to_queue`, `to_set`, `to_hash_set` and `to_hash_map`, called with method syntax on a list; the last two list their items or keys in the order of first occurrence, and `to_hash_map` keeps the value of a key's last entry. A `BitSet` is a hash key: it encodes as its size, then its words. The interpreter runs bit sets and the conversions; the native backend reports them as not supported yet. `spec/28 §9` and `§10` state the operations, their costs and their labels.
- **The verifier knows the collections of edition `0.5`** (#982, ADR 0119 V1 to V8, `spec/28 §11`). Each collection has its length, and the verifier knows each operation's effect: an item just added or set is a member, the length grows by one exactly when it was not, `get` after `set` gives the value, a stack pushed then popped is the stack before, a push puts its item at its end of a queue, and every read is `None` exactly when its collection is empty. An update at a key keeps every other key's membership, and a map's `get`, in sets, hash sets, hash maps, bit sets and, in edition `0.5`, maps (ADR 0069 amendment): a key that may equal the updated one keeps nothing, so such a goal is refuted, never proven. `union`, `intersection`, `difference` and `is_subset` give their membership and length facts; `same_entries` and `same_items` give equal lengths and nothing about order. `.len` of every collection, `b.size` and `has` of a set, a hash set, a hash map and a bit set are in the refinement language, so `requires queue.len > 0` and `kind Hall = BitSet where it.size == 100` can be written. Each query stays quantifier-free: the frames and set relations are instantiated at the keys the query looks up, O(k · t) facts for k updates and t keys.
- **The formal re-check refuses the collections of `std.collections` by name** (#983, ADR 0119 implementation plan step 8, `spec/28 §12`). F1 does not model `Stack`, `Queue`, `Set`, `HashMap`, `HashSet`, `PriorityQueue` or `BitSet` yet, so the re-check reports a declaration whose Core holds one of their types, methods or functions, and what reaches it, as not re-checked, never accepted, with the reason "F1 restriction (the collection ..., ADR 0119)". Such declarations were already not re-checked, as forms the importer did not read; the reason now names the collection, and `formal/UnaFormal/F1/CollectionsImportTests.lean` pins the verdicts on `una`'s own export.
- **`F32` and the finite kinds in edition 0.5** (#984, ADR 0120 rules 5 to 11 and V1, `spec/29 §18`). `F32` is IEEE binary32 under every rule of `F64`: one canonical NaN (`0x7FC00000`), `==` by identity, `<` by totalOrder, no contraction. A float literal is an `F32` where an `F32` is expected, an operator's other operand included, and is rounded once to binary32, never through binary64 (`0.1` is `0x3DCCCCCD`); a literal beyond the binary32 bounds is U2031. `F32` and `F64` do not mix (U2001, with a repair that converts); `to_f32()` converts an integer or an `F64`, `to_f64()` an `F32`, and `fma` rounds once in binary32. The prelude gains `parse_f32`, `f32_from_bits`, and the refined kinds `Finite64` and `Finite32`. The verifier encodes `F32` exactly as SMT-LIB `Float32`, both engines compute the same bits, native code holds an `F32` in one word without allocating, and the environment's self-test gains binary32 probes. `simd` is no longer reserved in edition 0.5. Edition 0.5 makes a declaration named `F32`, `Complex`, `Array`, `Matrix`, `Tensor` or `Lu`, and a package function `to_f32` on an integer, an `F64` or a type parameter, U1002.
- **`Decimal[s]` in edition 0.5: exact base 10 for money** (#985, ADR 0120 rules 12 to 21, `spec/29 §16`). A decimal is an integer coefficient and a scale from 0 to 38 that is part of its type. A float literal where a decimal is expected is its exact value, never rounded, taking the other operand's scale in `+`, `-` and `*` and its own digits in a comparison. `+` and `-` align scales, `*` adds them, comparisons are by value across scales, and `/` and `mod` are refused with repairs. Every rounding is written: `widen`, `rescale`, `rescale_exact`, `divide`, `remainder`, `to_int` and the prelude's `Rounding` with seven modes. There are also `abs`, `coefficient`, `to_f64`, `n.to_decimal()` on integers, and `x.to_decimal(scale:, rounding:)` on `F64`. The prelude functions are `parse_decimal` and `as_decimal`, under the shared digit cap of 4,300 (`TooLong`), and `json_decimal`. Decimals are `Eq` and `Ord`, key maps, carry labels, run the same in the interpreter and in native code, and verify as their coefficients (ADR 0120 V2), with `division` and `rescale_exact` obligations and counterexamples shown by value. New code U2037 (a decimal scale out of place).
- **`Complex[F64]` and `Complex[F32]` in edition 0.5** (#986, ADR 0120 rules 22 to 25 and V3, `spec/29 §19`). Two built-in types with the fields `re` and `im`, built by `complex(re: x, im: y)`, whose two arguments are both `F64` or both `F32` (U2001 otherwise); `Complex` of any other type is U2002. `+`, `-`, `*`, `/`, unary `-`, `conj()`, `scale(by:)` and `norm_sqr()` are defined by the operations of the components, in a fixed order and with no contraction, so both engines compute the same bits; `/` is Smith's algorithm, with its documented loss when the ratio of the divisor's components underflows. `==` compares both components by identity. A complex and a real operand are U2001, with repairs to `scale(by:)` and to `complex(re: x, im: 0.0)`; `<` and `mod` are U2001. `to_text`, interpolation and the Una form write `complex(re: 1.0, im: -2.0)`. In contracts, `re`, `im`, `complex(re:, im:)` and `==` are in the refinement language, encoded as an uninterpreted sort with its two field functions; the arithmetic and the methods are U2014. A complex value carries the labels of its components. A package's own `complex` keeps its meaning.
- **Dense values in edition 0.5, a preview: `Tensor[T]`, `Lu[T]`, the factorizations and the solves** (#987, ADR 0120, `spec/29 §17`). `Tensor[T]` takes `F64` or a fixed-width integer element type and has a rank from 1 to 8. Its shape, index and permutation are list literals, so each call gets the contract of its rank: `tensor(shape: [2, 3], elements: xs)`, `tensor_filled(shape: [...], value: x)`, `t.get(at: [i, j])`, `t.at(at: [...])`, `edit t.set(at: [...], item: x)`, `t.permute(axes: [1, 0])` and `t.reshape(shape: [...])`. Anything else in their place is the new U2038, and so is an axis that is not an integer literal from 0 to 7. `t.rank` and `t.dim(axis: k)` are in the refinement language. Tensors also have `sum`, `sum(axis:)`, `contract(with:, axis:, other:)` (each element an inner product in blocks of 64, so its bits are `multiply`'s), the element-wise methods and operators, and the conversions `to_tensor`, `to_array` and `to_matrix`. On `F64` matrices: `lu()` gives an opaque `Lu[T]`, with `size`, `factors()`, `pivots()` and `solve(rhs:)`, and rendered as `<lu size n>`. `solve`, `solve_array`, `cholesky`, `solve_lower` and `solve_upper` follow the blocked chains of ADR 0120 rules 48 to 50, so the bits are the same in every engine. A factorization and a solve run to their end whatever the values, and the top of their `Option` carries the labels of the elements. `Lu`, `Matrix` and `Tensor` have no constructor (U2001). Verification gains the tensor sorts with their rank, lengths and element count, the shape frame of a tensor, and the shapes that `lu`, `solve` and `cholesky` hold on `Some`. `to_finite` follows with `F32`; native code follows too, and until then `una build` reports a program with a dense value as not compiled yet.
- **Dense values in edition 0.5, a preview: `Array[T]` and `Matrix[T]`, the front end, the checker, the verifier and the interpreter** (#987, ADR 0120, `spec/29 §17`). `Array[T]` is the one fixed-length array, of any element type; `Matrix[T]` takes `F64` or a fixed-width integer element type (U2002 otherwise). The prelude gains `array`, `array_filled`, `matrix`, `matrix_filled` and `matrix_diagonal`. Reads (`get`, `at`), updates in place (`edit x.set(...)`, copy on write), O(1) views (`slice`, `row`, `column`, `block`, `transpose`) and `compact`, element-wise `+`, `-`, `-x` and a scalar `*` and `/` (with the element type's `overflow` obligation on fixed-width elements), `map`, `zip_map`, `update`, `fold`, `to_list`, and on `F64` elements `multiply_elements`, `divide_elements`, `sum`, `row_sums`, `column_sums`, `dot`, `norm2`, `all_finite`, `multiply`, `multiply_array`, `add_product`, `add_scaled` and `scale`; on integer elements `sum_exact` and `dot_exact`. `*` and `/` between two dense values are U2001, with `multiply(by:)` and `multiply_elements(by:)` as repairs, and no scalar is broadcast. Sums add in 32 lanes combined in a fixed tree, and inner products in blocks of 64 `fma` terms, so a dot product and the matching element of a matrix product are the same bits; both sizes are unsettled points of the preview. Shapes and integer elements are in the refinement language, and the shape frame keeps a matrix's rows and columns through a loop that only updates it. In edition 0.5, a declaration named `Array`, `Matrix`, `Tensor` or `Lu` is U1002. `Tensor`, `Lu` and the factorizations, the other element types, and native code follow; until then `una build` reports a program with a dense value as not compiled yet.
- **The dense kernels in `una-core`** (#988, ADR 0120 rules 44 to 54, `spec/29 §17` rule 32). Sums, inner products, `norm2`, matrix products, LU, Cholesky and the triangular solves have blocked kernels beside their reference chains, with the same bits: products run by tiles of 64 columns and blocks of 64 terms, LU and Cholesky are right-looking with trailing updates by tiles, and a triangular solve runs every column of its right-hand side at once, each in O(1) space beyond its result. Their `fma` is the instruction on AArch64 and, on x86-64, an exact emulation by error-free transformations and rounding to odd, which falls back to the exact integer routine outside the range of its proof, so it is correctly rounded for every operand. Every stored float is canonical and no kernel exits early on a value. The interpreter runs the blocked kernels, reading contiguous operands in place. A corpus of inputs and the bits of their outputs, written by `scripts/dense-corpus.py` from an independent implementation with exact rational `fma`, is checked bit for bit against every kernel, and exact operation counts at growing sizes check the stated complexities.
- **Dense kernels use the processor's FMA where it has one** (#989, ADR 0120 rules 53, 56 and 57): the kernels of `una-core` gain target variants for x86-64-v3 and x86-64-v4, each enabling exactly the features its detection checks (a test asks the toolchain for their implications), and a native executable chooses one once at start, with the bits of the baseline on every processor; the build report's `native` section gains `kernels`, the variants and the FMA allowlist.
- **Dense values in native code** (#989, ADR 0120 rules 26 to 43, `spec/29 §17`). `una build` compiles programs with `Array`, `Matrix`, `Tensor` and `Lu` values: one header per value over a store of raw canonical `F64` elements shared by its views and, across tasks, by the copies of a `together` block, or of words otherwise; every buffer reserved within the memory budget before it is allocated (U6003 at the call, never an abort of the process); `set`, `update`, `add_product`, `add_scaled` and `scale` in place when the value and its store are held once, and a copy of the value's own elements otherwise; the kernels of the interpreter, so both engines print the same text and bits.
- **Units of measure on the new numbers** (#990, ADR 0114 rule 12, ADR 0120 rules 61 to 65, `spec/25 §9`). `of` follows `F64` from edition 0.3, and `F32`, `Decimal[s]`, `Complex[F64]` and `Complex[F32]` in edition 0.5, and a dense value carries its unit in its element type, as `Matrix[F64 of Meter]`. `+`, `-` and the comparisons need one unit, the comparisons of decimals across scales; `*`, `/` and the products (`dot`, `multiply`, `multiply_array`, `multiply_elements`, a scalar's `*`, `scale(by:)` of a complex value) multiply or divide units, and `sqrt()` and `norm_sqr()` halve and double the exponents; each method keeps, needs or refuses a unit by its rule, as `to_bits()` and `coefficient()` need a unitless receiver. `convert()` multiplies or divides an `F64` by a ratio of at most 2^53 and an `F32` by one of at most 2^24, and multiplies a decimal's coefficient by an integer ratio, exactly; a currency is a unit, and an exchange rate a value of a quotient unit. Mismatches are U2028 and refused conversions U2030. Units are erased before verification and code generation, so they cost nothing and change no value.
- **`F64` in contracts: the verifier's FloatingPoint encoding** (#1033, #819, ADR 0114 rule 8, `spec/29 §8`). In edition 0.3, float literals, `nan()`, `infinity()`, `+ - * /`, unary `-`, `==`, `!=`, the orders, the IEEE comparisons, `abs()`, `sqrt()`, the classifications and `to_f64()` of an integer are in the refinement language, encoded exactly as SMT-LIB `Float64` with `RNE`, so `kind Unit01 = F64 where it.ieee_ge(other: 0.0) and it <= 1.0` and `ensures result <= 1.0` are proven or refuted with the run-time semantics. A query with an `F64` term is in the logic `ALL` with the fixed step budget 5,000,000; every other query is byte-identical. Integer obligations of float code keep only the float facts they need (the cone), so an index beside float arithmetic stays a cheap integer query. A float query heavier than the bound of 4 is U5002 before solving, and every float `unknown` names its reason and repairs. Counterexamples show an `F64` by its rendering (`"0.1"`, `"-0.0"`, `"nan"`).
- **`F64` in native code** (#1034, #819, ADR 0114 rules 6 and 9.6, `spec/29 §9`). `una build` compiles programs that compute with `F64`, every value boxed until the raw cells of ADR 0107 land, every operation computed by the same routines as the interpreter's, so both engines print the same bits. A native executable of edition 0.3 writes the default floating-point environment on each of its threads and tests it before `main`, stopping with U6006 when it differs.
- **Decimal runtime: no default rounding for an invalid word** (#1042, `spec/29 §16` rule 4): a `Rounding` word that is no variant of `Rounding`, which only a defect of the generated code or the runtime can pass, ends the run with U8001 instead of rounding as `HalfEven`; outside a run it is a counted fault with a result of the right shape, as the any-word entry-point test requires.
- **A correctly rounded `exp` in una-core** (#1061, ADR 0114 rule 18, step 2, part 1), not yet reachable from a program. `una_core::elementary::exp` evaluates in fixed point on Una's own integers with a rigorous error bound and returns a result only when both ends of its bracket round to the same `F64` (Ziv's strategy, from 128 bits, doubling), so it is correctly rounded on every input with no platform math library and no table; tests check it against an independent interval oracle on dense grids, every binade, the thresholds and the hard cases near 1.
- **`Decimal.to_f64()` no longer depends on the target** (#1090). Its fast path divides by 10^s read from a table of exact binary64 values, instead of `powi`, whose precision Rust leaves to the target. No released version had `Decimal`.
- **Una's own big integer** (#1093, epic #1097). `Int`, the coefficients of `Decimal` and the exact steps of floating-point conversions now use `una_core::bigint`, safe Rust with no dependency, in the interpreter and the native runtime; num-bigint, num-integer and num-traits leave the build graph of every crate. Every result is the same bit for bit, checked against num-bigint on random operands before the switch. Multiplication is schoolbook below 40 limbs, Karatsuba, and Toom-3 from 256 limbs; division is Knuth's algorithm D below 64 limbs of divisor or quotient and Burnikel-Ziegler above; text conversion divides and conquers past 32 limbs; a value of one limb never allocates. The 4,300-digit bound on writing and parsing an integer as text stays as specified (#1223 lifts it). `scripts/no-libm.sh` now asserts that the release graph holds none of those crates.
- **Big integer follow-ups** (#1368, after #1093). Products whose shorter operand has more than 9/16 of the longer one's limbs take Toom-3 from 256 limbs, where Karatsuba was up to 15% slower than the num-bigint baseline; `Int` and `BigUint` operands of at most two limbs divide in 128-bit arithmetic (a 2-limb by 1-limb division was 1.44x to 1.47x the baseline, now 0.355x to 0.665x); reading decimal text keeps Horner's rule up to 14,592 digits (faster from 7,399 to 12,330 digits, but 1.03x instead of 0.95x the baseline at 19,729 digits, sizes only internal callers reach); writing digits divides by radix powers without their zero low limbs, 5% to 10% faster on random values from 128 limbs. Results are unchanged bit for bit. `docs/plan/1093-bigint-design.md` now states the measured ratios, including the cases still slower than num-bigint (powers of ten written as text: 1.01x to 1.40x at the sizes measured inside the 4,300-digit bound, 1.68x to 2.50x from 256 limbs). An allocation of an impossible size reports the layout that was asked for. ADR 0004, 0055 and 0120's sections of #1093 are headed as amendments.
- **The formal re-check no longer rejects `checked` of a collection** (#1676, ADR 0103 rules 3 and 4). F1 types `checked` of a scalar only; a `checked` of a list, whose predicate is on the whole value, is now reported as not re-checked, a form F1 does not model, instead of as a rejection. A `checked` of a labeled collection, or of one under a raised program counter, is still rejected (U4002). No declaration becomes accepted.

### Checker, diagnostics and tools

- **Counterexamples with a value of a sum kind** (#855, `spec/12 §5`, `§7`, `spec/13 §6`): the solver script declares every symbol of the requested terms together with the query's own, so a shown value of a sum kind that no assertion mentions no longer makes the model unreadable; and when the solver gives no values, the human note is the fixed `counterexample: not available (the solver gave no values for the model)`, with the solver's message, which names internal symbols, kept in the `--json` document only.
- **`una check` and `una build` report U3012 for `grants = []`** (#925, `spec/13 §5`). A listed empty `[run] grants` was treated as absent, so `una check` and `una build` accepted a `main` that uses host capabilities, as `spec/13 §5` did not allow. Such a program never ran: `una run` and the executable stopped with U3012 before starting. A listed `grants`, empty or not, is now the final set for both commands, in every edition.
- **A `together` body whose last line is not a `let` reports U2024** (#928, `spec/19 §1` rule 3). When the block was also the last line of its own block, the parser reported only U0101; it now leaves the body to the checker, which reports U2024 at `together` as for any other position.
- **`together for` placement: the spec and the message name its three forms** (#929, `spec/19 §1` rule 2). A `together for` may be the value of a `let`, the value of an assignment or the last line of a block; `spec/19` now says so, and from edition `0.2` the U0101 message for a misplaced `together for`, `when`, `if` with blocks or `first of` names assignments too. No change to what parses.
- **What an invalid `una.toml` keeps** (#930, `spec/13 §7`): the spec and the guide's page for `[package] name` now state the behavior exactly. Only a TOML syntax error gives the default settings, and so edition `0.1`; an unknown table or key, and a value of the wrong type or outside its range, are U7002 and keep that key's default, while every other key, the edition included, is read as written. Two conformance cases pin both sides. No change to the compiler.
- **U2019 on a loop variable suggests a separate `let edit` binding** (#931, `spec/17 §1`). Assigning the variable of a `for` loop or of a `together for` used to suggest declaring it `let edit`, which a loop variable cannot be; the message now says that the variable is read-only and suggests a copy in the body, as `let edit next_x = x`.
- **Each diagnostic is reported once, and the conformance runner checks it** (#932). The duplicate U3007 seen with una 0.3.15 does not reproduce on main: a scan of every conformance case, example and Learn package, at tiers 0 and 1, finds no diagnostic reported twice. A new case reports U3007 once at each of eight sites (loop bodies, a lambda in a loop, a `when` arm, a handler operation, a private function without `fails`, `within` and a whole body), and the runner now fails any case in which `una check` reports a diagnostic twice with the same code, span and message.
- **The exact repair of U0101 for `if c then` at the end of a line keeps an `else if` chain** (#935, ADR 0096 rule 3). When the `else` line continues the chain with `else if d` and blocks of its own, the edit no longer turns that line into an unindented branch under a bare `else`: the chain stays at the `if`'s indentation, each branch with its block, and a `then` that ends an `else if` line goes with the same edit, so the repaired range parses, as an exact edit must (`spec/13 §5`).
- **LSP: show canonical escape and widening costs in repair suggestions** (#1108). Expose canonical escape and widening costs in editor repairs, with CLI parity and transitive-effect hover coverage.
- **A file named to `una fmt`, `una check` and the other commands uses the edition of the package that holds it** (#1175). Before, `una fmt src/x.una` in an edition 0.4 package joined its wrapped lines and `una check` reported U7001 on it. Only the edition is taken from the nearest `ita.una` or `una.toml`; a file with no manifest above it keeps edition 0.1.
- **A list literal given for a field of a generic kind takes the type the expected type fixes** (#1657, ADR 0103 rules 3 and 5, `spec/18 §2` rule 1). `Box(v: [5])` where a `Box[List[Pos]]` is expected, with `kind Pos = Int where it > 0`, was U5001: the literal was typed `List[Int]`, so its flow into the field `List[Pos]` asked of a fresh element with no facts, which is refuted. A list literal now takes the field's type at the expected type arguments, as an integer literal and a constructor already did, also for a field of type `List[T]` and inside `Some`, so each element is its own obligation: `Box(v: [5])` is proven and `Box(v: [0])` stays U5001. For the same reason `Box(v: [1, 2])` where a `Box[List[U8]]` is expected is accepted, as `spec/18 §2` rule 1 says, where it was U2001.
- **U0103 messages no longer promise versions that shipped nothing** (#1899, `spec/01 §5`, `spec/17 §9`). `law`, `all`, `costs`, `traced`, `service`, `table`, `query`, `observe` and `commit`, and `shared`, `iso` and `weak` from edition 0.2, now say "reserved for a later edition (`<area>`)" instead of naming v0.3 or v0.4. The words stay reserved and the code is unchanged.
- **Elaboration hardening** (#1975).

### Performance

- **Parsing JSON objects is linear when their keys are in order** (#886, spec/23 §6 rule 1). Both engines build a parsed object's map in bulk from the parser's sorted entries, rather than inserting each key, so parsing JSON whose objects' keys arrive in order, as `to_json` writes them, takes time linear in its size. A test bounds the key comparisons per entry at sizes up to 2^16 in both engines.
- **Updates inside nested loops stay in place in the interpreter** (#1196). A `for` loop no longer keeps a second reference to the values it edits, so an update of a uniquely held collection inside an inner loop no longer copies the whole value on each entry to that loop: nested loops that update a collection are linear again, not quadratic. A value that another binding still holds is still copied when written.
- **Native text reads no longer copy their operands** (#1341, `spec/22 §2` and the complexity rule of the Proposed ADR for the standard library). In native code, `starts_with`, `ends_with`, `contains_text`, `==` and `<` on texts, structural equality of a text leaf, and the order of `Text` in `contains`, `sort`, `min` and `max` now read the texts in place, borrowed from the heap, so a call costs what it reads rather than the sizes of its operands: `ends_with` is O(m), a comparison stops at the first difference, and `contains` of a long item over many short texts is linear in the size of the list. Results are unchanged; a conformance case covers empty texts, a prefix longer than the text, equal prefixes, a shared first byte and multi-byte scalar values, and two scaling tests hold the bounds.
- **`fake_hash` is linear in its seed** (#1342, `spec/14 §4`, the complexity rule of the Proposed ADR for the standard library). `std.testing.fake_hash(seed)` no longer writes its seed in decimal: it hashes a fixed domain prefix and the seed's hash encoding of `spec/28 §1` rule 2 (sign and magnitude bytes, self-delimiting), in O(d) for a seed of d limbs. Equal seeds still give equal values and different seeds different values; the concrete digests change, but a `Hashed` value is observable only through `==`, so the results of programs do not change; only the digests that traces and failed examples print (`hashed:<hex>`) differ. A conformance case covers zero, signs, magnitudes and seeds past 64 bits, and a scaling test holds seeds of d and 10d limbs within 30 times.
- **`Files` confinement makes O(c) lookups on its normal path** (#1343, `spec/14 §3`, ADR 0038 D9). `confine`, which both engines use, resolved every intermediate directory by canonicalizing the whole lexical prefix up to it, O(c²) lookups of components for a path of c components. Each directory is now resolved from the one before it, already canonical, through a new `World::step` that the worlds of `una run` and of native executables answer with one `lstat` (and a canonicalization only for a link); one canonicalization of the lexical prefix where that resolution stops confirms it, and the next one decides as before. Where the two disagree, which takes a host limit (more links in one path than the host follows, a lexical path longer than the host takes) or a name spelled in another case on a case-insensitive file system, the earlier prefix-by-prefix resolution decides, at its earlier cost of O(c²) lookups, which any path can reach; the bound of O(c) holds for paths that do not. The rules of `spec/14 §3` and every outcome (`Denied`, `NotFound`, the path opened) are unchanged: a differential test compares the earlier algorithm with the new one over every path of up to three components, links and dangling links included, on the disk and on an in-memory model, a test runs the same link cases in both engines, and a counting world holds paths of c and 10c components within 20 times as many lookups.
- **Checking a file with many syntax errors is no longer quadratic, and the tests that failed under load are deterministic** (#1573, ADR 0096 rule 12). The recovery of broken declarations and the repairs of U0101 and U0001 found the declaration or line of each error by a scan, and the renames of names that start with `_` read the whole file again for each error; they now use binary searches and one set of the file's words, O(n log n) in the declarations, lines and errors of a file, with a new scaling test for the renames (one very large declaration with thousands of `_` names is still quadratic in its own size and has its own card, #1635). The tests of the solver child's stand-in, the tick and operation waits, the native trace of S-39 under the real clock and the HTTP time-out at a limit no longer depend on the machine's load: a bounded retry keyed to a late handshake only, a latency floor measured beside the waits, a comparison task by task as `spec/27 §8` defines it with whole traces under `--virtual-time`, and a time-out that leaves the first exchange room.
- **The renames of names that start with `_` stay linear in one large declaration** (#1635, ADR 0096 rule 12). The repairs of U0001 read each declaration once, into its words and their offsets, and compute each rename once per declaration and name, reused by every error of that name; the declaration of an error is found by a binary search over the declaration starts, read once per file, in these renames and in the exact rewrites of U0101. A function with `n` names `_x` took 0.24 s at 500 names and 15 s at 4,000; the pass is now O((n + e) log n + r) for `n` bytes, `e` errors and `r` bytes of repairs. The repairs themselves are unchanged.
- **The lexer does less work per word and per file** (#1894). A keyword is looked up by a `match` on the word instead of a scan of the table with a comparison per keyword; a lowercase word is read as a slice of the text instead of a copy; the names a file uses are collected only when a reserved word needs a rename; and the repairs of `_` names read the file's lines only when there is such an error. Tokens, errors and repairs are unchanged (tests pin the keyword table and that the standard modules take the path without the collection). `una check` of a small edition 0.2 package retires 2.0 million fewer instructions of 48.4 million (4.1%); `una check examples/hello` 1.1 million of 40.0 million (2.7%). The standard modules, lexed on every check, take most of it.
- **The command line is parsed once per `una` process** (#1894). Building the command line parser twice was a fixed cost of every `una` invocation: about 0.65 million instructions of the 40.8 million that `una check` of a small package retires (1.6%). A command line that does not parse is parsed again to report its error, as before, and a check with no obligation still starts no solver child, which a test now pins.
- **The proof cache key's owner check reads the effective user directly** (#1894). The check that a key file belongs to the user (#1164) read the user by creating and removing a probe file in the temporary directory on every command that verifies; it now reads it through the toolchain's one binding of `geteuid`, which is the user that owns a file the process creates (a test keeps the probe and checks that both agree). The rule is unchanged: a key file owned by anyone else is refused. `una check` of a small package retires about 0.85 million fewer instructions of 48.4 million (1.8%), and no longer writes to the temporary directory.
- **The optional standard modules are parsed only by the checks that take them** (#1894). `std.collections`, `std.limit` and the map module of edition 0.5 were parsed on every check of every edition and then given to the checker only when the package's edition and imports asked for them; now they are parsed only then, which no check of edition 0.1 to 0.3 does. The checker's result is unchanged: it received the same modules before. A test pins which modules each load parses. The cost of loading the standard modules is linear in the text parsed, a third less for editions 0.1 to 0.3. `una check` of a small edition 0.2 package retires 1.4 million fewer instructions of 48.5 million (2.9%); `una check examples/hello` 1.5 million of 40.0 million (3.7%).

### The solver, the runtime and the linker inside `una`

- **`una-sys`, the crate of the runtime's unsafe code** (#943, ADR 0116 rule 18 and Appendix A). It holds the attention words that check points read and the handlers of SIGINT and SIGTERM, which record a host stop with atomic adds, wake waiting threads through a non-blocking pipe, and at the third request restore the default actions; nothing uses them yet. It reaches the operating system through the `libc` crate, Rust bindings only, allowed for `una-sys` alone.
- **Withdrawn: the release backend that emits C** (#1099, ADR 0106 amendment of 2026-10-31). No C compiler goes into `una` and no C is emitted into executables (ADR 0124). `una build --release` was never implemented, so no shipped behavior changes. Release builds become Una's own optimizing code generation over Core IR (#1305); the amendment keeps the two build profiles, the rules that no optimization removes or weakens a check and that a wrong proof stays a wrong value, equivalence with the interpreter, the Gate 2 parity targets and the measurement that optimizing backends run checked kernels 6x to 27x faster than Cranelift, and lists the requirements and open questions of the new design. Until then, `una build` (Cranelift) is the native backend.
- **Own cryptography for TLS, slice 1** (#1100, ADR 0133, Proposed). A new crate, `una-crypto`, pure Rust with `unsafe` forbidden: SHA-256/384/512, HMAC, HKDF, ChaCha20-Poly1305 and X25519 in constant time, wiped on drop, checked against the RFC vectors, the vendored Wycheproof vectors (pinned by digest) and `ring` and `curve25519-dalek` as development references. `rustls` still uses `ring`; the provider switches in a later slice.
- **No unused system library in `una` or in a native executable** (#1103). On macOS, `una` no longer carries a load command for `/usr/lib/libiconv.2.dylib`, which the libc crate's link directive added although no code calls iconv: `una` is linked with `-Wl,-dead_strip_dylibs`, and `una build` passes the same flag on macOS and `-Wl,--as-needed` before its system libraries on Linux. `scripts/load-commands.sh` checks the release `una` and a program it links in `scripts/local-ci.sh` and in the `build` release gate, and fails on any load command, dylib, `NEEDED` entry or interpreter that `scripts/load-commands.txt` does not list.
- **The solver's release gates, and an installer that downloads nothing but `una`'s archive** (#1206, ADR 0124 rules 11, 18, 19, 31 and 32, the sixth slice of step 1, `docs/release-gates.md`): the gates `solver-identity` (the solver child against the standalone `z3` built from the same library, byte for byte, and against the Z3 project's release by status), `limits` (memout, cpu and wallclock each end as their reason) and `toolchain-free` (the release `una` alone checks and tests a package under a sandbox that starts no other program, and in a bare container); `scripts/z3-library.sh` gives every gate and `scripts/local-ci.sh` the cached library; `scripts/release.sh` embeds the library whose digest `supply-chain/z3.lock` records for the target and links the C++ runtime statically on Linux; `scripts/check-release.sh` refuses a development build and a build with the solver's test hooks; `install.sh` no longer downloads Z3 and needs no `unzip`.
- **The solver's memory limit, admission and record** (#1206, ADR 0124 rules 6, 10 and 28, `spec/12 §5`, `§9`, `spec/13 §5`, `§6`, the fifth slice of step 1): every command that takes `--solver-wallclock` takes `--solver-memory <MiB>` (256 to 1,048,576, default 4096), the limit of each solver child; the number of children running at once is bounded by the processors and by half the machine's (or the control group's) memory over the per-child limit, so a query's outcome never depends on how many run beside it; the proof cache's key covers the solver library's digest, so caches are verified once again after `una` changes its solver; the report's `tcb` gains `solver_build`, `solver_confinement` and `solver_outer_denied`; ADR 0003 gains the amendment of ADR 0124, and `docs/security/tcb.md` §3.2 records the confined solver.
- **The solver inside `una`** (#1206, ADR 0124 rules 3 to 8, 10 and 11, `spec/12 §5`, the fourth slice of step 1): `una check` runs each query in `una` itself, re-executed as `una __solver`, which confines itself (descriptors, limits, the seccomp filter on Linux or the sandbox profile on macOS), writes the handshake `una-solver 1 z3 <version> <build>` and runs the Z3 front end built into `una`; no `z3` on `PATH` is ever run, and the lock's pin is compared with the built-in version with no process started. A proof needs the handshake, a normal exit with status 0 and `unsat`; a child past its processor time, past its memory, or crashed is `unknown` with reason `cpu`, `memout` or `crash`, never cached; a child killed by its confinement is U5003. `scripts/local-ci.sh` builds the Z3 library of `supply-chain/z3.lock` once and names it by `UNA_Z3_LIB`; the toolchain's own tests use a stand-in solver child under the feature `test-hooks`.
- **The crate `una-z3`, and the development-build line** (#1206, ADR 0124 rules 2, 19, 20 and 35, the second slice of step 1): `una-z3` holds the call into the Z3 front end built by `scripts/build-z3.sh`, linked by its build script from `UNA_Z3_LIB` (development) or `UNA_EMBED_Z3` with its checked digest (release), and runs it only in a process that recorded its confinement, which `una-sys` keeps behind the feature `solver`; `una --version` ends with `development build`, and its document gains the key `build`, in a build without the release solver; `una` carries a build record with the solver's pin and library digest; ADR 0058 gains the amendment of ADR 0124.
- **Z3's supply chain** (#1206, ADR 0124 rule 1, the first slice of step 1): `supply-chain/z3.lock` pins Z3 5.1.0 by the release tag's commit, its tree and the SHA-256 of a source tarball made from that tree, mirrored as a release asset of this repository; `supply-chain/z3/` holds the recipe, the CMake settings of Z3's own release build with no GMP and no bindings, and a patch that also builds the SMT-LIB front end as `libz3shell.a` with its entry renamed; `scripts/build-z3.sh` checks the digest before extracting anything, builds the library and the standalone `z3` from the same objects into `target/z3/<digest>/` with a build record, and with `--reproducible` shows two cold builds equal; the Linux check image gains cmake, g++ and make.
- **Release archives without `lib/`** (#1207, ADR 0059's amendment): the archive holds `una`, `LICENSE`, `README.md` and the guides; `scripts/check-release.sh` expects exactly those, refuses a `lib/` entry, scans the embedded runtime once its digest is the packed `una`'s, and builds `examples/hello` with `UNA_RT_LIB` and runtime files beside `una` that must change nothing; `install.sh` installs no `lib/` from such an archive, still installs the `lib/libuna_rt.a` of an earlier release, keeps an earlier installation's runtime in its record for uninstall, and refuses an archive with any other entry.
- **The runtime inside `una`** (#1207, ADR 0124 rules 12, 14, 15, 18 to 20 and 28, step 2, `docs/plan/0124-step2-design.md`): `scripts/release.sh` builds `libuna_rt.a` first, strips its debug sections and builds `una` with it embedded by digest (`UNA_EMBED_RUNTIME`, `UNA_EMBED_RUNTIME_SHA256`, checked by `crates/una-cli/build.rs`); a release `una build` links that runtime, checks it against its digest before every link (U8001, exit code 3, when it is missing or damaged) and reads no `UNA_RT_LIB` and no `lib/`, so a runtime older than `una` can no longer fail the link (#203); until step 3 it writes the runtime for `cc` into the link's private directory (mode 0700) as a new file of mode 0600, removed with it; development builds keep the search of ADR 0059 rule 2; `una --version` says `development build` without the embedded runtime too; the build record gains `runtime_sha256`, and the report `native.runtime_sha256` (`spec/13 §6`, `spec/16 §2` rule 8).
- **`una build` links by itself on macOS arm64** (#1208, ADR 0124 step 3, `spec/16 §6`). The new crate `una-link`, in safe Rust, links the program's object and the runtime built into `una` into a Mach-O executable for macOS 13 and later that loads `/usr/lib/libSystem.B.dylib` alone, with chained fixups bound at load, `__unwind_info`, dead code removed, no debug information, a deterministic `LC_UUID` and an ad-hoc signature with the hardened runtime, so macOS refuses injected libraries and debuggers; `una build` runs no `cc` there, and every output is read back by the linker's verifier. `una build --debuggable` lets a debugger attach. A refusal of Una's own inputs is U8001, and an executable too large to lay out is the new U7015. The report's `native` section gains `linker` and `debuggable`. Linux keeps the system linker until step 4.
- **ELF relocation engine for Linux, step 4b of the internal linker** (#1209, ADR 0124 rules 24 and 33). `una-link` gains a reader of ELF relocatable objects for `x86_64-unknown-linux-musl` and `aarch64-unknown-linux-musl` and the relocation engine of rule 24: every supported type of both targets, the x86-64 GOT relaxations, the thread-local relaxations to the local-exec model (general- and local-dynamic through `PLT32` or the GOT, initial-exec, arm64 descriptors), relative fixups for 64-bit pointers in writable sections, and the refusals of rule 24 (narrow absolute and text relocations, PC-relative references a position-independent executable cannot make, places outside their section or overlapping, values that do not fit, branches beyond their reach as U7015), with bounded reads of any input. The arm64 types no input uses and `ld.lld` does not apply (the checked `TLSLE_*_TPREL_LO12` forms and the tiny-model `TLSDESC_LD_PREL19`/`ADR_PREL21`) are left out of the list, which the musl scan now shares. Each type is checked byte for byte against `ld.lld` in `cargo test` on macOS, and against GNU `ld` by `scripts/link-oracle-elf.sh`; every relocation of the toolchain's musl inputs and standard library is applied in a test; a new fuzz target, `elf_link`. Nothing is linked with it yet: `una build` on Linux still links through `cc` until the layout of the next slice.
- **musl inputs for Linux, step 4a of the internal linker** (#1209, ADR 0124 rules 12, 16, 20 and 24). `rust-toolchain.toml` installs the `x86_64-unknown-linux-musl` and `aarch64-unknown-linux-musl` targets, and the local-ci image carries them. On Linux, `scripts/release.sh` takes musl's `libc.a`, `rcrt1.o`, `crti.o`, `crtn.o`, `crtbeginS.o`, `crtendS.o` and `libunwind.a` from the pinned toolchain's `self-contained` directory of the musl target of the machine's architecture, refuses any whose digest differs from the new `supply-chain/musl.lock`, prints each digest (`musl: <target>: <file> sha256 <digest>`), and `una`'s build script checks them against the lock and the target's architecture and embeds them, for that target only (`scripts/check-release.sh` checks the packed `una` records the pinned digests); the build record gains `musl_sha256s` and `libunwind_sha256`, empty on macOS and in a development build; the report does not list them yet (rule 28, later in step 4). A Linux `una` grows by about 6.8 MB. A Linux release checks them against their digests before each link, as it checks the runtime. Cranelift compiles for the musl triple on Linux, which changes no byte of the objects it writes (a test pins it). `scripts/scan-musl.sh TARGET` scans those inputs and the standard library of the target through `una-link`'s new ELF reader: no `R_*_IRELATIVE` or indirect function, every relocation type in a loaded section in rule 24's list, the position-independence restrictions, no common symbol in the standard library, the archives' symbol tables both ways, relocation sections the reader would skip, and musl's version (1.2.5 in Rust 1.98.1). `una build` still links Linux executables through `cc`, unchanged; the internal linker of Linux comes in the next slices. The getting-started guide notes the name-resolution limit of musl executables.
- **Release checks with the solver built into `una`** (#1277). The C math library check (#1092) and the load-command check (#1103) keep their full strength on what a Una program contains, the native runtime `libuna_rt.a` and every executable that `una build` links, and now also on `una` built without the solver, which `scripts/no-libm-build.sh` builds for the check so that the interpreter's code is held to the rule on its own. The release `una`, which holds Z3 (ADR 0124), is checked against two explicit lists of its own: `scripts/no-libm-una.txt`, the math functions Z3 imports in its search heuristics (`exp`, `exp2`, `log`, `log2`, `pow`), each of which must also be imported by the Z3 library `una` was linked with, and `scripts/load-commands-una.txt`, the C++ runtime it loads (`/usr/lib/libc++.1.dylib` on macOS; glibc's `libm.so.6` on Linux, not yet observed). Any other name or load command still fails. New canaries, `scripts/no-libm-test.sh` and `scripts/load-commands-test.sh`, link small programs that must fail each check in every scope, and run before the checks in the `build` gate and in `scripts/local-ci.sh`. `docs/spec/29-numerics.md` §6 rule 5 carries a dated note on the scope.
- **The macOS Z3 library is reproducible** (#1277). Two builds of the same objects gave two libraries because the archive's member headers carried each object's time, owner and group; `scripts/build-z3.sh` now builds with `ZERO_AR_DATE=1`, and two cold builds give one digest, which `supply-chain/z3.lock` records for `aarch64-apple-darwin`.
- **A fresh checkout builds and passes its tests with one setup command** (#1288, #1287, #1278, ADR 0124, `docs/compiler/testing.md` §3). `eval "$(scripts/z3-library.sh --export)"` builds the Z3 library into the cache when it has none for this machine's toolchain and sets `UNA_Z3_LIB`, which the testing guide now names; a build of `una-z3` without it warns that tier-1 tests will fail with U5003. The solver child's handshake window and deadline now start at the spawn, after the parent has resolved what it needs, and the toolchain's own test harness runs its `una` once before its first solver child, so the operating system's first launch of a newly linked `una` (more than a second for the debug build on macOS) no longer falls inside the 2-second window of that child; a shipped `una` re-executes the file it already runs from and never paid it. The determinism test of the JSON documents compares runs that all read a warm proof cache, and a new test pins that a cold and a warm report differ only in `obligations.cached` and `tcb.solver_confinement`.
- **Records read without emitted C** (#1309, #1311): ADR 0112 (check elision) gains a dated amendment that restates where elision happens, how the present checks are read back and its C-based reasons for Una's own release path (ADR 0106's amendment of 2026-10-31, RP-1 to RP-10), with the same rules on which checks may be elided and `--no-elision` unchanged; ADRs 0107, 0108, 0109, 0113, 0114, 0120 and 0124 gain dated notes that point their C passages at that amendment; `docs/plan/c-emission-inventory.md` lists every such passage; `docs/security/tcb.md` §5 now describes the five performance decisions as accepted. No rule and no behavior changes.
- **The solver child proves under a compliant outer sandbox on macOS** (#1360, ADR 0124 rule 7, Question 6): `sandbox_init`, refused under an outer sandbox, writes its own line on stderr, and a proof that ends with exit status 1 requires nothing on stderr, so every query ended as `unknown (crash)`; the child now catches that line on a pipe of its own during the call, so its stderr stays empty and the proof condition is unchanged.
- **On Linux, the solver child's filter refuses opening a file with `EACCES`** (#1455, ADR 0124 rule 7, probe P6). glibc 2.39 reads `/sys/devices/system/cpu/online` again each time it counts processors, during the query, so the filter's tripwire on `openat` killed the solver child (U5003, "the solver child broke its confinement (SIGSYS)") and every verifying run failed on such a system. `open`, `openat` and `openat2` now fail with `EACCES` without running, as rule 7 provides, and glibc, refused `/sys/devices/system/cpu/online` and then `/proc/stat`, falls back to `sched_getaffinity`; a traced `una check examples/events` on arm64 Linux shows those two opens as the only refused calls of its 11 solver children. No call became allowed: `creat` and every other tripwire still kill, and the child still opens nothing after the filter. New tests run the filter's program over every call number and open a real file under the filter. macOS is unchanged.
- **The debug runtime's memory budget no longer charges the slots its quarantine adds to the slot table** (#1506, ADR 0061). A debug `una-rt` reuses a freed slot only after 65,536 others were freed. Its slot table therefore grew about 5 MiB past a release build's, which made native tests with two such heaps under 16 MiB flaky with U6003. The budget now credits the freed slots past a heap's peak of live objects, only in table capacity above twice what a release build's table ever needed, and at most a quarter of the budget in total. Live slots are never credited, and neither is capacity a release build's table may hold. The hard ceiling of twice the budget stays on the bytes really held. Release builds are unchanged.
- **The solver child's handshake window is 10 seconds** (#1791, ADR 0124 rule 4, amendment of 2026-11-09 accepted on 2026-11-10, `spec/12 §5` rule 6). A loaded machine missed the 2 seconds; a late or missing handshake is still U5003, never a proof, and the window stays inside the wall-clock guard. The processor-time scaling test of `systems` overflow obligations no longer runs alone.
- **The documentation says that the solver is built into `una`** (#1820, ADR 0124). `una explain U5003`, the README, Getting started, Una for agents, the language guide and the `verification.lock` page no longer tell you to install Z3 or put it on `PATH`: the release `una` carries Z3 and never looks for a `z3` program. U5003 now explains its four causes: a lock that pins another Z3 version, a development build with no solver built in, a solver that could not be confined (such as one run as root, with the workaround), and a failed handshake. Building from source now runs `eval "$(scripts/z3-library.sh --export)"` first, without which the `una` it builds has no solver. The pages also say that on macOS arm64 `una build` links with its own linker and needs no `cc`, that the installer needs no `unzip`, and, on the known-limits page, which foreign code the `una` binary holds.
- **`una build` with a release archive on macOS arm64** (#1833, ADR 0124 rules 12 and 25). The release runtime is stripped with Apple's `strip -S`, which rewrites each object through `ld -r`: `__TEXT,__eh_frame` becomes S_REGULAR and each FDE's CIE pointer a SUBTRACTOR/UNSIGNED pair of two local symbols of the section. The internal linker refused the section (U8001), so `una build` failed with every packed `una`. It now knows `__TEXT,__eh_frame` by its name, as ld64 does, and reads a CIE pointer pair, which must name two local symbols of the section; any other relocation there stays U8001. The oracle test links a release-built Rust program that unwinds after `xcrun strip -S`, against Apple's `ld`.
- **Z3 library pin for aarch64-unknown-linux-gnu** (#1901, ADR 0124 rule 1). `supply-chain/z3.lock` records the digest of `libz3shell.a` built in the linux/arm64 image of `scripts/local-ci/Dockerfile`, shown reproducible by cold builds in separate fresh volumes with equal digests, so `scripts/release.sh aarch64-unknown-linux-gnu` can take the library by digest; a test keeps a `[library]` line for every target that embeds it.
- **The Linux containers get the Z3 library built into una** (#1902). `scripts/local-ci.sh` and `scripts/hunt-linux.sh` mount the local copy of the pinned Z3 tarball that `UNA_Z3_SOURCE` names read-only into their containers (`scripts/z3-source.sh`, which refuses a value that is not a readable file); the build container of `scripts/hunt-linux.sh` mounts the Z3 cache volume and builds `una` with `UNA_Z3_LIB`, so the hunt gate checks with the solver; `scripts/local-release.sh` mounts the cache volume read-only with `UNA_Z3_CACHE` for `scripts/release.sh` and no longer installs a standalone Z3, which no step of it used. A missing library is an error in each.
- **una-z3 links Z3 from a verified copy** (#1925). The build script hashed the Z3 library at the path that `UNA_EMBED_Z3` or `UNA_Z3_LIB` names and then linked it by its directory, so the linker read the file again after the digest check, and searched that directory first for the C++ runtime: a `libstdc++.a` (Linux) or `libc++.a` (macOS) planted beside the pinned library was linked unverified while the library's digest still matched. The build script now copies the library into a directory of its own output that holds the copy alone, hashes and checks the copy, makes it read-only, and names only that directory to the linker. The digest check, its messages, the two variables and the C++ runtime directives of both targets are unchanged. Tests run the build script's code on temporary directories: a library beside the given one is never in the link's search, a change of the given file after the check never reaches the link, and what an earlier build left in the directory is removed; reverting to the old behavior fails them. On macOS a planted `libc++.a` beside the library broke the link before and is ignored now.
- **The Linux `una` no longer needs `libstdc++.so.6`** (#1956, ADR 0124 rule 1). una-z3's build script asked for `libstdc++` as a shared library, and the release flag `-static-libstdc++` does not reach an explicit `-lstdc++`, so the aarch64 Linux `una` of `scripts/release.sh` needed the system's `libstdc++.so.6` and `scripts/load-commands.sh` refused it. The C++ runtime of the solver is now linked statically on Linux (`static:-bundle=stdc++`), and the shipped `una` depends dynamically on glibc and on GCC's unwinder `libgcc_s.so.1`, which every Rust program for Linux on glibc loads, as before. Every Linux build of `una`, a development build included, now needs the toolchain's static `libstdc++.a` (on Fedora and RHEL, the package `libstdc++-static`). `scripts/load-commands-test.sh` gains two Linux canaries: a dynamic `libstdc++` fails as `una`, a static one passes. macOS is unchanged.

### Release gates, the installer and the tests

- **The release gate checks the Learn pages for neutrality, and handles its patterns more strictly** (#789). The `site` gate checks every text file of Una by Example, English or translated, against the neutrality pattern with `grep -E`, naming any match by path only, and a run without the pattern stops unless `UNA_GATES_UNCHECKED=1` marks it as not for publication. `scripts/release-gates.sh` reads the neutrality pattern from `UNA_NEUTRALITY_PATTERN` or the Keychain item `una-neutrality-pattern`, as it reads the privacy pattern. Both patterns now reach the steps that apply them on a pipe, never in an environment or an argument. Each must be a valid extended regular expression that matches a fixed canary (`docs/release-gates.md` §3), so a pattern that matches nothing stops the run. The script refuses to run under a trace, and a run without either pattern says in its summary that it is unchecked and is never published. A pattern that grep cannot apply now fails the docs bundle instead of marking every file neutral.
- **The security hunt's family W, tasks and time** (#973, ADR 0117 S8). Generators for `within`, `first of`, `tasks` and `std.limit` over code whose work a secret decides, edition 0.4: accepted forms that keep every timing outcome at the secret's label, and closed calls that miss it, which a correct checker rejects. They are kept apart from the release gate's fixed run (seed 767, unchanged) and reached by `python3 -m hunt gen --ops W`, for the oracle under the cost clock.
- **The security hunt's timing run** (#973, ADR 0117 S8). `python3 -B -m hunt timing` runs family W through must-reject and a new mode of the two-run oracle under the cost clock (`una run --virtual-time --cost-clock`): a secret that decides how much work a `within`, a `first of` or a `limiter`'s statement does, and a timing label that misses it, now show as a difference of values. A canary must leak under the cost clock and stay clean on the plain virtual clock. The release gate's fixed run is unchanged.
- **Release scripts: no override of the random source, and no test hooks in a release** (#1015). `scripts/release.sh` refuses build flags, in the environment or a cargo configuration file, that set `getrandom_backend`, which would replace the operating system's random source in the shipped `una` and `libuna_rt.a`. `scripts/check-release.sh` fails when a shipped `una` or `libuna_rt.a` holds the marker of keyed hashing's test-only hooks, by `nm` or by its bytes.
- **The release gates undo the clone's release tag** (#1017). The `release` and `reproducible` gates of `scripts/release-gates.sh` make the tag `v<version>` in their clone for `scripts/local-release.sh` and now undo it when the gate ends, however it ends (a tag the clone had from its source is put back), so a later `mac`, `linux` or `linux-arm64` run on the same `OUT`, in a run split into groups, no longer sees the version as released; a tag left by a killed run is undone before the next run's first gate. `scripts/release-gates-tag.sh` changes only the clone that the `clone` gate made under `OUT`, checked by a marker, never another repository or the remote, and `scripts/release-gates-tag-test.sh` checks it on temporary repositories. One run at a time holds an `OUT` (`OUT/release-gates.lock`, exit code 2 for a second run), `OUT` is compared by its physical path, so a spelling through a link is the same `OUT`, and the gates unset `GIT_CONFIG_PARAMETERS`, `GIT_CONFIG_COUNT` and its keys with the other git variables. An `OUT` cloned before this change needs the `clone` gate again.
- **The site gate checks the Learn book with the una it pins** (#1039). `scripts/site-website.sh` checks Una by Example with this checkout's `una` while the release the book's `una.lock` pins is being prepared, and once it is published, or when the versions differ, with that published release, whose archive's SHA-256 must be the one its validation record gives and whose `una --version` must name the pinned version (`scripts/site-learn-una.sh`, with a self-test the gate runs). The release gate requires the two versions to be equal, so the book is re-released for a new una before the tag.
- **The release gates pin their Docker backend** (#1082, `docs/release-gates.md` §3). `scripts/release-gates.sh` refuses, before any gate, to run a gate that uses Docker (`linux`, `linux-arm64`, `release`, `reproducible`, `hunt-linux-arm64`) unless `DOCKER_CONTEXT`, or else the active context, is Docker Desktop's `desktop-linux` and `DOCKER_HOST` is unset, so that another container runtime that holds the active context never runs a gate. It exports the context for every gate, and the record names the context and the Docker server's version.
- **Release check: no C math library symbol in what ships** (#1092). num-bigint is now a dependency without its default `std` feature, whose code called the C math library (`log2` while parsing a big `Int`, and `ln`, `exp` and `cbrt` in its integer roots); its other code is integer-only, and no value changes. (Superseded by #1093, below: no workspace crate depends on num-bigint any more, and the check asserts that the release graph holds none of num-bigint, num-integer and num-traits.) `scripts/no-libm-build.sh` links, with a release `una`, every runnable program of `tests/conformance/runtime` that the native backend compiles, the new conformance package `tests/conformance/runtime/no_libm_run` among them, which uses big `Int`, `F64`, text, lists and maps. `scripts/no-libm.sh` then asserts that the release dependency graph enables no `std` feature of num-bigint, num-integer and num-traits, and fails on any name of `scripts/no-libm.txt`, the C math library's functions with their `f` and `l` variants, that the release `una`, the release `libuna_rt.a` or one of those executables leaves undefined (`nm -u`), with no exception. It reads release artifacts only, built for the check, since a test build unifies the features of dev-dependencies in. It fails closed, runs in `scripts/local-ci.sh` after the tests and in the release gates' `build` gate, and on macOS needs the pinned toolchain's `llvm-tools` component.
- **Scaling tests that machine load cannot fail** (#1197, #1460). The scaling tests of the checks of calls into a boundary, of dead failure handling, of the charge of installations and of `L_lim` now count their work first: a test-only counter, `pass_steps`, behind `una-core`'s feature `counters` that only tests enable, must grow at most 1.1 times as fast as the size, which a quadratic pass doubles at every doubling; a wall-clock check over 16 times the size, against a bound of 32 times, stays as a coarse backstop. Every wall-clock scaling test of `una-cli`, `una-check`, `una-link` and `una-project` that uses the shared helper `tests/common/timing.rs` now retries a missed bound in up to five interleaved rounds, each size run as often as makes up one run of the largest, keeping the shortest run of each size; the per-doubling bounds are unchanged. A release build counts nothing.
- **Release check: the FMA disassembly gate** (#1216, ADR 0114 Appendix C rules 7 and 8, as ADR 0120 Appendix A amends them): `scripts/check-release.sh` runs `scripts/check-fma.sh`, which fails on any FMA instruction in a shipped x86-64 `una` or `libuna_rt.a` outside the dispatched kernel variants of the allowlist the build report records, and reports the scan as not applicable on AArch64.
- **Release check: the FMA gate reads Mach-O** (#1356): `scripts/check-fma.sh` takes the architecture of a Mach-O file or archive from `otool -hv`, since the `objdump -f` of LLVM refuses Mach-O, refuses a universal file, and gains Mach-O canaries, so `scripts/check-release.sh` passes on macOS.
- **Release gates: the toolchain-free gate runs the released una** (#1357): it unpacks the `una` of the release gate's macOS and Linux arm64 archives, checked against their digests, instead of a development build that `scripts/toolchain-free.sh` refuses.
- **The unused release workflow matches ADR 0124 step 2** (#1358): `.github/workflows/release.yml` says at its top that GitHub Actions is not used, and no longer names `lib/libuna_rt.a` or a Z3 on PATH.
- **Release scripts: the review findings of the embedded runtime** (#1359): `install.sh` refuses links and entries outside the archive's directory; `scripts/check-release.sh` lists dotfiles, checks every entry's place and type, and checks that the embedded runtime has no debug section; `scripts/release.sh` strips by `xcrun` on macOS and the validation record and summary name the strip; `una build` removes private directories over an hour old that an interrupted build of the same user left, and compares the runtime's name without regard to case.
- **The Linux local-ci container runs as an unprivileged user** (#1394). The image of `scripts/local-ci/` runs as uid 1000, never as root, since the solver child refuses root (ADR 0124 rule 7) and the abi tests failed with U5003 in the container; `scripts/container-volumes.sh` hands the named volumes that a root container made before to that user, and `scripts/local-ci.sh`, `scripts/hunt-linux.sh` and `scripts/local-release.sh` run it first. The solver's refusal is unchanged.
- **A fuzz smoke run** (#1430, `docs/release-gates.md`): `scripts/fuzz-smoke.sh` runs the canary and every fuzz target of `fuzz/` at a twentieth of the `sanitize` gate's counts (at least 2,000 inputs), with the gate's flags, seed and checks, for the `fuzzing` check. The targets, counts and floors move to `scripts/fuzz-targets.sh`, which `scripts/sanitize.sh` sources unchanged in what it runs. `fuzz/Cargo.lock` records the path dependency of `una-http` on `una-hash`, without which `--locked` refused to build the targets.
- **The `sanitize` gate's Miri count** (#1432): `scripts/sanitize.sh` states the 19 mentions of `miri` that `una-rt` holds since the timing wheel moved to `una-http` (#1006), not 24, and its prose names the eighteen tests that run smaller under Miri; the gate would otherwise fail at that check.
- **Test runs use `cargo nextest`** (#1649). `.config/nextest.toml` runs the tests of all binaries side by side, builds the runtime library once before them, and gives each timing or scaling test the whole machine for itself; a test that links natively no longer rebuilds the library in its own process. `cargo test --workspace --locked` still runs the same tests, and no test is skipped or changed in what it asserts.
- **Each case of the five corpus tests is its own test** (#1652).
