The five tests that looped over a whole corpus run one test per case: `case::<name>` for each conformance case, `interpreter::<name>` and `linkers::<name>` for each runnable program of the native differential, `determinism::<name>` for each target of the determinism test and `core_ir::<name>` for each case of the Core IR export test, so `cargo nextest` spreads them over all cores. The lists are generated at build time from the corpus by the dev-only crate `una-test-cases`, and a test of each file fails when its list differs from the cases on disk. No case is dropped and no assertion is relaxed.
- **Ten scaling tests measure processor time and run beside the others** (#1656). The collection, text, list and JSON scaling tests of `una run` and of native executables that took the longest in the gate time the run of `una` or of the built executable by the user and system time of the children the test waited for (read with the POSIX `time -p` utility, which the Linux image of `scripts/local-ci/Dockerfile` now installs), not the wall clock, so other tests running beside them no longer move the measure. Each holds `cpu_time` in its name, which the timing pattern of `.config/nextest.toml` leaves out, so it no longer reserves every slot. Sizes, bounds and retries are unchanged.
- **Five more native scaling tests measure processor time** (#1662). The scaling tests of a matrix set through nested loops, a list appended through calls, a list built through function values, and text reads and slices in native code time the executable by its user and system time (`cpu::cpu_output`) and run beside the other tests; sizes, bounds, floors and retries are unchanged.
- **Twelve more scaling tests measure processor time** (#1662). The serve section, shape and overflow obligations, label inference, dense access, text append, search and contains, and list append tests read the user and system time of `una`, of the executable or of a child of the test binary (`cpu::in_child`) and run beside the other tests; sizes, bounds, floors and retries are unchanged, and the longest FFI test of `una-rt` starts early.
- **The language-server tests run alone under nextest** (#1689). The tests of the `lsp` binary wait for a solver handshake within two seconds, which failed once in a gate on a busy machine (`a_newer_change_cancels_tier_1_and_leaves_no_solver_behind`); like the timing tests, they now take every slot. No test is skipped or changed.
- **The exclusive timing tests run first under nextest** (#1695). A test that reserves every slot waits until the pool drains, so the timing tests now run first, one after another, and the other tests then fill every slot: the full run went from 1,720 s to 998 s on the gate machine, with the same 9,843 tests passing. No test is skipped or changed.
- **The tests build at opt-level 1** (#1697). The development profile of the per-PR gate builds at opt-level 1 with debug assertions and overflow checks on; the crates `una-rt` and `una-http` stay at opt-level 0, and the release gates, the Linux legs included, keep the unoptimized build (`CARGO_PROFILE_DEV_OPT_LEVEL=0` from `scripts/sanitize.sh`, `scripts/release-gates.sh` and `scripts/local-ci.sh`). The full nextest run went from 998 s to 778 s on the gate machine, with the same 9,843 tests passing.
- **Gate tiers: a fast layer per pull request and a nightly full suite** (#1701). The `tests` gate runs `scripts/gate-tests.sh` (the copy `origin/main` holds): a subset of the tests for documentation, corpus and test-only changes, the whole suite for every other change and for the security, soundness, performance and release workflows, and nightly on `main` (`docs/compiler/testing.md` section 8).
- **The `sanitize` gate's Miri bookkeeping** (#1703): `scripts/sanitize.sh` states the 23 mentions of `miri` that `una-rt` holds outside its ignore attributes, not 19, and its prose names the four added by the tests of the debug credit of the quarantine (#1506); three of those tests, which check only full-size tables, are now ignored under Miri by name rather than returning at once. `scripts/sanitize-miri-ignored.txt` lists the 114 tests Miri ignores, read from the source: the eight ignored since it was last updated and those three added, and one removed test dropped. The gate would otherwise fail at both checks. `docs/release-gates.md` says what the count pins, and `docs/security/tcb.md` states both numbers.
- **The installer: 0.4.0 or later** (#1819). The installer refuses a `UNA_VERSION` before 0.4.0, compared as numbers (0.10.0 is after 0.4.0), and anything but MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH: a release before 0.4.0 needs a separate Z3, which the installer no longer installs, so its `una check` could prove nothing. The README, Getting started and the known limits say so.
- **The reproducible and toolchain-free gates take every shipped archive** (#1903, ADR 0124 rule 31). Both read `scripts/release-targets.txt` through its fail-closed reader and fail on a missing archive or on one of a target the list does not name, `reproducible` still compares every archive of the first build, the record names the shipped targets and its verdict fails unless its archives are exactly one per shipped target, `scripts/local-release.sh` fails when the tag's list differs from its own, and tests show each refusal.
- **The release gates follow the shipped targets** (#1903, #1933, ADR 0124 rule 31). The required gates are those of the gate machine and of each target of `scripts/release-targets.txt`: the emulated `linux` gate is required only when x86_64 ships, a new `limits-linux-arm64` gate runs the limits and confinement cases natively on linux/arm64 (and `limits-linux` for x86_64, refused under emulation), the macOS `limits` gate also runs the confinement cases, `scripts/release-gates.sh` exits with its verdict (1 for FAIL, 3 for INCOMPLETE), and the docs bundle's manifest names the shipped targets (`targets`).
- **One list of shipped targets** (#1903, ADR 0124 rule 31). `scripts/release-targets.txt` lists the targets a release ships, `aarch64-apple-darwin` and `aarch64-unknown-linux-gnu` for 0.4.0; `scripts/local-release.sh` builds exactly their archives (no emulated x86_64 archive), `scripts/release.sh` and `scripts/check-release.sh` refuse any other target, and the tests of `supply-chain/z3.lock` and `install.sh` read the same list, failing closed on one that cannot be read or is empty.
- **The installer: Linux on arm64 with glibc 2.39 or later** (#1904). `install.sh` installs the `aarch64-unknown-linux-gnu` archive on Linux on arm64. Before anything is downloaded it refuses a musl system (Alpine and others), a glibc below 2.39 (compared as numbers), a system whose glibc version `getconf GNU_LIBC_VERSION` does not show, Linux on x86_64 (not shipped in this release), and every other system. After installing on Linux it says that `una build` needs a C compiler on `PATH` as `cc` (`una check`, `test` and `run` do not), that the executables it writes link the system's glibc dynamically, and that `una check` refuses to run as root, which it also says to root on macOS. Tests on fake machines show each case, that a refusal downloads nothing, and that the installer offers exactly the targets of `scripts/release-targets.txt`.
- **The hunt gate passes again on the current language: S2 names its function `probe`, the two-run oracle reads liveness and quiet operations from an example probe, and a guard in the test suite checks a sample of the generated programs** (#1911). The security hunt's S2 programs named their function `test`, a keyword from edition 0.3 (ADR 0105), so the gate refused them with U0101, a generator error; the function is `probe` now. Since S-41 and S-42 a run's trace leaves out every operation performed in quiet code and those of instances quiet code installed, so the oracle could no longer find any program live, nor see an operation under a secret branch: each variant now also runs once as an example (`una test --json`), whose trace keeps the operations of quiet code, and its events join the observers' views and the liveness view (`docs/plan/security-hunt-oracle.md` §12); the gate's `internal` canary installs its handler under a public program counter, the live forms are pinned again from a measured run (`H3:install-under-pc` is compared and not live, since no trace shows an operation of an instance quiet code installed), and the programs whose probe is unavailable are pinned by reason. A test of `una-cli` checks the first seeds of every operator in every edition the gate runs them in for an unexpected lexical or syntax code.
- **Tests that failed on Linux for reasons of their own now run as written there** (#1920). The poller's huge-timeout test re-arms its fired one-shot registration with a delete and then an add, as the reactors do, since `epoll` refuses a second add with `EEXIST`, and a new test pins that contract on both platforms; the wake pipe placement test holds its descriptors with `dup2` in the child, since `dash` takes no shell redirection above descriptor 9, so its check of a busy limit now runs on Debian and Ubuntu too; four tests of the native backend that shared a package directory with another test now have their own; and the `una-rt` test of a bit set the allocator cannot give runs in a process of its own, so its announced 2^59 bytes no longer end a test that runs a program beside it.
- **Docs samples check on the site** (#1923). The edition 0.4 example of the agent guide is a package with its `ita.una` and `ita.lock`, and the failing example of U7010 vendors a release of a name without an owner, so the site's check of the samples reads each as one package.
- **The Linux release checks its build image's toolchain** (#1926, #1958). `supply-chain/z3.lock` records the Debian package versions of the arm64 build image (binutils, cmake, make, GCC 13, libstdc++ and libgcc development files, glibc) and the SHA-256 of the files the linker puts into the Linux `una` from it (`libstdc++.a`, `libgcc.a`, `libgcc_eh.a`, the start-up objects and `libc_nonshared.a`). `scripts/release.sh` runs the new `scripts/check-toolchain.sh` before it builds `una` and refuses (exit 2) an image whose packages or files differ, so a rebuilt image cannot ship until the lock is updated deliberately. The lock's comment on the Linux Z3 build now names `scripts/build-z3.sh --source`.
- **Two tests of the nightly suite no longer depend on its environment** (#1932). The test of the signal handlers expects the wake pipe as high as the soft limit of open files allows, as under a job that launchd starts, and the test of a limiter whose deadline comes before its refill runs on a run clock that stays still, so a late thread no longer lets a token in.
- **The installer: the C library as the system runs it, a 64-bit userland, and root** (#1938). On Linux, `install.sh` reads the C library from the running system instead of from a file being there: a glibc system that also has musl's loader installed (Debian's `musl` package) now installs, and musl is still refused, by its `ldd --version`, by musl's loader when `getconf GNU_LIBC_VERSION` shows no glibc, and when the aarch64 loader `/lib/ld-linux-aarch64.so.1` that `una` needs is missing, is musl's (gcompat) or does not answer `--version` as glibc's. A 32-bit userland on a 64-bit kernel, which reports aarch64, is refused before any download (`getconf LONG_BIT` must be 64). As root, the installer refuses, before any download or removal, a `UNA_HOME` that another user could change: a symbolic link for `UNA_HOME`, a directory it writes in or a file it writes, and a directory there or above it that is not owned by root or is writable by its group or others (one above may be writable by others when it is sticky, as `/tmp` is); a file there that is not root's or that others can write; and a `TMPDIR` (default `/tmp`, which may be sticky) that another user could change, since the archive is checked and unpacked there. It works on the physical path, refuses when `id -u` cannot be read, and no longer runs the installed `una` as root. Only owners and mode bits are read, not ACLs. Every refusal is tested on fake machines, on `uname -m` both `aarch64` and `arm64`.
- **The limits legs check what they run** (#1939, ADR 0124 rule 31). `scripts/limits.sh` refuses to run unless the new `scripts/emulation.sh` finds the process native: besides the marker of `scripts/local-ci.sh` and Rosetta on macOS, it now detects on Linux an emulator that runs the shell as a `binfmt_misc` interpreter (Rosetta for Linux, QEMU's user mode), by the ELF machine of the shell's executable. Each of its two runs must pass exactly its count of cases (3 limits cases; 12 confinement cases on macOS, 8 on Linux), held to the test files by a test. `scripts/local-ci.sh --linux` runs the `linux/amd64` container natively, with no emulation marker, when the Docker engine is x86-64, so `limits-linux` can pass on such a host (not yet run on one). `docs/release-gates.md` states in §1 rule 2 that a tag needs every required gate, records the reading of rule 31 and its sign-off for the `linux` gate, and lists the cases of rule 31 that the limits gate does not run yet.
- **The timing oracles' programs check again, and stay checked** (#1947). The programs of two security timing oracles no longer passed the front end, so those oracles measured nothing: the residue program now binds `@env.get` with `let` and is in canonical form, and the phase program's `tasks` block ends with `unit`, its key now `internal` since a loop bound above `internal` in a program that starts tasks is U4002, the `secret` twin being checked as refused. The phase oracle's short computation is now `crunch(10000)`, about 1 ms, since `crunch(100000)` took longer than its 6 ms deadline on any schedule. A new test, run in the ordinary test run, checks every program the timing oracles build, run or check.
- **The C math library check reads cargo's "nothing to print"** (#1949). The pinned cargo reports a crate that `Cargo.lock` records but the release graph does not hold with `warning: nothing to print.`, an empty tree and exit status 0, which `scripts/no-libm.sh` read as the graph holding num-bigint, num-integer and num-traits, so the check and its canaries failed on every host. The verdict on each `cargo tree -i` run is now `scripts/no-libm-graph.sh`: a tree printed is still a finding, as is any other failure or an empty tree without that warning; only that warning with an empty tree, or a package ID specification that matches no package, is not. `scripts/no-libm-test.sh` gains graph canaries: each kind of result given as files, and the real graph, where `una-core` must be a finding and `num-bigint` must not.
- **The C math library check knows the solver's imports on Linux** (#1951). On aarch64 Linux the release `una` imports `frexp`, `frexpf`, `frexpl` and `remainder`, and `scripts/no-libm.sh` failed there. Each comes from the Z3 library built into `una` alone (`nm -A -u` of the library of `supply-chain/z3.lock`, and the GNU linker's trace of the link): the three `frexp` names from `std::__format::__formatter_fp<char>::format` of GCC's libstdc++ 13, a header template that six Z3 objects instantiate, and `remainder` from `hwf_manager::rem`, Z3's own floating-point numbers. `una` built without the solver, `libuna_rt.a` and all 126 executables of the check import none of them, nor does any Rust object of the build. macOS does not show them: Apple's libc++ formats through `std::to_chars` in its dylib, and ld64 drops the unreferenced `hwf_manager::rem` that GNU ld keeps with the rest of `hwf.cpp.o`. The four names join `scripts/no-libm-una.txt`, each still tied to the Z3 library's own imports and still rejected everywhere else, and `scripts/no-libm-test.sh` now checks every name of that list on its own in each scope.
- **`scripts/release.sh` builds with the embedded Z3 alone** (#1954). A development library named by `UNA_Z3_LIB`, which `scripts/local-ci.sh` exports for every step and `scripts/z3-library.sh --export` sets in a shell, made una-z3's build refuse the release build beside `UNA_EMBED_Z3`, so the local CI's pack step failed. The script now unsets `UNA_Z3_LIB` once it has chosen the library the lock pins: the release embeds only that library, by its digest, and still fails when no cache entry holds it. A test runs the script with a fake `cargo` and checks the build's environment, with and without the caller's `UNA_Z3_LIB`.
- **The hunt gate's floor on live programs is pinned again from measurement: 66** (#1916, decision D-37). The floor of 105 came from the trace channel that S-42 closed; on the current language at most the programs of the two live forms can be live, and two runs of the fixed run found exactly 66, the same programs. Every canary is caught, and the pinned live forms still fail the loss of either one. No other floor, count, canary or budget changes.

### Documentation and records

- **Guide: document the [contracts] dry-run lock** (#871). Document the conditional effect lock with executable dry-run and live-write examples.
- **Docs: installing, checking the version and updating** (#905). Getting started now says how to keep `~/.una/bin` on `PATH` in a shell profile, what `una --version` prints, what a release older than 0.3 answers instead, how `which -a una` finds an older copy, and that running the installer again updates it.
- **Docs: the guide links to the 19-chapter Learn book** (#933). The language and configuration pages link to the Learn section that teaches each construct, such as `/learn/information-flow/branches-and-failures-implicit-flows/`, under the new chapters and section titles.
- **Guide: executable recipes and edition-consistent availability** (#1107). Correct guide availability and proof claims, add units and generic-fold cases, and check positive examples against their declared editions.
- **Validate feature availability in documentation bundles** (#1109). Add a deterministic reviewed catalog tied to conformance packages and reject document editions absent from the bundling compiler.
- **Protocols and configuration files, proposed** (#1290, #1302, ADR 0130 and ADR 0131 Proposed): ADR 0130 lets a capability declare facts about the resources behind it, which its operations require, establish and invalidate, a failure making them unknown; the verifier tracks them step by step, across calls through frames in headers and across loop iterations, inside QF_UFLIA, with no run-time cost; B04's violation fails at build, and B05's batch form is left to a record on facts over list elements. ADR 0131 makes every configuration file a person writes for Una a `.una` file in Una's lexical syntax with a closed grammar, told from a module by its place and its first word, read by the one syntax crate; `quality.una` replaces `quality.toml` when its slice lands, through `una migrate`, and the interim TOML reader is accepted until then (with a dated amendment of ADR 0122). Nothing is implemented.
- **Guarantee diff and specification mutation, proposed** (#1291, #1293, ADR 0128 and ADR 0129 Proposed): `una diff` compares a package with a baseline snapshot and lists what changed in what a reader can trust (contracts by implication where the solver decides, otherwise `review_required` or `inconclusive`; authority, `trusted`, `checked`, declassifications, obligations, the solver pin, requirements), with approvals bound to one change in `guarantees.toml` and a strict `--gate`. Specification mutation testing reports the test mutation score and the verifier rejection rate apart, with their denominators, measures specification sensitivity, and adds solver-decided trivial-contract checks. Nothing is implemented.
- **Una Quality, proposed** (#1296, ADR 0127 Proposed, `spec/32` Draft): the foundation record of `una quality`, a deterministic report of measured quality with three gates (assurance, structural, evolution) that never compensate each other; `quality.toml` with a severity (`off`, `info`, `warn`, `block`) for every metric, presets, waivers and baselines; a status on every value; codes `UQ` plus four digits. The draft chapter defines every metric, the finding record, the documents, the exit codes and the first `UQ` codes with their explain texts. Nothing is implemented.
- **Records accepted** (#1399, #1398): the project accepted on 2026-11-02, with changes recorded in a dated "Decisions of acceptance" section of each, ADR 0127 (Una Quality), ADR 0128 (the guarantee diff), ADR 0129 (specification mutation testing), ADR 0130 (protocols and typestate, edition 0.5 preview), ADR 0131 (Una's own configuration files, with `guarantees.una` joining the family) and ADR 0133 (own cryptography for TLS: the answers to its eight questions, the exit criteria of slice 8 and the conditions of the switch); ADR 0123 gains the dated amendment of its slice S4 (the serve threads), with the fault policy of the serve engine (contain a fault to its connection, stop the run on a panic of a program callback, a panic with a grant lock held, or past a fixed threshold per run); the Draft chapter `spec/32` is aligned with the accepted records (approvals of UQ9101 to UQ9109 only from the guarantee diff's approvals file, UQ9201 with no severity, the body-only fingerprint, `high_assurance`, refuted and unknown counted apart, and Draft rows for UQ4102, UQ5401, UQ9105 to UQ9109 and UQ9302). Nothing is implemented, and no shipped behavior changes: `ring` stays the default TLS provider.
- **Known limits** (#1768): a guide page, `docs/guide/known-limits.md`, that states what this release does not do, or does only with limits, for edition `0.4`: servers (`spec/31`), tasks and timing (`spec/26`, `spec/27`), the editions of collections and numerics (ADR 0139) and the platforms of the installer, each item with the rule or record that states it.
- **The tasks and time guide** (#1803, `spec/19`, `spec/27 §5` to `§8`). `docs/guide/tasks-and-time.md` teaches `together` (edition 0.2) and `tasks`, `start`, `within` and `first of` (edition 0.4 preview): their forms, deadlines in milliseconds, what cancellation does to a task, how the interpreter and the executable schedule tasks, and the timing labels behind U4001, U4002 and U4003. Its code blocks check, and each output it shows is checked against `una run` in both engines, `una test` or `una check`.
- **Release v0.4.0 ships edition 0.4 as a preview; two amendments accepted** (#1897, #1791, #917): ADR 0124 rule 4 as amended on 2026-11-09 (the solver handshake window of 10 seconds, `spec/12 §5` rule 6) and ADR 0073 J5 as amended on 2026-11-09 (`Json` values that carry labeled data in edition 0.4) are accepted on 2026-11-10; ADR 0139 gains an amendment (accepted on 2026-11-13, next entry) by which v0.4.0 ships edition 0.4 as a preview under the terms of ADR 0121, the release that makes it stable named later, and `spec/13 §7`, the README, the edition guide page and the roadmap say so. No behavior changes.
- **Five amendments and a known limit recorded on 2026-11-13** (#1897, #898, #1152, #1903, #1883): ADR 0139's amendment of 2026-11-10, by which v0.4.0 ships edition 0.4 as a preview, is accepted, with the note it asks for appended to ADR 0121; ADR 0116's amendment of 2026-11-09 (labels on `Clock` readings) and ADR 0117's amendment of 2026-11-09 (pooled task stacks sized from a frame bound) are accepted; ADR 0055 gains an amendment by which a release ships the targets of `scripts/release-targets.txt` (`aarch64-apple-darwin` and `aarch64-unknown-linux-gnu` for v0.4.0), and ADR 0124 one that admits `libgcc_s.so.1` beside glibc as a library of the Linux `una`; the known-limits page states that v0.4.0 has no fuzz target for the HTTP request reader, response parser or chunked decoder. No behavior changes.
- **The specification defines the documents of `una test`, `una ita` and `una migrate`** (#1898). `spec/13 §5` gives the form of `una.test/2`, and `spec/30 §6` and `§8` open with a `una.ita/1` and a `una.migrate/1` document as this toolchain prints them; `una ita tree` gives `root` and `packages` in place of `written`; the Core export of a program with dependencies is named as not written by this release; and two message placeholders of `spec/31 §8` are written as code.
- **Assurance in this release** (#1899): the known-limits page says what v0.4.0 proves, tests, trusts and assumes, and what moved to R0.5; the TCB, README, SECURITY and guide pages say what the formal re-check refuses, that engine equivalence is tested, and which release lines get fixes.
- **CLI help and references match the toolchain** (#1899). `una run --help` and the usage message of a native executable say that `--serve-script` and `--serve-out` run only in the toolchain's own test builds, and `--grant` names the `serve:` grants and the limit suffixes of `http:`; the README, the syntax reference, the guide for agents, the grant and manifest pages, and `spec/10` and `spec/14` list the commands, flags, grants, standard modules and diagnostic ranges this toolchain has.
- **Itá registry and edition 0.4 guide wording** (#1899). The publishing, registry and offline pages and the roadmap say that the registry is designed but not served in this release: there is no https transport, registry packages are read only from a mirror directory, and `publish` and `yank` send nothing. Getting started gains a step that moves `wordfreq` to edition 0.4 with `una fix` and `una migrate`, and it and `una-compact.md` state the editions they cover. The `together` page scopes its timing and task sentences to editions 0.2 and 0.3, the example READMEs name Learn chapters by title and route instead of by number, known limits lists the security hunt's server run of #1011 as done, and the prose of the guides, the specification and these notes spells "canceled", keeping the trace value `cancelled`.
- **The guides say what Linux on arm64 needs** (#1905, ADR 0124 rule 1). The README, Getting started and the known limits name the targets of 0.4.0, macOS and Linux on arm64 (`scripts/release-targets.txt`), and say that Linux on x86-64 is not a target and Windows is not supported; that the Linux `una` needs glibc 2.39 or later, is dynamic against it with `libstdc++` and `libgcc` linked in, and does not run on musl systems such as Alpine; that `una check`, `una test` and `una run` need no other program while `una build` on Linux links through `cc`, writing an executable dynamic against the glibc of the machine that built it, with no static or musl executable yet; and that the solver refuses root there too. The foreign code inventory lists the GNU C++ runtime linked into the Linux `una`.

## v0.3.17

A patch of the Beta, with three security fixes, made from the v0.3.16 tag: no other change of main is in it. In the interpreter, a function value releases the values it closes over by the worklist that releases records, so a value that a loop nests deeply through function values no longer overflows the stack of `una run` when it is released (#1071, advisory UNA-SA-017). And `una-http`'s TLS trust store no longer holds the distrusted emSign root (#1130, RUSTSEC-2026-0381, RUSTSEC-2026-0382). And `una fmt`, `una fix`, `una build` and the proof cache no longer follow a symbolic link planted in the package (#1160, advisory UNA-SA-018). Validation: `docs/releases/0.3.17-validation.md`.

- **Function values release what they hold without recursion** (#1071, advisory UNA-SA-017). In the interpreter, a function value releases the values it closes over and the handlers of its capabilities by the same worklist as records, so a value that a loop nests deeply through function values ends normally. Before, releasing such a value recursed on the native stack, and a deep enough one aborted `una run` with a stack overflow, in every release up to v0.3.16. Native executables are not affected.
- **Security: the distrusted emSign root is out of the TLS trust store** (#1130, RUSTSEC-2026-0381, RUSTSEC-2026-0382). `una-http` updates `webpki-roots` from 1.0.9 to 1.0.11, which removes `emSign ECC Root CA - G3`, and `rustls-webpki` from 0.103.15 to 0.103.16, which rejects certificate paths through the emSign intermediates whose private keys are public. A TLS connection made by `una-http` no longer trusts a server certificate issued through them.
- **Security: writes under a package no longer follow a planted link** (#1160, advisory UNA-SA-018). `una fmt` and `una fix` used to write a temporary file at a fixed name next to the file they replace (`.<name>.fmt-tmp`), following a symbolic link at that name, in every release up to v0.3.16. A repository could commit such a link and have a formatting run overwrite a file outside it. The temporary file is now created exclusively, under a name unique to the write, then synced and renamed: for a replacement with mode 0600 until it takes the destination's permissions, and for a new file under the umask, as before. A source that is a symbolic link is still written through onto the file it resolves to, but only when that file and its directory are inside the package: a link that leads outside it is refused, naming where it leads. `una build` creates `target/una` one level at a time, refusing a `target` or `target/una` that leads outside the package, and refuses any link at the executable's name or at `<name>.o`. The linker now writes the executable, beside its object file, in a fresh private directory that `una build` creates inside `target/una` (or inside the directory `--out` names: a plain relative `--out` is rooted at the current directory and created one level at a time in the same way, an absolute one or one with `..` is created as given) and always removes; the executable is then checked to be a regular file there, synced and renamed into place, so a link planted at the final name while the linker runs is never written through. Before, it wrote the object with a write that follows a link, and the linker wrote the final path directly. A `target` deliberately linked to another volume now needs `--out` with an absolute path there, or one with `..`. The proof cache stores its entries the same way, without the fsync since a torn entry fails its tag and is solved again, stores nothing when `.una`, `.una/cache` or `.una/cache/proofs` is a link that leads outside the package, and replaces a link at an entry's name instead of following it, even one that leads inside the package. Hardening beside it (#1164): the proof cache key file is used only when it is a private regular file of the user: a regular file, not a symbolic link, with no other hard link, holding 16 to 4096 bytes, owned by the user, and its mode grants nothing to group or others. Any other existing key file now turns the proof cache off (the report's `tcb.proof_cache_key` is `null`) instead of being used. Each of these refusals (a link, not a regular file, another user's file, a mode that grants group or others anything, another hard link, a size outside 16 to 4096 bytes) prints one note on stderr naming the file and the reason, with a hint where one applies (`chmod 600` it for a mode that grants group or others anything); under `--json` stderr stays empty and the report's `null` is the only signal. A missing key file is made, with no note. The proof cache also reads an entry only from a regular file of at most 64 KiB.

## v0.3.16

A patch of the Beta, with one security fix. In every edition, `to_int` returns `None`, and from edition `0.2` `parse_int` fails with the new `ParseError` variant `TooLong`, for a text of more than 4300 digits, checked before any conversion: converting d digits took O(d²) time, so untrusted text could make one call quadratic, in every release up to v0.3.15 (#914, advisory UNA-SA-016). A `when` that names each `ParseError` variant without an `else` arm must now handle `TooLong`. Structural equality compares each pair of shared parts once (#815), and counterexamples show a sum value reached through a record field (#902). A `together` block checks its bookkeeping, its copies and its joins against the memory budget (#777, ADR 0113). Accepted records: ADRs 0105 to 0112, 0114, 0116 and 0117. The `hunt` gate runs on Linux, samples every family of edition `0.3` and compares diagnostics' messages (#793, #813, #818, #874). Validation: `docs/releases/0.3.16-validation.md`.

- **Accepted: profiles `app` and `systems`** (#22, ADR 0109). In edition `0.3`, `[package] profile` will be `"app"` (the default, today's language) or `"systems"`, which only adds obligations: `Int` arithmetic is proven to stay in a machine word, and the memory budget is declared. No profile removes a check; foreign code and SIMD are gated to `systems` by their own ADRs.
- **Accepted: no pipeline operator and no shorthand lambda; method calls already chain** (#35, ADR 0110). `|>` and an `it` shorthand for lambdas are not added: method-call syntax is Una's pipeline, `x.f(a: 1).g(b: 2)`, and a lambda names its parameters. The `|>` shape stays U0101 with the description of ADR 0096 rule 3, and the backlog row of #35 closes as decided. No language rule changes in any edition.
- **Accepted: arenas and index-based graphs** (#82, ADR 0111). In edition `0.3`, `Arena[T]` will hold values under opaque `Id[T]` ids that are unique per run, printable never and comparable only by `==`; reading or writing an id needs a proof that the arena holds it, and an arena's structure carries its own label, so graphs and trees with parent links need no references. Accepted on a condition: no code relies on it until the formal model (F1) covers its frame facts.
- **Accepted: an optimized release backend that emits C** (#522, ADR 0106). `una build --release` will emit C in a subset with no undefined behavior and compile it with the system C compiler, keeping every check and the interpreter's meaning, with three new release gates; Cranelift stays the default. Measured on checked kernels, the optimizing route runs 6x to 27x faster than Cranelift (`docs/adr/evidence/0106/`).
- **Accepted: data layout for the native backends** (#736, ADR 0107). Raw fixed-width values, records as one object with inline fields behind checked handles, packed lists of raw elements, and loop state in registers, with the uniform word kept at generic boundaries; regions deferred.
- **Accepted: eliding the run-time checks the verifier proves** (#737, ADR 0112). Release builds will drop only the `Int` promotion path and the fixed-width overflow test of operations whose range a proof covers (a proven `overflow` obligation, or an advisory query for `Int` that never changes acceptance), each listed in the build report's `native.elided` with its proof. Every memory-safety, resource, cancellation and division check stays, a wrong proof gives a wrong value and never undefined behavior, and `--no-elision` builds with every check.
- **Accepted: the test declaration is written `test` in edition `0.3`** (#740, ADR 0105). An edition `0.3` package will write `test "name"` where editions `0.1` and `0.2` write `example "name"`, with the same body and rules. `test` will be a reserved keyword in edition `0.3`. An `example` declaration there will be U0103, with an exact rewrite to `test` that `una fix` applies. `una test` will print "test" and "tests" for such a package, and its `--json` the schema `una.test/2`, with the key `tests`. Editions `0.1` and `0.2`, `una.test/1` and Core IR do not change. Nothing is implemented yet.
- **Accepted: small records off the heap** (#750, ADR 0108). Small non-generic records will live unboxed in registers, parameters and results, boxed at most once per value where a uniform word is needed; no lifetime analysis is required, since values have no identity.
- **A `together` block checks its bookkeeping and its task starts against the memory budget** (#777, ADR 0113 step 4). In native code, the block's result list is reserved exactly before its first batch, each batch's state before it forks, each task's start before its thread starts, and each adoption of a batch's events before it is made, so a block whose bookkeeping or starts would pass the budget stops with U6003 at the block instead of reaching the allocator's hard ceiling. A task's heap is torn down whole as its thread ends. A debug build of the runtime checks the bound with a high-water mark of the heap, each start and adoption against what it reserved, and that no copy of a block is made while one of its tasks runs code. The interpreter does not change.
- **The copies of a `together` block are made before its tasks run, and every step is checked** (#777, ADR 0113 step 2). Native code forks a block's tasks batch by batch: the block copies a task's inputs out, the task copies them in, and only when the whole batch has does any task run, so the site where a copy stops no longer depends on how threads are scheduled (#314 stops at one site again). Each step of the copies of a task's inputs and results, with the containers the copies make, is announced and checked against the budget before it allocates, with the copies of other blocks in flight, so such a copy reports U6003 instead of reaching the allocator's hard ceiling. A thread that cannot start, a refused copy of a task's inputs, a copy of more than 4294967295 objects, and a panic during such a copy each end the block before any task of their batch runs, after the tasks of earlier batches ran and with their events kept. A panic while a task's result is copied, at the task or at the join, is U8001 at the block. The interpreter does not change.
- **A `together` block joins its tasks batch by batch, copying one result at a time** (#777, ADR 0113 step 3). In native code, each batch of tasks has its own join: the block adopts the batch's events in task order, then copies the results in one task at a time, each task's thread and heap gone before the next one copies out. At its deciding batch's join, a block that aborts copies no result, a failing block copies only the deciding failure value, and a block whose own task was cancelled copies nothing; a failure leaves the later batches unforked. The block keeps one count instead of a cancellation flag per task, and reads each task's closure or element when it forks it. A task that stops with U8001, an internal error of the runtime, now decides its block before any failure or abort, whatever its index, and cancels every other task of the block, the lower-indexed ones included, so the run ends with U8001 once those tasks reach a check: a task inside an operation in progress, a handler's or the host's, sees the cancellation when that operation returns. The interpreter does not change.
- **The `hunt` release gate runs on Linux** (#793). `scripts/hunt-linux.sh` and the new gate `hunt-linux-arm64` run the same fixed run in a hardened container (no network, read-only root, no capability, not root, limits on processes, memory and time), where Landlock (ABI 6 to 8) confines each run of the compiler to its own directory and the mock's port and scopes its signals; the gate refuses to run when any of these is missing. On both platforms every run now writes only in a directory made for it, and its process group is killed when it ends. A new canary checks on both platforms that a run cannot write outside its directory or open another socket.
- **Advisory UNA-SA-015** (#801). `docs/security/advisories.md` lists the interpolated-pattern fix of v0.3.15 (High), and names the other fixes of v0.3.15 under "Not advisories" with the reason for each.
- **The `hunt` release gate covers edition 0.3's boundaries and conditions** (#813). Two new families generate programs for authority boundaries (ADR 0093) and for conditions on allowed effects and contract locks (ADR 0092), in edition 0.3 and, as a probe of the edition gate, in edition 0.2: must-reject probes, oracle programs with closed calls, and programs that only the full check rejects (U5001, U3023), judged with the pinned solver. The oracle's runs now have the solver too, a run the solver did not decide is a gate error, and two canaries cover edition 0.3.
- **Structural equality on shared parts** (#815): `==`, `!=` and `contains` compare each distinct pair of parts once, in both engines, so values that share their parts no longer take time in the number of paths through them, which doubles with each level of sharing. A comparison takes time and space in the number of distinct pairs of values with parts it meets, compares leaves as it meets them, and allocates no set for its first 1024 steps. It counts the pairs of values with parts it holds at 128 bytes each against `--max-memory`, and over the budget it aborts with U6003 at the comparison, or stops its task there (ADR 0061, Amendment of 2026-10-17; `spec/11 §7`). The runtime functions that compare take the comparison's site first, under new names (`una_rt_equal_at`, `una_rt_contains_at`, `una_rt_generic_equal_at`, `una_rt_generic_contains_at`), so a `libuna_rt.a` built before this change fails to link with the stale-runtime message instead of misreading their arguments: rebuild it with `cargo build -p una-rt`.
- **The `hunt` release gate runs a sample of every family in edition 0.3** (#818). Each operator that does not run edition 0.3 also runs in it its first 10 programs and one program of each further form of any of its editions, 688 programs, so every form the fixed run gives an operator runs on edition 0.3's code paths; the residual check now bounds its solver, so an undecided obligation there is a gate error, and a run killed at its limit, its check deciding, is a gate error, so no comparison is lost to time.
- **Accepted: floating point, `F64`** (#819, ADR 0114). Edition `0.3` will gain `F64`, IEEE 754 binary64, with literals such as `0.5` and `6.674_30e-11`, correctly rounded arithmetic and `sqrt`, and explicit conversions only. Results would be bitwise identical in both engines and on every target: one canonical NaN in memory and at every observation, no fused or reassociated operations, subnormals kept, the floating-point environment set and self-tested at start (U6006), and the runtime's own routines instead of the platform math library. `==` would compare bits, so `nan() == nan()` and `-0.0 != 0.0`, and `<` would be IEEE totalOrder, so floats work as map keys and in `sort`; the IEEE comparisons are named methods (`ieee_lt` and the others), and comparing with a literal zero is an error (U2033), repaired by `is_zero()` for an equality and by the IEEE methods for an ordering. Floats would print by the shortest text that reads back to the same bits, and parse correctly rounded. Contracts over floats would be verified exactly, through the solver's floating-point theory, on a slice of the hypotheses and under a fixed size bound; the queries of every program without `F64` stay byte-identical, and every float query runs with a fixed step budget. Units apply (`F64 of Meter`). A package function named `to_f64` on integers becomes an error in edition `0.3`. Transcendental functions and `F32` are later steps.
- **Example READMEs explain their programs** (#823). Each package of `examples/` now has a README that says what the program does and which Una features it shows, how to run it with the real output, a walkthrough of its key declarations with what `una check` proves about them, common mistakes with the real diagnostics, exercises to extend it, and links to the construct pages and the specification. The `events` README no longer describes code that the package dropped, and a comment in `examples/journal` no longer cites an internal identifier.
- **Every excerpt in the examples' READMEs is checked** (#827). Each `text` block of `examples/*/README.md` names what it shows in its fence's info string: `una="ARGS"` for what a command prints in the package, which must start and end where the output does unless the block starts or ends with `...`, and `file="PATH"` for an excerpt of one of its files. `crates/una-cli/tests/guide.rs` checks each one, with trailing spaces removed and `...` as the only wildcard, a whole line. A block without a marker fails, and so does an empty excerpt or one whose path leaves the package. The blocks that show what a change to a package reports name the README's mistake they belong to, and are checked next.
- **The examples' READMEs check what each mistake reports** (#827). Each change a README's common mistakes describe is a set of files under `tests/fixtures/examples/NAME/mistakes/N/`. `crates/una-cli/tests/guide.rs` lays them over a copy of the package and requires `una check .` to reject it. It then checks the README's diagnostic as an excerpt of what the check prints, and the changed code it shows as an excerpt of the changed file.
- **The `site` gate checks the Learn release's downloads** (#831). When the pinned website's `learn.lock` names a Una by Example release, `scripts/site-learn-release.sh` downloads it from github.com and fails unless it is published and holds exactly the files the build makes from the pinned commit, every archive and `SHA256SUMS`, byte for byte, and nothing else; the gate's output records each file's SHA-256. The release line is read with the website's grammar, and the website's own reading of the lock must name the same repository, commit and release; a release line that does not parse, or two release or repository lines, fail. The release gates unset the site scripts' source overrides, and the gate runs the script's self-test first.
- **The `site` gate builds the website with the new Learn chapters** (#842). `site/website.lock` pins the website whose Learn section is Una by Example with all fourteen chapters on its second template, checked against una 0.3.15, and linked to the release `for-una-0.3.15`, cut from that same commit.
- **`scripts/doc-links.py` reads headings in block quotes and list items** (#843). It reads a document's block structure as the website does for headings and code: block quotes and list items, nested either way, fenced code with backticks or tildes, which ends with its container, and indented code. A heading inside a quote or a list item (`> ## Amendment`, `- ## Step`) gets the website's id, and later headings keep theirs; code inside a container is skipped by the link check too. The site gate's comparison of heading ids with the website found the difference.
- **The Learn release check's self-test is tighter** (#851). The download-failure case checks its reason, the case without `gh` runs with a `PATH` that holds only the tools the script needs, and the site gate compares the website's reading of `learn.lock` with its own as JSON.
- **The standard library reference: numbers, lists, options and results, maps** (#853). `docs/std/reference.json` now describes `Nat`, `abs`, `min` and `max` in a Numbers area; the list functions; `Option`, `Result` and their functions; `Entry`, `empty_map` and the map methods, with the edition 0.3 rules for refined keys and values; and `.len`. Each has a checked example. Entries say how an edition 0.1 declaration differs (`describes`, `earlier`, which quotes each line where the declarations differ), whose effects and failures a function has (`effects_of`), a method's receivers, and a kind's fields or definition. Costs stated in a sentence rather than a table are given too: O(log n) for map lookups and updates, O(1) for `at`, `first` and `set` on a list, and amortized O(1) for `append`, with an O(n) copy for an update of a value that is shared. Every prose text follows the rules of doc comments. Where a list function and a map method share a name (`set`), the checker, not the types written in the example, decides which one each example calls.
- **The standard library reference: the fixed-width integer methods** (#853). `docs/std/reference.json` now describes the conversions `to_i8` to `to_u64` and `to_int`, and the wrapping, saturating and bit operations and the shifts of the fixed-width integers, in the Numbers area, each with a checked example at the edges of the range. A conversion says from which types it widens and when it narrows with an `overflow` obligation. The shifts give their precondition, `0 <= by and by < w`, which the test ties to the rows of `spec/18 §5`.
- **The standard library reference: JSON and `KeyError`** (#853). `docs/std/reference.json` now describes `Json`, `JsonNumber`, `is_json_number`, `parse_json`, `JsonError`, `to_json` and `quote_json`, and `KeyError`, each with a checked example. `parse_json` states each failure as `spec/23 §3` gives it: the first error in reading order, a depth of at most 512, integers of at most 4300 digits, and no size limit beyond the memory budget. The costs of parsing and writing come from `spec/23 §6`. The examples' manifests are checked to be minimal.
- **The standard library reference, its first area** (#853). `docs/std/reference.json` describes each item of the Text area: what each parameter means, what it returns, its preconditions, when each failure happens, the variants of `ParseError`, what to use instead and from which edition, related items, its cost where ADR 0070 states it with each term defined, and an example in `docs/std/examples/`. `crates/una-cli/tests/std_listing.rs` checks it against every edition's declarations and ADR 0070's table, runs every example, and requires each example to stop checking once its item is renamed; the other items, built-in methods included, are listed as pending.
- **Configuration guide pages read as guides** (#859). The pages for the keys of `una.toml`, `[boundaries]` and `verification.lock` use a store package instead of placeholders, keep their spec citations in the "Normative source" part, and name decision records only there.
- **Language guide pages read as guides** (#859). The construct pages under `docs/guide/language/` (failures, capabilities, control flow, types and contracts, information flow, examples and requirements) use store examples instead of placeholders, keep their spec citations in the "Normative source" part, and name decision records only there. Each page links the Learn section that teaches its construct, explains its terms at first use, and says how to read its grammar.
- **Documents may link the website's Learn pages** (#859). `scripts/doc-links.py` accepts a root-relative link of the form `/learn/<route>/`, with lowercase route segments and an optional plain anchor, and refuses any other root-relative link. The website build checks that each such route exists.
- **The `site` gate builds the website with Learn in section pages and the standard library reference** (#860). `site/website.lock` pins the website that renders Una by Example one page per numbered section, with a collapsed sidebar and numbered subsections, linked to the release `for-una-0.3.15-2` cut from that same commit. That website also renders the reference's sections on the standard library pages (#853) publishes spec chapter 25, and resolves the guide's links to Learn pages, checking each route and anchor.
- **The standard library's doc comments hold only text and code spans** (#861). The check that keeps them free of markup now refuses an escaped or unpaired backtick, and any run of two or more backticks, none of which Markdown reads as the edge of a code span the way the check does. It also refuses brackets outside code spans (inline and reference links, images and link definitions), headings, quotes, and control or invisible format characters.
- **The `hunt` gate compares diagnostics' messages** (#874). Each observer's view of a run now keeps the message of every diagnostic of `una run --json`, which redacts what a message renders above `public`, so a secret that reaches only a message, the class of S-28, is a leak between variants; a new canary plants one.
- **A test and a note on native list building** (#878). A list built by `append` in a `for` loop is updated in place at every size, with no copy, which a new test pins through the debug runtime's copy counter. `docs/compiler/testing.md` explains the fixed footprint that debug builds add, from their quarantine of freed slots, and the budgets that native tests with `--max-memory` must use.
- **One spelling for the key file grant** (#885). `spec/13 §5` and the agents guide now write the grant as `keys:<keyfile>` everywhere, as the list of grant specs does; one paragraph wrote `keys:<file>`. Editorial: the grant's meaning is unchanged.
- **Accepted: long-running programs** (#888, ADR 0116). Edition `0.4` will let a package hand its loop to the host with a `[drive]` table and a total `step` function called once per tick, and will gain `for until` with a verified `decreases` measure, `for ever` in functions whose header says `diverges`, `for ever within` a time limit per iteration that cuts only waiting, and `stop`, `stop when` and `skip`. SIGINT, SIGTERM and `--deadline` will make the waiting operations of a `for ever` fail with `Interrupted` and end the loop at its next iteration start, where an `on stop` block cleans up; a grace period bounds the wait (U6007 past it), and a third signal still ends a stuck process. Every value that a deadline can change, including through the program's own tasks that run beside it, will carry a timing label; host stops, as inputs, need none. Nothing is implemented yet.
- **Accepted: concurrency for servers** (#896, ADR 0117). Edition `0.4` will run tasks as coroutines on a few worker threads, so that a waiting request parks only its task, with no `async` or `await`. It will gain `within d`, a time limit on an expression that fails with `Timed[E]` or `Deadline`; `first of`, where the first branch to succeed wins; and `tasks` with `start`, a bounded, dynamic number of tasks that cannot fail, all ended before their scope. `Http` grants can carry rate and concurrency limits, enforced below every handler without deadlock and checked at start-up, with `HttpError.RateLimited` when the wait queue is full, and `std.limit` covers rates computed in code. Operations that a deadline or a race can cut are checked under the timing label, and there are still no task values. Nothing is implemented yet.
- **The site with Learn at teaching depth** (#899). `site/website.lock` pins website b25d5c42, which builds Learn from the release `for-una-0.3.15-3`: every chapter at teaching depth, with new pages 4.5 and 11.5 and no whole-chapter pages. The configure guide's boundaries page links Learn's new page on boundaries, 11.5.
- **Counterexamples show a sum value reached through a record field** (#902). When a refuted obligation's counterexample showed a value of a sum kind whose tag no assertion of the query used, for example a record's field passed to a function with no `ensures`, the model query named the tag's function undeclared, and the counterexample was "not available" with a solver error. The query now declares such a tag, with its range, which every model already satisfies, so the verdict is unchanged and the value shows by its variant.
- **Docs: neutral wording for the benchmark baseline** (#907). The spec and the charter name the benchmark baseline that stays on edition `0.1`, with the same meaning as before.
- **Security: integer parsing is bounded at 4300 digits** (#914). Converting a text of d digits to an `Int` took O(d²) time, so a program parsing untrusted text could be made to spend quadratic time in one call. `parse_int` now fails with the new `ParseError` variant `TooLong`, and `to_int` returns `None`, for an optional `-` and more than 4300 digits, the bound JSON already had (`NumberTooLong`). The check scans the text once, before any conversion, so both cost O(b). One constant, `MAX_INT_DIGITS`, serves JSON, `parse_int` and `to_int`, in both engines. In editions `0.2` and `0.3`, a `when` that names each `ParseError` variant without an `else` arm becomes non-exhaustive and must handle `TooLong`.

## v0.3.15

A patch of the Beta, with five security fixes. In every edition, a text pattern with interpolation in a `when` arm or an `is` expression is now U4008: such a pattern let a `secret` value choose a branch or a `public` `Bool` with no diagnostic, in every release up to v0.3.14 and v0.1.10 (#801, advisory UNA-SA-015). The Core export labels values computed from a labeled value with its label (#799). Native executables report U6003 where the interpreter does, instead of aborting, for a wide range in a `together for` and for a copy between the heaps of a block's tasks (#773, #775), and a native reference count past its 32-bit limit stops the run (#777). The TLS dependency moves to rustls 0.23.46 for four advisories (#779). Edition `0.3` gains units of measure (#34, ADR 0104) and refined elements of collections (#39, ADR 0103); native calls check their limits on a fast path (#598, ADR 0102). The release gates gain a dependency audit, sanitizers and fuzzing of the native runtime, and a security hunt with a two-run noninterference oracle, a comparison with the formal model's re-check and a native sample (#770, #766, #767, #791, #792, #807). Validation: `docs/releases/0.3.15-validation.md`.

- **Units of measure** (#34, ADR 0104, `spec/25`). In edition `0.3`, `measure` declares a unit (`pub measure Meter`, `measure Kilometer = 1000 * Meter`) and `of` attaches one to an integer type (`Int of Meter / Second`, `I64 of Millisecond`). A unit is part of the type: operands, arguments, results and comparisons agree on it (U2028), constants take the unit their context expects, `*` and `/` combine units, and a measured type satisfies `Eq` and `Ord` but not `Integer`. `measured()`, `magnitude()` and `convert()` are the only ways to change a unit, `convert()` only by an exact integer ratio that fits the magnitude type (U2030); an invalid unit declaration is U2029. Units are erased after the type check, so a program's output, its run time, its Core export and its verification queries are those of the same program without them. Editions `0.1` and `0.2` do not change.
- **Refined elements of collections in edition `0.3`** (#39, ADR 0103, accepted on 2026-10-14). `List[Pos]`, `Option[Pos]`, `Map[Text, Pos]` and other refined type arguments, U2002 by ADR 0040 until now, are well-formed in edition `0.3`. Every flow of a value into a refined position is a `refinement` obligation: each element of a list literal, the arguments of `append`, `set`, `or_else` and a map's `with` and `set`, the results of `map`'s and `fold`'s functions, and a whole collection, compared by variance over a fresh binder. Every element read through `first`, `at`, a pattern, a loop variable, a lambda's parameter or a map's `get` carries the refinement as a fact. Every query stays quantifier-free. Editions `0.1` and `0.2` do not change. Edition `0.3` accepts programs that edition `0.2` rejects, under the amendment of the edition's preview rule in spec/13 §7.
- **A risk profile of the trusted computing base** (#65). `docs/security/tcb.md` rates each trusted component of the toolchain by what a bug in it could cause, lists the tests, gates and formal results that guard it, ranks the open gaps, and says how the proposed native-backend and profile decisions would move it.
- **Native calls check their limits on a fast path** (#598, ADR 0102). Every call and loop iteration of a native executable checks the call depth, the stack, the memory budget and cancellation. These checks now read a per-thread record of plain values first, and take the full path, unchanged, only when a limit might be reached or a cancellation flag is set. Every check is kept, fires at the same call or iteration, and gives the same diagnostic and exit status; the record allocates nothing, so the memory budget fires at the same byte. On the benchmark of #595, 10^7 calls in edition `0.2`, a release executable takes 1.039 s against 1.207 s, 14% less, about 17 ns per call (medians of 15 interleaved runs). The fast path is O(1) on `main`'s thread and O(k) in a task k blocks deep, one flag load per enclosing task.
- **The checker records `effect_condition` sites** (#615, ADR 0092 rule 3). Tier 0 now lists the `effect_condition` obligations that tier 1 generates, so the editor shows a hint at each one: an operation, a call, a call of a function value, a function value passed to a standard function or a `uses of` parameter, a `with` that installs a handler, and an operation in a lambda. The cross-check that compares the checker's obligation sites with the verifier's over the conformance corpus no longer leaves this kind out. The obligations themselves are unchanged.
- **A `[contracts]` lock whose condition the verifier cannot encode is U5002 at its entry in `una.toml`, should the checker ever admit one** (#638, `spec/12 §3`). The checker rejects such a condition as U7002 at the entry, and a new conformance case pins that for a comparison with `unit` and a call of a function that is not a predicate. Behind it, the verifier no longer reports the refuted `contract_lock` obligation as U3023 on the function's header, with a repair that could not fix it: the obligation is unknown, its diagnostic names the locked condition, points at the lock and keeps the header's operation as the related location, and the package is still rejected. The conformance suite and the verifier's corpus cross-check fail on any obligation with a locked condition outside the encoding, so a gap between the checker and the verifier cannot pass unseen. No accepted program changes.
- **`una fix` writes the condition that an effect condition asks of a callee** (#639, ADR 0092 and ADR 0084, amendments of 2026-10-14). A refuted `effect_condition` obligation at a call or a `with` now carries an edit as its first repair: the caller's condition, with each of its parameters replaced by the parameter it is passed to, written on the callee's or handler's operation, or conjoined with the condition already there in parentheses. It is offered only for a private function or handler of the same module that lists the operation by name and is never used as a value, when each parameter of the condition is passed as itself and is not an `edit` parameter, its names resolve alike in the callee, both conditions are within the verifier's encoding, and its conjuncts are not all written already. `una fix` checks tier 1 in each round whose tier-0 check is clean, never with `--tier 0`, and applies these edits from refuted obligations only. While any obligation of the package is unknown, no such edit is given, and the repair says why, so solver timing can only stop the rounds earlier. The edit adds a condition and removes none; the guard and `requires` repairs keep a description only. `spec/05 §3` and `spec/13 §5` state it.
- **The `[boundaries]` page shows how an example reaches a boundary** (#642). An example's `do` line takes no `with`, so an example outside a boundary calls a function of its own module that grants the boundary. The page now shows that pattern as a checked package, and states that a `given` handler granted on that way is checked at its `given` line.
- **Security advisories** (#649). `docs/security/advisories.md` lists every security fix of a published release that affected an earlier published release, newest first: its severity on a stated scale, the releases affected as `CHANGELOG.md` names them, the release of each line that fixed it, a summary and its references, and the cases left out with their reasons. `crates/una-cli/tests/advisories.rs` checks that every entry has its fields and a severity of the scale, that every release in "Fixed in" is a tag, and that every published release whose `CHANGELOG.md` section fixes a security issue, on `main` and on the v0.1 line, has an advisory or is named under "Not advisories". `scripts/site-bundle.sh` adds the page to the docs bundle, with the kind `security`, so the website can publish it.
- **The website publishes the security advisories** (#649). `site/website.lock` pins una-lang/website at the commit that builds `/docs/security/advisories/` from `docs/security/advisories.md`, linked from the security model page, when the docs bundle lists the file; a bundle that does not list it builds no such page (website#28).
- **A counterexample never shows an empty value** (#668, `spec/13 §1`). When the solver refuted a query but rejected the request for its values, the values were padded with empty text, so a counterexample could read `flag = , y = Unknown#`. The obligation stays refuted, and its counterexample now says `not available (the solver gave no values: <message>)`; in `--json` its `bindings` are empty and a new `unavailable` key holds the message. A term without a value is never shown. The conformance suite and the verifier's corpus cross-check fail on any obligation whose solver gave an error in place of values, whatever its outcome, since it means a wrong solver script (ADR 0101). Proof cache entries written before this change are solved again once.
- **F1 states that only markers are named with `@`** (#676, `formal/`). A handler installed by `with` is a marker binding `@o` in the model, and the model assumed, without checking, that no other binder of a program is so named. `Program.LWellFormed`, which every certificate requires, now states it: a parameter named with `@` is the marker of an operation its function invokes, and no `let`, case, error, tuple, loop or lambda binder is so named. The whole-program check verifies it (`Fun.markersOk_sound`), so an imported program that names one gets no certificate even if the importer's own refusal of such documents missed it. The re-check of every conformance case and example gives the same verdicts as before. 434 theorems.
- **The documents' links are checked without mdBook** (#708). `scripts/site.sh`, which built an mdBook site that nothing published, is replaced by `scripts/doc-links.py`, which keeps its checks on the documents themselves: every link of README.md, the root documents, the charter, the guides, the specification, the release records, the ADRs and the security advisories, an image included, names a file or directory that git tracks (#305), and every `#fragment` to one of those documents now names a heading of it, by the id the website gives that heading. It needs only Python 3 and git, so `scripts/local-ci.sh` runs it on every run, where it skipped the site without mdBook 0.5.4. In the Linux runs it checks the documents on the host before the container, since git cannot list a worktree's files there; the container then runs its self-test, and its document check only when git can read the checkout. The `site` release gate runs it first, and `scripts/site-website.sh` checks that the pinned website gives the documents' headings the same ids. `site/book.toml` and its theme are removed.
- **The Linux runs of `scripts/local-ci.sh` from a worktree pass the advisories test** (#715). In the container, a worktree's `.git` links outside the mounted checkout, so `crates/una-cli/tests/advisories.rs` (#649) read no tags and failed. The host now runs the test with the repository's tags before starting the container, and only when it passes does the container's run check the page alone, saying the host checked its releases. Anywhere else, a repository without tags still fails it. `scripts/release-gates.sh` unsets the variable, as it does the released sections' one.
- **Each release has a public validation summary, which the installer reads from the repository** (#717, `docs/release-gates.md`). `scripts/release-summary.py` writes the summary of a validation record by a fixed template: the commit, the run's date and platform, the toolchain, each gate's result and seconds, the archives' SHA-256 and the verdict, with every field checked against its shape. It is committed as `docs/validation/<version>.md` and is the release's notes and an asset. `install.sh` reads it from the release's tag, then from `main`, never from the release's assets, accepts a repacked macOS archive of v0.3.3 to v0.3.13 only as `docs/validation/repacked.md` pairs it with the gated digest, and says when an archive was withdrawn or a release has no summary. `docs/releases/validation.md` is now `docs/release-gates.md`.
- **The release gates read the privacy pattern from the Keychain, and stop without it** (#717, `docs/release-gates.md`). `scripts/release-gates.sh` takes `UNA_PRIVACY_PATTERN` from the macOS Keychain item `una-privacy-pattern` when the variable is not set, never exporting it to a gate, printing, logging or writing it, and stops before any gate when neither exists, unless `UNA_GATES_UNCHECKED=1` asks for an unpublished run. `scripts/release-summary.py` passes the pattern to `grep` through a pipe, never on a command line.
- **A pre-release has a validation summary and is published as a pre-release** (#717, `docs/release-gates.md`). The summary's version and archive names accept a pre-release suffix such as `0.4.0-alpha.1`, and the publish command that `scripts/release-gates.sh` prints, now from `scripts/release-publish.sh`, adds `--prerelease --latest=false` for one.
- **The documents' link check reads reference definitions and percent escapes** (#719). `scripts/doc-links.py` now checks the target of every reference definition, `[ref]: target` with or without angle brackets or a title, as it checks inline links. It decodes a percent-encoded path or fragment before looking it up, and refuses a malformed escape or one that is not UTF-8. Its self-test refuses each of these when broken; the 154 documents pass unchanged.
- **`una fix` restores a locked condition** (#725, ADR 0092 and ADR 0084, amendments of 2026-10-15). When a locked public function's header is weaker than its `[contracts]` entry, the first repair of the refuted `contract_lock` obligation (U3023) now carries an edit that writes the locked condition back on the header's operation, `op where C_lock`, or `op where (C_src) and (C_lock)` when the header already writes a condition, and `una fix` applies it in its tier-1 rounds. The edit only adds a condition and never changes `una.toml`. It is given only when the header lists the operation by name, the lock's condition is on one line and both conditions are within the encoding, and it is withheld while any obligation of the package is unknown.
- **A `[contracts]` lock string with escapes, or a multi-line one, is read where it is written** (#727, ADR 0092 rule 8). Each position of a lock clause was placed one byte past the opening quote plus its offset in the decoded value, so after an escape such as `\"` every position fell too early in `una.toml`, and a condition was quoted from the wrong bytes: `e valid(sk` for `valid(sku)`. Each position is now mapped through the string's escapes, its trimmed first newline and its line-ending backslashes, and a condition is quoted as the string reads, `name == "a"`, in U7002, in U3023 and in the `contract_lock` goal. A string that cannot be read back to its value is U7002 at its entry, never a lock left out. Locks are matched by their parsed conditions, as before, so no package is accepted or rejected differently. The id of a `contract_lock` obligation is hashed from its goal, so it changes for a locked condition that is written after an escape, holds one, or is in a multi-line string, and only there.
- **The release gate checks Una by Example** (#733). When the website that `site/website.lock` pins names a commit of una-lang/una-by-example in its `learn.lock`, the `site` gate clones that repository at that commit, builds its download archives, and runs its `scripts/check.py` with the release's `una`: every example's outcome, each Try it change and every archive. The website then builds its Learn section, `/learn/`, from the clone, and the gate fails unless it does. A website without `learn.lock` is built as before, and the gate says the examples were skipped.
- **The website is pinned at its Learn section** (#733, website#30). `site/website.lock` now pins una-lang/website at the commit that adds `/learn/`, Una by Example, whose `learn.lock` pins una-lang/una-by-example with all 14 chapters and 79 pages, so the `site` gate checks them.
- **Common first mistakes are reported with their rule and a name to use** (#743). A reserved keyword written as a module path segment or an operation name, such as `module when` or `op take`, is now reported as "`when` is a reserved keyword, so it cannot be a module path segment", with a free name to use instead (`when_module`, `take_op`). A requirement identifier off the convention gets a suggestion derived from its own name (`SOLD_OUT` gives `SOLD_OUT_1`, the first free one) rather than the guide's example. That suggestion is an edit when no `derived from` clause names the identifier, and otherwise advice that lists those clauses. A method such as `to_int()` in a contract now says what the refinement language offers instead.
- **A declaration keyword used as a local name is renamed by an edit, and `%` names the remainder rule** (#743, ADR 0096 amendment of 2026-10-15). Inside a function body or a handler operation body, a `let`, a lambda parameter, a `when` arm binding or a handler operation parameter named with a declaration keyword such as `op`, `kind` or `module` now gets one edit that renames it and its uses to `<word>_value`, including uses in interpolation holes. A name after `.` and an argument label are left as they are. A function parameter and a handler declaration parameter keep the advice, since their names are part of an interface. A `%` gets a description saying that Una writes the remainder as `mod`, which is Euclidean, and gives no edit, since `%` in C, TypeScript and Rust truncates and the two differ on negative operands.
- **Changelog entries are fragments, assembled at release** (#744, `CONTRIBUTING.md`). A pull request adds one file, `changelog.d/<issue>-<slug>.md`, holding one bullet, and never edits `CHANGELOG.md`, so two pull requests never conflict on the `## Unreleased` lines. The release pull request runs `scripts/changelog-assemble.sh`, which checks every fragment, writes the bullets under `## Unreleased` ordered by issue number and then file name, and removes the fragments; the released sections and their check (#645) are unchanged. The entries that were under `## Unreleased` are now fragments.
- **The `sanitize` release gate fuzzes the native runtime** (#766). Three libFuzzer targets in `fuzz/`, a crate outside the workspace with its own lock, run under AddressSanitizer: `literal` (the entry points that read bytes by pointer and length), `words` (sequences of calls of the entry points on words the generated code never passes) and `json` (the runtime's JSON parser, whose canonical text must parse back to itself and agree with the entry points). Each runs a fixed number of inputs from committed seeds with a fixed seed, after a canary target whose planted crash must be found and minimized; a crash prints where its input and the minimized input are kept. No fuzzing tool is installed: the gate builds the crate with the checking toolchain and states its flags. The `deps` gate covers `fuzz/Cargo.lock` too, with cargo-deny and the review record `supply-chain/fuzz-crates.tsv`.
- **The runtime is tested against words the generated code never passes** (#766). A test calls every entry point of the native runtime's export table with boundary words (every tag pattern, freed and out-of-table handles, objects of the wrong kind) and fixed-seed pseudo-random ones, and requires each call to return a word or count a fault, never to panic, with the runtime still usable after. The same calls run again inside a started run, with its heap and a memory budget, where a call may also end the run as the model says (U6003, U8001) but never on a signal; this phase found the two divergences fixed in #773 and #775. It runs on every change, and `docs/security/tcb.md` records it.
- **A `sanitize` release gate checks the native runtime with AddressSanitizer and Miri** (#766). `scripts/sanitize.sh` builds the runtime with AddressSanitizer and runs the native equivalence test, every runnable program, against it, with a canary that fails the gate when AddressSanitizer is not active; and it runs the runtime's unit tests under Miri, with the set of tests Miri does not run pinned by name in `scripts/sanitize-miri-ignored.txt`. It uses a nightly Rust pinned in `scripts/check-toolchain.toml` (installed by `scripts/install-check-toolchain.sh`) for checking only, never to build an artifact, and fails when that toolchain is missing or differs. macOS only for now. Three tests that do not finish under Miri are tracked in #778.
- **A `hunt` release gate runs the security hunt's generators and a two-run oracle** (#767). `scripts/release-gates.sh hunt` runs a fixed, seeded set of 5,000 generated programs: a program built to break a flow rule must be rejected, a refactor that keeps every label must not turn a rejection into an acceptance, and for an accepted program with a literal labeled above `public`, every variant of that literal must show each observer below its label, at `public` and at `internal`, the same status, output, failure, diagnostics and visible operation events, each variant run twice. The gate fails closed on any finding, any check or run that ends in an unknown way, any nondeterministic run, and any count of judged cases other than the fixed run's; an argument the run redacted above what a visible operation accepts is a finding by itself; four planted canaries must be caught. Pairs where only one variant aborts on a resource limit are outside the noninterference guarantee and are recorded, not judged. macOS only (its sandbox is `sandbox-exec`); the rest of the oracle's scope and a Linux sandbox are #782.
- **A `deps` release gate audits the dependencies** (#770). `scripts/deps-audit.sh` runs `cargo-deny`, at the version that `supply-chain/tools.lock` pins, against the RustSec advisory database fetched for the run: advisories with none ignored, licenses, bans and sources, crates.io only (`deny.toml`). The database's commit goes into the record and the public summary. `supply-chain/crates.tsv` records every crates.io package of `Cargo.lock` by name, version and checksum, and `crates/una-cli/tests/dependencies.rs` fails on a package it does not list, so a new or moved dependency needs a reviewed change; the packages with no source must be exactly the workspace's members. An advisory database more than 7 days old fails the gate. A missing tool, a mismatched version or an advisory database that cannot be fetched fails the gate.
- **A wide range in a `together for` is U6003 in native code too** (#773). A `together for` over a range makes its list before its tasks, and the interpreter checks the memory budget at each element. Native code did not, so a wide range under `--max-memory` grew past the allocator's hard ceiling and the executable aborted on a signal where `una run` reported U6003. The native runtime now checks the budget at each element and ends the run with U6003 at the loop, as the interpreter does. The runtime entry `una_rt_range` takes the loop's site.
- **A copy between tasks over the memory budget is U6003 in native code too** (#775). Native code copies a value between the heaps of a `together` block's tasks, at the fork and at the join, where the interpreter shares it. The copy was made in one step, so under `--max-memory` it could grow past the allocator's hard ceiling and the executable aborted on a signal where `una run` reported U6003. Each copy now checks the budget as it grows and ends the run, or stops the task, with U6003 at the block.
- **Accepted: the copies of a `together` block, made in sequential phases and checked before each allocation** (#777, ADR 0113). Native code will copy a block's inputs and results one task at a time, while none of the block's tasks runs code, and check before each allocation that it fits the memory budget, the copy's working memory included.
